Final approval decision

### Table 45

| Proposal | Decision |
| --- | --- |
| Sequential city gate system | Approved |
| 200+ waitlist leads | Approved only as qualified leads |
| 100 interviews | Reduce to 30–50 structured interviews initially |
| 20 paid deposits | Approved with transparent refund terms |
| 100 sitter applications | Useful funnel target, not a launch criterion |
| 20–30 approved sitters | Use 8–15 active plus verified reserve |
| Five backup sitters | Approved when matched by cluster and time |
| Ten vet/grooming partners | Split into smaller service-specific targets |
| Dedicated city manager before research | Not always necessary |
| Named city owner before beta | Mandatory |
| 90% completion | Reject; require 95%+ |
| 5–10% no-show | Reject; require below 1% |
| 4.5 rating | Increase to 4.6+ before scale |
| City P&L review | Mandatory |

Final city-launch principle

Do not launch a city when marketing is ready. Launch it when demand, active supply, backup capacity, local support, safety processes, payment systems and cluster economics are ready together.

Phase 10 city win condition

A new city reaches dependable repeat bookings and positive cluster contribution through the documented playbook, without founder firefighting or deterioration in safety and customer trust.

City Launch Gate System: GO—with six gates, stronger quality thresholds and cluster-level economics

Phase 10 City Operating System

Team Structure, City-Manager Scorecard, P&L, Unit Economics and Cluster Expansion 🌍🐾

Executive verdict

Your city operating model is directionally correct, but it should be organised by city maturity, not copied as a fixed seven-person team into every new market.

The recommended operating principle is:

Start with one accountable city leader and a small cross-functional launch pod. Add specialised roles only when booking volume, support load and society activity justify them.

The expansion hierarchy should be:

City

↓

Service cluster

↓

Society/locality

↓

Primary provider pool

↓

Backup provider pool

↓

Customer and subscription capacity

A city should never be opened merely because providers have applied. It should open only when the target cluster has:

Qualified demand

Active, schedulable supply

Backup coverage

Local operational ownership

Emergency escalation

Positive projected unit economics

Capacity for the services being advertised

Part I — City Team Structure

1. Review of the proposed team

### Table 46

| Role | Proposed count | Decision |
| --- | --- | --- |
| City Manager | 1 | Mandatory before public beta |
| Sitter Onboarding Executive | 1–2 | Add according to recruitment volume |
| Customer Support Executive | 1–2 | Can initially be shared centrally |
| Society Partnership Executive | 1 | Add when the society pipeline is active |
| Operations Coordinator | 1 | Highly important from beta onward |
| Vet/Groomer Partner Manager | Shared/part-time | Appropriate initially |
| Marketing Executive | Shared/part-time | Appropriate initially |
| Safety/Quality owner | Not listed | Add as a central or regional function |
| Finance/reconciliation | Not listed | Keep central, with city-level reporting |

The city manager must own city outcomes, but should not personally execute every recruitment call, booking reassignment, complaint, event and financial reconciliation.

That creates a founder-style bottleneck inside each city.

2. Stage-based staffing model

Stage C0: Research and pre-launch

Volume: No public bookings

### Table 47

| Role | Staffing |
| --- | --- |
| City launch owner | 1 |
| Supply/operations generalist | 1 |
| Central support | Shared |
| Society partnerships | Shared |
| Marketing | Shared |
| Finance/compliance | Central |

Responsibilities

Demand validation

Area selection

Sitter recruitment

Society meetings

Pricing research

Local compliance mapping

City P&L forecast

Emergency-provider mapping

A full seven-person team is unnecessary at this stage.

Stage C1: Closed beta

Volume: Approximately 50–150 completed services monthly

### Table 48

| Role | Staffing |
| --- | --- |
| City Manager/Launch Manager | 1 |
| Operations and onboarding executive | 1 |
| Customer support | Shared central support |
| Society partnerships | Shared or part-time |
| Add-on partner manager | Shared |
| Marketing | Shared |

Minimum viable launch pod

One strong city manager + one operations/onboarding generalist + central support

This is the smallest sensible structure for a controlled beta.

The generalist may handle:

Sitter screening

Availability collection

Booking assignments

Backup activation

Basic society coordination

But the city manager remains accountable.

Stage C2: Validated cluster

Volume: Approximately 150–500 completed services monthly

### Table 49

| Role | Staffing |
| --- | --- |
| City Manager | 1 |
| Operations Coordinator | 1 |
| Sitter Onboarding Executive | 1 |
| Customer Support | 0.5–1 dedicated equivalent |
| Society Partnerships | 0.5–1 |
| Add-on partner management | Shared |
| Marketing | Shared |

At this stage, onboarding and live operations should no longer be handled by one person.

Stage C3: Growth city

Volume: Approximately 500–1,000 completed services monthly

### Table 50

| Role | Staffing |
| --- | --- |
| City Manager | 1 |
| Sitter Onboarding Executives | 1–2 |
| Operations Coordinator | 1 |
| Customer Support Executives | 1–2 |
| Society Partnership Executive | 1 |
| Vet/Groomer Partner Manager | Shared or part-time |
| Marketing Executive | Shared or part-time |
| Quality/safety reviewer | Shared regional function |

This is close to your proposed team and is appropriate for a city approaching maturity.

Stage C4: Mature city

Volume: 1,000+ completed services monthly across several clusters

Add according to operational load:

Cluster operations leads

Dedicated society account manager

Dedicated partner quality lead

Second operations coordinator

Local event coordinator

Training and verification personnel

City finance analyst or central finance business partner

Do not automatically add staff merely because revenue increases. Add staff when a measurable queue, SLA or control requirement justifies the role.

3. Shared versus city-local functions

Keep central

Product and engineering

Payment infrastructure

Finance and reconciliation

Tax policy

Legal and compliance

National verification standards

Incident taxonomy

Customer-support platform

Partner score framework

Data protection and security

National marketing strategy

Keep city-local

Provider recruitment

Practical trials

Availability and capacity

Society relationships

Live schedule management

Backup activation

Local event delivery

Local partner relationships

Customer-service recovery requiring physical intervention

Hybrid

Customer support

Partner training

Marketing

Veterinary/grooming partnership management

Quality assurance

The operating rule is:

Centralise systems and standards; localise relationships and fulfilment.

Part II — City Manager Responsibilities

4. What the city manager should own

The city manager should be accountable for:

Demand

Monthly completed bookings

Paying customers

Repeat customers

Subscription adoption

Society conversion

Area expansion

Supply

Active walker and sitter capacity

Backup coverage

Provider activation

Partner retention

Provider earnings health

Document validity

Quality

Booking completion

On-time service

Customer ratings

Complaints

Refunds

Report completion

Primary/backup continuity

Economics

City GMV

Platform revenue

Direct contribution

Acquisition cost

Support cost

Refund cost

Society economics

Cluster profitability

Safety

Incident escalation

Provider suspension

Evidence preservation

Root-cause reviews

Corrective actions

Emergency network readiness

Team

Hiring

Training

Performance

Shift coverage

Operational discipline

5. What the city manager should not own alone

The city manager should not have unilateral authority to:

Approve their own unsafe provider exception

Delete or downgrade incident records

Override expired documentation

Hide refunds to improve city metrics

Change clinical veterinary decisions

Settle serious complaints without review

Modify recognised revenue

Access unnecessary customer medical information

approve their own bonus score

Critical safety, compliance and financial controls need independent review.

Veterinary clinical decisions must remain with registered veterinary professionals. VCI professional regulations also prohibit veterinarians from offering or receiving commissions for procuring or referring clients, so city managers should not build veterinary revenue around ordinary lead commissions. Use transparent coordination, event-management or software fees subject to legal review.

6. City manager RACI

### Table 51

| Workstream | City Manager | Central Team |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Sitter recruitment | Accountable | Standards/support |
| Verification | Operationally responsible | Policy and audit |
| Booking fulfilment | Accountable | Product tools |
| Customer support | Escalation owner | First-line/shared |
| Pricing recommendation | Consulted/responsible | Final approval |
| City P&L | Accountable | Finance validates |
| Safety incidents | Immediate owner | Independent review |
| Veterinary services | Coordination only | Clinical boundaries |
| Privacy | Local compliance | Data-protection owner |
| Tax and payouts | Provides inputs | Finance owns |
| Society agreements | Accountable | Legal templates |

Part III — City Manager Scorecard

7. Why the proposed scorecard needs modification

The original metrics are useful, but three corrections are needed:

Revenue growth cannot override poor quality or safety.

“Incident rate below 3%” is too broad.

Gross margin must have a defined denominator.

Use a balanced scorecard with hard safety overrides.

8. Recommended weighted scorecard

### Table 52

| Dimension | Weight |
| --- | --- |
| Quality and safety | 30% |
| Supply and fulfilment | 20% |
| Demand and retention | 20% |
| City economics | 20% |
| Society and strategic growth | 10% |

Quality and safety — 30%

### Table 53

| Metric | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Average customer rating | 4.6+ |
| Complaint/refund rate | Below 3–5% |
| Critical preventable incidents | Zero |
| Serious safety incidents | Below 0.1–0.3% |
| Minor reportable incidents | Below 1% |
| Incident reporting accuracy | 100% |
| Critical unresolved cases | Zero |

A city manager should lose safety-score eligibility for deliberately hiding or misclassifying an incident.

Supply and fulfilment — 20%

### Table 54

| Metric | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Booking completion | 95%+ |
| On-time arrival | 95%+ |
| Provider no-show | Below 1% |
| Provider cancellation | Below 5% |
| Cancellation covered by backup | 90%+ |
| Subscription plans with backup | 95%+ |
| Provider payouts on time | 98%+ |

“Sitter reliability” should not be one vague number. Separate no-shows, cancellations, on-time fulfilment and report completion.

Demand and retention — 20%

### Table 55

| Metric | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Monthly bookings | Stage-specific growth |
| Repeat customer rate | 35%+ in mature city |
| Subscription conversion | Increasing |
| Subscription renewal | 60%+ |
| Referral and organic share | Increasing |
| Customer concentration | Controlled |

Do not reward bookings generated entirely through unsustainable discounts.

City economics — 20%

### Table 56

| Metric | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Direct city contribution | Positive |
| Contribution per booking | Positive and improving |
| CAC payback | Stage-specific |
| Refund/service-recovery costs | Controlled |
| Marketing efficiency | Improving |
| Partner payout reconciliation | Complete |
| City P&L close | On time |

Society and strategic growth — 10%

### Table 57

| Metric | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Pilot societies | Stage-specific |
| Pilot-to-paid conversion | 30–50% starting target |
| Resident paid conversion | 15–25% |
| RWA satisfaction | 4.5+ |
| Society contribution | Positive |
| Gate/security incidents | Minimal and resolved |

AWBI’s published guidance specifically addresses pet owners, caregivers, RWAs and apartment associations. Society operations should support responsible coexistence and reasonable access rather than using the platform as a pet-restriction mechanism.

9. Hard scorecard overrides

A manager should not qualify for a growth bonus when any of these applies:

Critical preventable incident

Concealed safety event

Expired provider credentials used knowingly

Partner payouts materially delayed

City P&L unreconciled

Severe privacy breach

Customer grievance process bypassed

Systematic false completion reporting

Part IV — City P&L Structure

10. First separate GMV from revenue

A marketplace must distinguish:

GMV

Total customer spending through the city.

Platform revenue

The amount the platform recognises from:

Commissions

Service fees

Subscription fees

Society-management fees

Event-management fees

Software fees

Partner payouts

Amounts owed to walkers, sitters, groomers or other providers.

Depending on whether the company acts as principal or agent, recognised revenue may be gross or net. Obtain a written accounting position rather than deciding this only through product terminology.

11. Recommended city P&L

A. Customer spending/GMV memorandum

### Table 58

| Revenue-origin category | GMV |
| --- | --- |
| Walking | ₹ |
| Sitting | ₹ |
| Boarding | ₹ |
| Grooming | ₹ |
| Training | ₹ |
| Veterinary coordination | ₹ |
| Subscription service plans | ₹ |
| Society resident services | ₹ |
| Events | ₹ |

This is not automatically recognised revenue.

B. Recognised platform revenue

### Table 59

| Revenue stream | Accounting category |
| --- | --- |
| Walking commission/service margin | Platform service revenue |
| Sitting commission/service margin | Platform service revenue |
| Boarding margin | Platform service revenue |
| Grooming commission | Partner-service revenue |
| Training commission | Partner-service revenue |
| Vet coordination fee | Fixed coordination/software/event fee |
| Consumer membership | Recurring membership revenue |
| Walking subscription retained share | Recurring service revenue |
| Society management fee | B2B recurring revenue |
| Event-management fee | Event revenue |
| Partner software subscription | B2B software revenue |
| Sponsored placement | Advertising revenue, clearly disclosed |

Do not assume veterinary referral commission is an acceptable revenue line. VCI rules prohibit commissions or bonuses for procuring or referring clients.

C. Direct variable costs

Sitter and partner payouts

Payment-processing fees

Refunds

Service credits and make-goods

Emergency replacement cost

Customer acquisition directly attributable to bookings

Society event delivery

Background/document checks

Per-booking support

Provider travel subsidy

Consumables where supplied

D. City operating costs

City manager salary

Onboarding staff

Operations coordinator

Local customer support

Partnership executive

Local marketing

Recruitment campaigns

Training and trials

City travel

Event setup

Local office or coworking

Telephone and field tools

Insurance allocation

E. Central allocated costs

Technology

National customer support

Finance

Legal/compliance

Brand marketing

Leadership

Data infrastructure

Show city performance both:

Before central allocation

After central allocation

This prevents strong cities from appearing unprofitable merely because all corporate overhead is allocated too early, while still showing the full business path to profitability.

12. City contribution levels

Contribution 1

Contribution 2

City operating contribution

Fully allocated city result

Use each level for a different decision:

### Table 60

| Metric | Decision use |
| --- | --- |
| C1 | Is the individual transaction viable? |
| C2 | Is the cluster operationally viable? |
| City contribution | Can the local city operation sustain itself? |
| Fully allocated result | Can the company scale profitably? |

13. Tax and settlement controls

If the platform collects customer consideration for services supplied by third-party providers, e-commerce-operator and TCS obligations may apply depending on the exact transaction structure. Maintain separate service, provider and city settlement ledgers, and obtain a written CA/GST position before scaling across states.

Tax payable is not ordinary operating revenue or contribution. Keep:

GST collected

TCS

Provider settlements

Refund liabilities

Subscription deferred obligations

separate from commercial margin reporting.

Part V — Unit-Economics Targets

14. Review of proposed targets

### Table 61

| Metric | Proposed | Decision |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Gross margin | 25–35% | Define accounting basis first |
| Net contribution after support | Positive | Approve |
| CAC payback | Under 2–3 months | Strong mature-city target |
| Repeat rate | 35%+ | Approve for mature city |
| Subscription share | 25%+ | Define denominator |
| Society bookings share | 30%+ | Use as channel hypothesis, not universal gate |
| Refund/dispute | Below 5% | Approve; mature target below 3% |

15. Correct definitions

Take rate

Gross margin

If the platform records only its fee as revenue:

If the platform records customer payments as revenue and provider payments as cost of service, the calculation differs.

Do not compare margins across cities until all cities use the same accounting convention.

16. Recommended maturity targets

Closed beta

### Table 62

| Metric | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Contribution per completed service | Improving, preferably positive |
| Refund/dispute | Below 5% |
| Repeat intent | Positive |
| CAC payback | Not yet definitive |
| Provider utilisation | Sustainable |

Validated cluster

### Table 63

| Metric | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Direct contribution | Positive |
| CAC payback | Below 4–6 months |
| Repeat customer rate | 25–40% |
| Subscription share | 15–25% of active recurring users/revenue |
| Refund/dispute | Below 5% |
| Support cost per booking | Stable or falling |

Mature city

### Table 64

| Metric | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Gross/direct margin | 25–35% under a clearly defined basis |
| City operating contribution | Positive |
| CAC payback | Under 2–3 months |
| Repeat customer rate | 35–50% |
| Recurring platform-revenue share | 25%+ |
| Refund/dispute | Below 3–5% |
| Provider payout reliability | 98%+ |
| Critical incidents | Zero |

A 2–3 month CAC payback is an excellent mature-city target, but requiring it during the first few weeks may lead to underinvestment in supply density and society relationships.

17. Society share target

“Society bookings should be 30%+” is appropriate only when society-first acquisition is the chosen local strategy.

Use:

### Table 65

| City model | Healthy society-origin share |
| --- | --- |
| Society-first Gujarat/Gurugram cluster | 25–40% |
| Digitally led Bengaluru cluster | 10–25% |
| Hyperlocal Mumbai premium cluster | Variable |
| Corporate-led cluster | Lower society share may be acceptable |

Avoid dependence on:

One society

One RWA decision-maker

One corporate account

One provider

One acquisition channel

Recommended concentration control:

No single society should produce an excessive share of city contribution once the city matures.

Part VI — Area Expansion Inside a City

18. The city is not the launch unit

The correct expansion model is:

Cluster 1

↓

Cluster 1 passes quality and contribution gates

↓

Adjacent Cluster 2

↓

Cluster 2 passes gates

↓

Cluster 3

↓

Multi-cluster city operation

Cluster boundaries should be based on real travel time, not only postal codes or neighbourhood names.

19. Why travel-time clusters matter

TomTom’s 2025 data reports average congestion of approximately 74.4% for Bengaluru and 49% for Ahmedabad. Bengaluru therefore requires tighter operating radii and stronger peak-time capacity controls than Ahmedabad.

This is an operational inference:

An Ahmedabad cluster may sometimes cover several adjacent residential areas.

A Bengaluru cluster that appears close on a map may still be unworkable during morning and evening peaks.

Walker routes should be defined using measured service-to-service travel times.

20. Ahmedabad cluster review

Proposed sequence

Bopal, South Bopal, Ambli

Satellite, Prahlad Nagar, SG Highway

Vastrapur, Thaltej, Bodakdev

Wider Ahmedabad

Assessment

This is a credible planning hypothesis, but the areas should be split or combined based on:

Provider home locations

Society density

Peak-hour travel

Gate-entry times

Customer time windows

Local pricing

Emergency-vet coverage

Recommended Ahmedabad structure

Cluster A — Bopal corridor

Bopal

South Bopal

Ambli

Nearby societies within the validated travel radius

Cluster B — Prahlad–Satellite corridor

Prahlad Nagar

Satellite

Selected SG Highway societies

Cluster C — West-central premium corridor

Vastrapur

Bodakdev

Thaltej

Do not open “wider Ahmedabad” as one final step. Continue adding smaller operational zones.

Ahmedabad’s 2025 average congestion was lower than Bengaluru’s, but the city still averaged close to 29 minutes for a 10 km drive, so route design remains material.

21. Bengaluru cluster review

Proposed sequence

Whitefield, Brookefield, Kundalahalli

HSR, Sarjapur Road, Bellandur

Indiranagar, Koramangala

Wider Bengaluru

Assessment

The first cluster is directionally sensible because the areas are comparatively adjacent.

The second proposed cluster may be too broad during peak periods. HSR, Bellandur and different sections of Sarjapur Road may need to operate as separate capacity zones.

Recommended Bengaluru structure

Cluster A — Whitefield micro-market

Whitefield

Brookefield

Kundalahalli

Selected nearby societies

Cluster B1 — HSR

HSR Layout

Adjacent serviceable societies

Separate peak-time provider pool

Cluster B2 — Bellandur/Sarjapur corridor

Bellandur

Selected Sarjapur Road communities

Strict time-window and route rules

Cluster C1 — Koramangala

Cluster C2 — Indiranagar

Merge C1 and C2 operationally only when travel-time and provider-density data support it.

Bengaluru’s high measured congestion makes broad city-wide primary/backup pools particularly risky.

22. Cluster-status model

### Table 66

| Status | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Z0 Research | Demand and supply being assessed |
| Z1 Waitlist | Leads collected; not bookable |
| Z2 Closed beta | Invitation-only bookings |
| Z3 Public limited | Selected services and slots |
| Z4 Validated | Quality, repeat and economics proven |
| Z5 Growth | Additional supply and societies |
| Z6 Mature | Stable capacity and local management |

The website should display service availability based on cluster status.

Do not accept payment in Z0 or Z1 areas unless it is an explicitly refundable and clearly disclosed pre-launch deposit.

Part VII — Launch Density Rules

23. Why the proposed density rules need modification

Your target of ten approved walkers per area may be:

Too high for a 50-customer beta

Too low for a 300-subscriber cluster

Irrelevant if the walkers are unavailable during peak times

Count active, schedulable capacity, not just approved profiles.

24. Corrected launch-density targets

Closed-beta micro-cluster

### Table 67

| Requirement | Recommended target |
| --- | --- |
| Launch-ready walkers | 5–8 |
| Peak-time backup walkers | 2–3 |
| Pet sitters | 3–5 |
| Boarding hosts | 0–3, only if boarding is launched |
| Vet referral options | 2 |
| Groomers | 2–3 |
| Target societies | 5–10 |
| Qualified customer leads | 100–200 |
| Paying beta customers | 30–75 |
| Backup coverage | 90%+ |

Public validated cluster

### Table 68

| Requirement | Recommended target |
| --- | --- |
| Active walkers | 8–15 |
| Backup-capable walkers | 3–5 |
| Active sitters | 5–10 |
| Boarding providers | 2–5 if operationally approved |
| Vet referral options | 2–5 |
| Groomers | 3–5 |
| Society pipeline | 10+ |
| Monthly paying customers | 100–200 |
| Monthly completed services | 300–500 |
| Backup coverage | 90–95%+ |

25. Do not double-count providers

One person may offer both walking and sitting, but their available capacity cannot be counted twice.

Example:

Riya capacity:

80 walking slots/month

or

10 overnight sitting assignments

You cannot report:

80 walking slots

+

10 sitting assignments

if the same calendar days are required for both.

Use a time-based provider capacity ledger.

26. Required density metrics

Demand density

Qualified leads per cluster

Paying customers per square kilometre

Bookings per active customer

Society-origin bookings

Peak-time demand

Subscription commitments

Supply density

Active providers per peak window

Provider minutes available

Backup coverage

Travel time between services

Capacity utilisation

Provider earnings per productive hour

Operational density

Average provider travel time

Bookings per route hour

Society gate delay

Service completion

On-time arrival

Support incidents per 100 bookings

Financial density

CAC per activated customer

Contribution per booking

Contribution per provider hour

Contribution per society

Local marketing payback

27. Cluster-opening gate

Open a new area only when all critical criteria pass:

### Table 69

| Gate | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Qualified leads | 100–200 |
| Paid trial intent | 20–40 |
| Active launch providers | 5–8 |
| Peak-time backups | 2–3 |
| Vet escalation mapped | Yes |
| Support routing | Yes |
| Local pricing | Configured |
| Capacity limits | Configured |
| Refund/incident SOP | Tested |
| Projected contribution | Viable |

28. Cluster-scale gate

Scale beyond beta only when:

### Table 70

| Metric | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Completed paid services | 50–150 beta services |
| Rating | 4.6+ |
| Completion | 95%+ |
| On-time | 95%+ |
| Provider no-show | Below 1% |
| Provider cancellation | Below 5% |
| Refund/dispute | Below 5% |
| Backup fulfilment | 90%+ |
| Critical preventable incidents | Zero |
| Direct contribution | Positive or clearly improving |

29. Cluster-expansion gate

Open the next adjacent cluster only when the current cluster has:

300–500 monthly completed services

100–200 monthly paying customers

25–40% repeat rate

Positive direct contribution

Capacity utilisation below 85%

Peak utilisation below 90%

90–95% backup coverage

No critical unresolved incident

Local operational owner

Stable provider earnings

Do not use unused central-city provider capacity as evidence that a distant new cluster is ready.

Part VIII — Compliance Controls for City Teams

30. Gig and platform-worker operations

The Code on Social Security recognises gig and platform workers, and the Ministry of Labour’s June 2026 direction states that aggregators covered by the 2026 Rules must share required worker details through the eShram framework. A pet-care marketplace should obtain a formal classification opinion and build compliant provider-registration and reporting processes if it is in scope.

City onboarding should therefore maintain:

Provider identity

Engagement dates

Active/inactive status

Earnings and payouts

Government registration identifiers where applicable

Document expiry

Reporting-sync status

31. Data access

The DPDP Rules, 2025 have phased commencement. City expansion increases access to recurring schedules, home addresses, gate instructions, pet records and payment information, so city-specific role controls should be implemented before scaling.

Recommended access:

### Table 71

| Role | Access |
| --- | --- |
| Walker | Assigned booking and necessary pet instructions |
| Operations | Scheduling and fulfilment |
| Support | Customer-service and booking data |
| City Manager | Aggregated city controls and escalations |
| Society Executive | Society relationship data, not medical records |
| Marketing | Aggregated campaign data |
| Finance | Payments and settlements |
| RWA | Aggregate society reports only |

Final Approval Matrix

### Table 72

| Proposal | Decision |
| --- | --- |
| One city manager per public city | Approved |
| One to two onboarding executives | Stage-dependent |
| One to two local support executives | Stage-dependent; central support first |
| Society partnership executive | Add when pipeline justifies it |
| Operations coordinator | Mandatory from validated-cluster stage |
| Shared add-on partner manager | Approved initially |
| Shared city marketing | Approved initially |
| City manager owns all outcomes | Approved |
| City manager personally performs everything | Rejected |
| Rating target 4.6+ | Approved |
| Repeat rate 35%+ | Approved for mature cities |
| Reliability target 95%+ | Approved with separate metrics |
| Incident rate below 3% | Replace with severity-based targets |
| Gross margin 25–35% | Approve only with a defined accounting basis |
| CAC payback under 2–3 months | Mature-city target |
| Society booking share 30%+ | Use where society-first strategy applies |
| Open whole city at once | Rejected |
| Ten approved walkers per cluster | Replace with active capacity targets |
| No supply, no area launch | Approved—non-negotiable |

Final Recommended City Operating Model

Pre-launch

1 City Lead + 1 Generalist + Shared Central Functions

Closed Beta

1 City Manager + 1 Ops/Onboarding Executive

Validated Cluster

City Manager + Ops Coordinator + Onboarding + Shared Support

Growth City

City Manager + 1–2 Onboarding + 1 Ops + 1–2 Support

+ Society Executive + Shared Add-on/Marketing

Mature City

Add Cluster Leads, Quality, Partnerships and Capacity

based on measured workload

Phase 10 City Win Condition

Each city and cluster has enough demand, active supply, backup capacity, local accountability and positive contribution to operate without founder firefighting or degradation in safety and customer trust.

City Team, P&L and Cluster Expansion System: APPROVED—with stage-based staffing, severity-based safety gates and capacity measured by area and time window rather than provider headcount alone.

Phase 10 Expansion Technology Architecture 🌍⚙️

Multi-City Configuration, Database, APIs and Command Centres

Executive verdict

Your proposed features are correct, but the current schema is too city-centric and too static for production multi-city operations.

A reliable architecture needs six layers:

Country

↓

City

↓

Service zone / cluster

↓

Society or locality

↓

Service configuration and capacity

↓

Bookings, partners, pricing and P&L

The critical design rule is:

City should be a first-class operational boundary, while the service zone should be the actual booking, pricing and capacity boundary.

A service may be available in Bengaluru without being available throughout Bengaluru. Similarly, Whitefield may have walking capacity at 7:00 AM but no boarding supply or evening backup.

1. Core multi-city architecture

Every city must independently configure:

Launch stage

Public availability

Service zones

Services offered

Prices and partner payouts

Operating hours

Sitter capacity

Support contacts

Emergency veterinary directory

Notification templates

Society pipeline

Staff access

Subscriptions

Financial attribution

Incident ownership

The booking flow should become:

Customer enters address

↓

Resolve city and service zone

↓

Check whether service is active

↓

Check pet eligibility

↓

Check local price version

↓

Check partner and backup capacity

↓

Display available slots

↓

Create booking

↓

Attribute revenue, cost and incident ownership to city

Do not let customers select an arbitrary city from a dropdown and then assume the service is available.

2. City and area management

What the feature must do

City management should control:

Whether the city is visible publicly

Whether it accepts waitlist registrations

Whether it accepts paid bookings

Which clusters are active

Which services are enabled

Which payment and support workflows apply

Which staff can access the city

Recommended city states

RESEARCH

WAITLIST

SUPPLY_BUILD

CLOSED_BETA

PUBLIC_LIMITED

VALIDATED

GROWTH

MATURE

PAUSED

EXITED

Do not use a simple Boolean such as:

is_active = true

That cannot distinguish:

A city collecting leads

A closed beta

A city taking bookings in selected areas

A temporarily paused city

A fully mature operation

Recommended zone states

RESEARCH

WAITLIST

BETA

ACTIVE_LIMITED

ACTIVE

CAPACITY_PAUSED

SERVICE_PAUSED

CLOSED

A city may be GROWTH while one zone is ACTIVE, another is WAITLIST, and a third is paused because its morning capacity is full.

3. Geography model

Problem with the proposed areas table

This is too limited:

areas

- city_id

- name

- pincode

A neighbourhood can contain multiple postcodes, and a postcode may not precisely match your operational service boundary.

Use geographic polygons or multipolygons for operational zones.

Recommended model

service_zones

- id

- city_id

- code

- name

- slug

- boundary

- centroid

- status

- launch_stage

- priority

- timezone

- created_at

- updated_at

service_zone_pincodes

- service_zone_id

- pincode

Use boundary for accurate booking eligibility and pincodes for customer search, SEO and fallback matching.

PostGIS provides geographic data types and functions such as ST_DWithin; when geography values are used, distance is measured in metres. That makes it suitable for local service-radius checks.

Example radius matching

SELECT p.id

FROM partners p

JOIN partner_service_areas psa

ON psa.partner_id = p.id

WHERE psa.service_type = 'DOG_WALK'

AND psa.status = 'ACTIVE'

AND ST_DWithin(

psa.origin,

:booking_location,

psa.max_radius_meters

);

Distance is only the first filter. Final matching must also validate:

Availability

Time window

Pet size and behaviour

Required certification or permission

Society access

Travel time

Existing workload

Backup capacity

4. City-specific service catalogue

Do not use one global services table with an is_active flag.

A service can be:

Active in one city

Beta in another

Active in only one cluster

Available only during certain hours

Disabled because local supply is exhausted

Recommended structure

services

- id

- code

- name

- category

city_service_configurations

- id

- city_id

- service_id

- status

- booking_mode

- minimum_notice_minutes

- maximum_advance_days

- operating_hours

- cancellation_policy_id

- support_policy_id

- created_at

zone_service_configurations

- id

- service_zone_id

- service_id

- status

- booking_mode

- capacity_policy_id

- price_book_id

- waitlist_enabled

- launch_limit

- created_at

Service status

DISABLED

WAITLIST

MANUAL_BETA

ACTIVE_LIMITED

ACTIVE

CAPACITY_PAUSED

SAFETY_PAUSED

This gives you feature control such as:

Ahmedabad / Bopal

Walking ACTIVE

Sitting ACTIVE_LIMITED

Grooming ACTIVE

Boarding MANUAL_BETA

Pet Taxi DISABLED

5. City pricing architecture

Problem with the proposed table

This design is too simple:

city_pricing

- service_type

- base_price

- trial_price

- subscription_price

One service can have:

Several durations

Different pet sizes

Peak and off-peak prices

Society discounts

Subscription prices

Partner payouts

Taxes

Travel fees

Multiple versions over time

Correct architecture

price_books

- id

- city_id

- service_zone_id nullable

- name

- currency

- status

- valid_from

- valid_until

- created_at

service_packages

- id

- service_id

- code

- name

- duration_minutes

- entitlement_quantity

- rules

price_book_items

- id

- price_book_id

- service_package_id

- customer_price_minor

- partner_payout_minor

- platform_fee_minor

- tax_rate

- peak_window_modifier

- additional_pet_price_minor

- trial_price_minor

- is_active

- valid_from

- valid_until

Store ₹2,499 as:

249900 paise

Do not use floating-point values for money.

Versioning rule

Never overwrite historical pricing.

When a price changes:

Price Book Version 1 → retired

Price Book Version 2 → published

Every booking should store a price snapshot:

customer_price_snapshot

partner_payout_snapshot

tax_snapshot

price_book_item_id

This prevents historical invoices and city P&Ls from changing when a new price is published.

6. Sitter service radius and local supply

Recommended partner geography model

A partner may work in multiple cities or zones. Therefore, do not put a single city_id directly on the partner profile as the complete service definition.

Use a many-to-many assignment:

partner_service_areas

- id

- partner_id

- city_id

- service_zone_id

- service_type

- origin

- coverage_boundary nullable

- max_radius_meters

- travel_mode

- status

- valid_from

- valid_until

partner_availability

- id

- partner_id

- service_zone_id

- weekday

- start_time

- end_time

- maximum_services

- status

partner_capacity_allocations

- id

- partner_id

- service_zone_id

- service_type

- capacity_date

- time_window

- gross_capacity

- reserved_subscription_capacity

- reserved_backup_capacity

- one_time_capacity

- remaining_capacity

Important principle

A sitter’s service radius is not constant throughout the day.

For example:

2 km during the 7:00–9:00 AM peak

5 km during the afternoon

Same-society bookings only during high-density blocks

Your architecture should therefore support both:

Geographic radius

Time-window-specific operational radius

7. Capacity planning engine

Capacity planning is not merely a dashboard calculation. It must control checkout.

Sellable-capacity formula

Required entities

capacity_policies

- id

- city_id

- service_zone_id

- service_type

- backup_reserve_percent

- maximum_utilization_percent

- stop_sales_percent

capacity_slots

- id

- city_id

- service_zone_id

- service_type

- service_date

- window_start

- window_end

- gross_capacity

- reserved_capacity

- backup_capacity

- available_capacity

- status

capacity_reservations

- id

- capacity_slot_id

- booking_id nullable

- subscription_id nullable

- quantity

- reservation_type

- status

- expires_at

Checkout rule

Plan selected

↓

Zone capacity checked

↓

Recurring schedule capacity checked

↓

Primary capacity available?

↓

Backup capacity available?

↓

Only then allow payment

No capacity should mean:

Alternative slot

Another plan

Waitlist

Another zone

It should not mean collecting payment and manually solving the shortage later.

8. City-specific landing pages

Required page hierarchy

/cities/ahmedabad

/cities/ahmedabad/bopal

/cities/ahmedabad/bopal/dog-walking

/cities/bengaluru/whitefield/pet-sitting

Each city page should show

Exact supported areas

Services currently live

Starting prices

Local partner availability

Local society coverage

Service hours

Local support contact

Safety and verification process

Local FAQs

Waitlist for unsupported locations

Important trust rule

Do not publish:

Available throughout Bengaluru

when only Whitefield and Brookefield are active.

Use:

Available in Whitefield, Brookefield, Kundalahalli and selected nearby societies.

City and zone pages should read from the same configuration used by checkout. This prevents marketing pages from advertising services the operational system has disabled.

9. City admin roles and access control

Problem with the proposed structure

This is insufficient:

city_staff

- role

- permissions

Permissions should not be an uncontrolled list copied into every staff row.

Use separate role and permission definitions.

Recommended structure

staff_roles

- id

- code

- name

- scope_type

permissions

- id

- code

- description

role_permissions

- role_id

- permission_id

city_staff_assignments

- id

- user_id

- city_id

- service_zone_id nullable

- role_id

- is_primary

- status

- valid_from

- valid_until

Example roles

National Admin

Regional Manager

City Manager

City Operations

Supply Onboarding

Society Partnerships

Customer Support

Finance Viewer

Safety Reviewer

Marketing Viewer

Access principle

A Pune city operations user should not automatically access:

Mumbai customer addresses

Ahmedabad financial data

Bengaluru partner documents

National incident investigations

PostgreSQL supports row-level security policies that restrict which rows a role can read or modify. RLS should supplement—rather than replace—application-level authorisation.

Example security context

SET LOCAL app.user_id = '...';

SET LOCAL app.city_ids = '{city-uuid}';

Then apply policies to scoped tables such as:

Bookings

Partners

Societies

Incidents

Subscription records

City financial snapshots

Do not use database-owner or BYPASSRLS credentials for ordinary application requests.

10. City-level notifications

Notifications must be configurable by:

City

Zone

Language

Service

Customer status

Incident severity

Launch stage

Local support hours

Recommended tables

notification_templates

- id

- code

- channel

- locale

- status

city_notification_overrides

- support_phone

notification_events

Example city-specific notification

Ahmedabad:

Support available in English, Hindi and Gujarati

Local number: +91-...

Bengaluru:

Support available in English, Hindi and Kannada

Local escalation window: ...

Do not hard-code support numbers or city names into application code.

11. Partner city tagging

Partners should be assigned through service-area records, not simple tags.

Correct hierarchy

Partner

├── City assignments

├── Zone assignments

├── Service permissions

├── Radius

├── Availability

└── Capacity

A grooming company may:

Operate in two cities

Serve only selected zones

Offer cat grooming in one city

Offer dog grooming in another

Have separate staff pools and prices

Use:

partner_city_assignments

partner_service_areas

partner_service_permissions

partner_team_members

partner_availability

Do not duplicate the entire partner profile for each city unless they are legally separate branches.

12. Society grouping

Recommended structure

societies

- id

- city_id

- primary_service_zone_id

- name

- slug

- status

- location

society_towers

- id

society_gates

- name

society_city_programmes

- account_owner_id

- status

- start_date

- end_date

Why this matters

Society reporting must distinguish:

Identified societies

Contacted societies

Decision-maker meetings

Pilot societies

Paid societies

Resident leads

Resident subscribers

Event conversions

Gate incidents

Contribution

Do not store only:

society.city = "Ahmedabad"

The operational relationship needs account ownership, status history and programme configuration.

13. Corrected city schema

Cities table

cities

- id

- country_code

- state_code

- code

- name

- slug

- timezone

- currency

- lifecycle_stage

- public_status

- launch_date

- default_language

- support_phone

- emergency_support_phone

- created_at

- updated_at

Remove city_manager_id

The manager can change or multiple managers may exist during a transition. Keep manager assignments in city_staff_assignments.

A cached primary_manager_id may be added for read performance, but staff assignments remain the source of truth.

Service zones

service_zones

- id

- city_id

- code

- name

- slug

- boundary_geography

- centroid_geography

- status

- launch_stage

- priority

- created_at

- updated_at

Price books

price_books

price_book_items

service_packages

city_service_configurations

zone_service_configurations

This replaces the oversimplified city_pricing table.

City performance

Do not make city_performance the primary operational data source.

Bookings, payments, incidents and subscriptions remain the source of truth. Performance rows should be snapshots or aggregates.

city_metric_snapshots

- id

- city_id

- service_zone_id nullable

- period_type

- period_start

- period_end

- metric_version

- completed_bookings

- unique_paying_customers

- gross_booking_value_minor

- platform_revenue_minor

- contribution_minor

- active_partners

- repeat_customer_rate

- rating

- completion_rate

- on_time_rate

- refund_rate

- minor_incident_count

- serious_incident_count

- critical_incident_count

- generated_at

Use different columns for:

GMV

Platform revenue

Contribution

Do not store them all as revenue.

14. Materialized dashboard views

National dashboards should not continuously aggregate millions of raw booking and payment rows.

Use:

Operational queries for today’s live bookings

Materialized views or analytics tables for historical city metrics

A finance ledger for reconciled revenue

A separate incident source for safety metrics

PostgreSQL materialized views persist query results and can later be refreshed. A concurrent refresh allows reads to continue, but it requires an appropriate unique index on the materialized view.

Suggested views

mv_city_daily_operations

mv_city_monthly_financials

mv_city_partner_reliability

mv_city_subscription_metrics

mv_city_society_pipeline

mv_national_city_scorecard

Refresh cadence

### Table 73

| View | Refresh |
| --- | --- |
| Today’s bookings | Real time or near real time |
| Capacity | Every few minutes |
| Failed payments | Near real time |
| City quality | Hourly |
| Subscription MRR | Hourly/daily |
| Financial P&L | Daily after reconciliation |
| Monthly scorecard | Daily until month close |

Every widget should display its last refresh timestamp.

15. Index strategy

Typical high-value indexes include:

CREATE INDEX bookings_city_status_time_idx

ON bookings (city_id, status, scheduled_at);

CREATE INDEX bookings_zone_date_idx

ON bookings (service_zone_id, scheduled_at);

CREATE INDEX partner_service_area_geo_idx

ON partner_service_areas

USING GIST (origin);

CREATE INDEX active_price_items_idx

ON price_book_items (price_book_id, service_package_id)

WHERE is_active = true;

PostgreSQL supports multicolumn indexes and partial indexes, but the column order and predicate should match real query patterns. Indexes improve reads while adding write and maintenance overhead, so they should be driven by observed workloads.

Use pg_stat_statements to identify expensive and frequent queries rather than guessing every index in advance.

16. Table partitioning

Do not create one physical bookings table per city.

Begin with a shared bookings table containing:

city_id

service_zone_id

scheduled_at

When the volume justifies partitioning, append-heavy tables are generally better partitioned by time, such as month, rather than by a growing and uneven set of cities.

Good partition candidates:

Bookings

Notification events

Audit logs

Payment events

Capacity snapshots

Location events

PostgreSQL supports declarative partitioning and partition pruning; however, partitioning adds operational complexity and should be introduced only when row volume and query behaviour justify it.

17. Corrected multi-city API design

Public city discovery

GET /v1/cities?public_status=BOOKABLE

GET /v1/cities/{citySlug}

GET /v1/cities/{citySlug}/service-zones

GET /v1/cities/{citySlug}/services

Address resolution

POST /v1/serviceability/resolve

Request:

{

"latitude": 23.027,

"longitude": 72.465,

"pincode": "380058",

"service_type": "DOG_WALK"

}

Response:

{

"city": {

"id": "city_ahmedabad",

"name": "Ahmedabad"

},

"service_zone": {

"id": "zone_south_bopal",

"name": "South Bopal"

},

"serviceability": "ACTIVE_LIMITED",

"available_services": ["DOG_WALK", "PET_SITTING"],

"waitlist_available": false

}

Plan and price discovery

GET /v1/service-zones/{zoneId}/services

GET /v1/service-zones/{zoneId}/service-packages

GET /v1/service-zones/{zoneId}/prices

GET /v1/service-zones/{zoneId}/availability

Prices must come from server-side published price books—not from client-submitted amounts.

National admin APIs

POST /v1/admin/cities

GET /v1/admin/cities

GET /v1/admin/cities/{cityId}

POST /v1/admin/cities/{cityId}/transition-stage

POST /v1/admin/cities/{cityId}/staff-assignments

GET /v1/admin/cities/{cityId}/audit-log

Avoid a generic unrestricted:

PUT /api/admin/cities/:id

for every field. Use explicit commands for high-impact transitions such as public launch, pause and city closure.

Zone administration

POST /v1/admin/cities/{cityId}/service-zones

PATCH /v1/admin/service-zones/{zoneId}

POST /v1/admin/service-zones/{zoneId}/transition-stage

POST /v1/admin/service-zones/{zoneId}/services

POST /v1/admin/service-zones/{zoneId}/capacity-policies

Price administration

POST /v1/admin/cities/{cityId}/price-books

POST /v1/admin/price-books/{priceBookId}/items

POST /v1/admin/price-books/{priceBookId}/publish

POST /v1/admin/price-books/{priceBookId}/retire

Published price versions should be immutable.

City operations APIs

GET /v1/city-ops/dashboard

GET /v1/city-ops/bookings

GET /v1/city-ops/bookings/today

GET /v1/city-ops/partners

GET /v1/city-ops/capacity

GET /v1/city-ops/incidents

GET /v1/city-ops/subscriptions

GET /v1/city-ops/societies

The permitted city should be derived from the authenticated staff assignment.

Do not trust:

?city_id=another-city

as authorisation.

18. API security rules

Every write endpoint should support:

Authentication

City and zone authorisation

Input validation

Idempotency keys

Audit records

Version or concurrency checks

Explicit transition validation

Example

A city manager may:

Assign a backup sitter

Pause sales in their zone

Add a service-recovery credit within a limit

They should not necessarily be allowed to:

Publish national prices

Edit another city

Delete an incident

Override veterinary credentials

Mark an unpaid invoice paid

Change recognised finance data

PostgreSQL privileges and RLS provide database-enforced layers for these scopes.

23. Privacy and city-level data isolation

Multi-city operations increase access to:

Customer addresses

Recurring schedules

Gate instructions

Pet behaviour

Medical-related records

Payment references

Sitter identity documents

The notified Digital Personal Data Protection Rules, 2025 have phased commencement. The architecture should nevertheless implement city-scoped access, purpose-specific processing, security controls and grievance handling before expansion.

Minimum controls

Role-based permissions

Row-level city scoping

Field-level restrictions

Audit logs

Secure exports

Time-limited temporary access

No individual data in RWA dashboards

No cross-city customer browsing

Redacted analytics

Data-retention jobs

Example

A society partnership executive may see:

Society account

Meeting notes

Aggregate resident registrations

Event conversions

They should not see:

Customer health records

Home-access codes

Payment-card details

Detailed pet incident reports unrelated to the society relationship

25. Production acceptance tests

Before City 2 opens, test:

Customer address resolves to the correct city and zone.

Unsupported address receives waitlist—not checkout.

Walking can be active while boarding remains disabled.

City price differs without affecting existing bookings.

Existing booking retains its historical price.

Partner outside the radius is excluded.

Partner in the radius but unavailable is excluded.

Peak slot closes when capacity reaches the limit.

Backup capacity remains reserved.

City manager cannot access another city.

National admin can access all authorised cities.

RLS blocks direct cross-city database reads.

City landing page reflects service configuration.

Paused service disappears from checkout.

Society is attributed to the correct city and zone.

Booking revenue reaches the correct city P&L.

Refund updates contribution metrics correctly.

Incident routes to the correct city owner.

Dashboard refresh shows a freshness timestamp.

City closure disables new bookings without deleting historical data.

Final architecture decision

### Table 76

| Proposed component | Decision |
| --- | --- |
| City/area management | Approve with lifecycle stages and geographic zones |
| City pricing | Replace with versioned price books |
| Sitter service radius | Approve using spatial data plus availability |
| City landing pages | Approve; read from operational configuration |
| City admin roles | Approve with RBAC, assignments and RLS |
| City-level dashboards | Approve with actionable drill-downs |
| City notifications | Approve with city, locale and consent configuration |
| Partner city tagging | Replace with service-area assignments |
| Society city grouping | Approve with account and programme hierarchy |
| Capacity planning | Mandatory and checkout-enforced |
| city_manager_id on city | Use staff assignment as source of truth |
| Single pincode on area | Replace with polygons and postcode mappings |
| One mutable city-pricing row | Reject |
| Monthly city_performance as source of truth | Use reconciled snapshots/materialized views |
| Generic unrestricted PUT APIs | Replace with explicit commands and transitions |

Phase 10 technology win condition

The platform can activate, price, staff, monitor, pause and financially isolate every city and service zone without copying code, exposing cross-city data or manually rebuilding operations for each launch.

Multi-City Technology Architecture: GO—with service-zone geography, versioned price books, city-scoped access, capacity-enforced checkout and independent city P&Ls.

Phase 10 Multi-City Brand Strategy 🌍🐾

One National Brand, Six Local Market Playbooks

Executive verdict

Approve the national-master-brand model with city-specific positioning.

Do not create six independent brands. That would fragment:

Trust and reviews

Verification standards

Product development

Support systems

Search authority

Safety reputation

Marketing expenditure

Use one national promise:

Verified local pet care, documented at every visit.

Then localise five elements for each city:

Customer problem

Flagship product

Operational service area

Pricing and offer

Local trust evidence

India’s pet-care category continues to expand as pets are increasingly treated as family members and spending shifts toward professional services, digital discovery and premium care. However, national category growth does not prove that the same positioning or price will work in every neighbourhood.

1. National brand architecture

Recommended brand core

Your proposed core is:

Verified pet care with trusted sitters, live updates, report cards and emergency support.

A safer and stronger version is:

Verified local pet care with trusted caregivers, live service updates, detailed care reports and urgent-care coordination.

Why change “emergency support”?

“Emergency support” can sound like the platform provides veterinary treatment.

Your platform should promise:

Emergency clinic information

Human coordination

Record-sharing assistance

Transport coordination where available

Customer escalation

It should not promise:

Diagnosis

Treatment

Guaranteed clinic admission

Guaranteed veterinary response time

Medical prioritisation for paid members

Use:

Urgent-care coordination

rather than:

Emergency medical support

2. Four national brand pillars

Pillar 1: Verified people

Every provider profile should show exactly what was verified.

Valid badges

Identity checked

Contact verified

Documents reviewed

Practical trial completed

Dog-walking skills assessed

Cat-care skills assessed

Society access approved

Background check completed on [date]

Avoid vague badges

100% safe

Government approved

Fully certified

Police verified

Best sitter

unless the claim is literally true, current and documented.

A “police verification” badge should appear only when the relevant check was actually completed, with the type and date visible. It must not imply government endorsement or guarantee that the provider presents no risk.

Pillar 2: Visible service

The customer should be able to see that the promised care happened.

National service-proof standard

Start check-in

End check-out

GPS route where appropriate

Time and duration

Photo or media proof with consent

Toilet and behaviour notes

Food, water or medication confirmation

Incident flag

Digital report card

GPS tracking and live updates are already promoted by existing Indian dog-walking services. In competitive metros, these are becoming expected product features rather than sufficient premium differentiators.

Your differentiation must therefore combine tracking with:

Better caregiver continuity

Better backup fulfilment

Better reports

Faster problem resolution

Stronger society protocols

Pillar 3: Reliable continuity

Use:

Preferred caregiver with approved backup coverage

Do not use:

Guaranteed same sitter forever

Recommended service targets:

### Table 77

| Metric | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Preferred-caregiver fulfilment | 85%+ |
| Primary or approved backup fulfilment | 98%+ |
| Unintroduced replacement | Below 2% |
| Provider no-show | Below 1% |

The brand promise should be continuity with resilience—not dependence on one person.

Pillar 4: Local readiness

A city page should never imply city-wide coverage when only one cluster is operational.

Every local page must show:

Exact active neighbourhoods

Services currently available

Current operating hours

Starting prices

Verified local provider count

Backup coverage where applicable

Local support contact

Local urgent-care directory

Waitlist for unsupported areas

This prevents national marketing from outrunning actual city operations.

Urgent-care directory

Local support details

Events and campaigns

Provider radius

Same-day availability

Partner payout

Backup reserve

Stop-sales limits

### Table 78

| --- | --- | --- |

The labels “premium,” “high willingness to pay” and “strong digital conversion” should remain market hypotheses until paid conversion and contribution data validate them.

6. Bengaluru strategy

Recommended positioning

Reliable app-first pet care for busy Bengaluru pet parents—verified walkers, live updates and dependable backup coverage.

Recommended hero

Bruno’s routine, handled—even on your busiest days.

Flagship product

Active Weekday Walk Plan

20 × 30-minute walks

Primary walker preference

Approved backup

Live check-in and report

Monthly activity summary

Pause and reschedule controls

Existing operators already advertise monthly walking plans and GPS-enabled service in Bengaluru. A new entrant therefore cannot compete only on “book from an app”; it needs visibly stronger fulfilment, continuity and service recovery.

Corrected cluster rollout

Cluster 1: Whitefield micro-market

Whitefield

Brookefield

Kundalahalli

Selected Hoodi/Mahadevapura-adjacent societies where travel times work

This is the strongest grouping in your original plan.

Cluster 2A: HSR

HSR Layout

Selected nearby societies

Dedicated morning/evening provider pool

Cluster 2B: Bellandur–Sarjapur corridor

Bellandur

Selected Sarjapur Road communities

Strict peak-window radii

Do not treat HSR, Bellandur and all of Sarjapur Road as one undifferentiated operating zone.

Cluster 3A: Koramangala

Cluster 3B: Indiranagar

They may share branding but should have separate peak-capacity calculations.

Cluster 4A: JP Nagar

Cluster 4B: Electronic City

Electronic City and JP Nagar should not be grouped as one fulfilment cluster simply because they are both in south Bengaluru.

Bengaluru recorded a 74.4% average congestion level in TomTom’s 2025 index. I therefore recommend smaller zones and time-window-specific provider radii rather than city-wide matching.

GO, but split the proposed large clusters into smaller operational units.

7. Pune strategy

Recommended positioning

Dependable monthly dog-walking plans designed for Pune’s apartment communities.

Recommended hero

A consistent walking routine—without paying for more than your dog needs.

Flagship products

Regular Plan: 12 walks

Active Plan: 20 walks

Optional grooming credit

Society resident pricing

A current provider publicly advertises monthly Pune walking plans, including separate pricing for 20- and 40-minute walks. Customers can therefore compare unit price, making transparent value and service quality especially important.

Corrected cluster rollout

Cluster 1A: Baner–Balewadi

A coherent apartment and premium-residential cluster.

Cluster 1B: Wakad

Operate Wakad separately until measured travel-time and route data prove it can share capacity with Baner–Balewadi.

The delayed approach connection to the Balewadi–Wakad bridge illustrates why apparently adjacent neighbourhoods should not automatically be treated as one reliable service route.

Cluster 2: Kharadi–Viman Nagar

Kharadi

Viman Nagar

Selected Kalyani Nagar coverage only when capacity permits

Cluster 3A: Hinjawadi

Cluster 3B: Pimple Saudagar

Do not assume one walker pool can efficiently cover both during peak periods.

Cluster 4A: Aundh

Cluster 4B: Koregaon Park

These are distinct micro-markets and should not share operational capacity by default.

Pune recorded a 71.1% average congestion level in 2025, with a 10 km trip averaging more than 33 minutes. That strongly supports micro-cluster operations.

rather than:

8. Mumbai strategy

Recommended positioning

Premium pet care delivered by walkers and sitters who operate within your locality.

Recommended hero

A trusted local caregiver—not someone travelling across Mumbai.

Flagship products

12-walk local routine

20-walk premium local plan

At-home grooming

Pet sitting where building and provider controls are proven

Current Mumbai providers already advertise monthly dog-walking plans. The defensible premium is therefore not simply a monthly subscription; it is locality-level reliability, better building access and low reassignment friction.

Corrected cluster rollout

Cluster 1: Powai

Hiranandani Gardens

Powai

Selected Chandivali coverage

Nearby societies only

Cluster 2: Bandra–Khar

Cluster 3: Andheri West–Juhu

Cluster 4: Lower Parel–Worli

Cluster 5: Thane premium micro-market

Treat Thane as a separate city operation or satellite market with:

Its own manager or lead

Own pricing

Own partner pool

Own P&L

Own backup system

Do not use walkers from the main Mumbai clusters as routine Thane backup.

Mumbai’s average congestion level was 63.2% in 2025, and a 10 km drive averaged nearly 29 minutes. I therefore recommend travel-time polygons rather than a single universal 1–2 km radius.

Radius rule

Use:

1–2 km during the most congested periods where density permits

2–4 km during off-peak periods

Same-building or same-society route blocks

Maximum service-to-service travel-time limits

A fixed radius should not replace actual route-time data.

Walking

Pet sitting

Grooming at home

Vet coordination

Boarding only after host/facility controls are proven

Do not lead with home boarding merely because customer spending is high. Validate building restrictions, host capacity, escape prevention and emergency procedures first.

Main risk

Small operational delays can rapidly invalidate a premium promise.

Control

Exact locality pages

No cross-city provider matching

Realistic arrival windows

Local primary and backup pools

Building-access records

Stop-sales rules by peak window

Mumbai decision

GO later, after the first cities have mature routing, backup and support systems.

Ahmedabad’s 2025 residential market remained relatively affordable among large cities while higher-value demand increased around corridors such as SG Highway and SP Ring Road. This supports testing both accessible plans and premium micro-markets rather than one uniform city price.

Recommended positioning

14. Local proof hierarchy

Use the strongest proof available in this order:

Completed local services

Local repeat customers

Local verified reviews

Local society partnerships

Local provider fulfilment data

Local service capacity

National brand statistics

Do not make a new Surat page appear trusted by displaying only Bengaluru volume.

Use:

18 verified caregivers available in Vesu and Piplod.

rather than:

5,000 providers nationwide.

The local customer needs evidence that their booking can be fulfilled in their neighbourhood.

15. Brand and claims governance

Create a national claim registry.

### Table 80

| Claim | Evidence required |
| --- | --- |
| Verified sitter | Verification records |
| Background checked | Check type and completion date |
| Live tracking | Working product capability |
| Same-walker support | Continuity metrics and backup process |
| 24/7 support | Staffed service with documented SLA |
| Emergency support | Exact coordination scope |
| Best rated | Reliable comparative evidence |
| Starting at ₹X | Real bookable service at that price |
| Available in city | At least one active bookable zone |

Consumer-protection guidance prohibits deceptive interface and advertising practices. Claims, prices and availability must match the real product and should not use false urgency or misleading service badges.

Phase 10 brand win condition

Customers recognise one trusted national company, while every city page, offer, provider pool and service promise feels genuinely local and is supported by real operational capacity.

Multi-City Brand Strategy: APPROVED—with one master brand, smaller service clusters, evidence-based local claims and no city-wide marketing ahead of fulfilment.

Phase 10: Six-Month Multi-City Expansion Plan 🌍🐾

End-to-End City 2 and City 3 Execution Blueprint

Executive verdict

Approve the six-month structure, but treat it as a controlled replication programme—not a commitment to fully scale two cities within six months.

A realistic Month 6 outcome is:

City 1: mature and operating independently

City 2: validated, with one or two stable clusters

City 3: closed beta or early public launch

National system: shared dashboards, SOPs, finance controls and quality standards

The plan should not force City 3 forward merely because the calendar reaches Month 4. City 3 preparation begins only after City 2 passes defined quality, supply and economic gates.

India’s pet-care category is growing, supported by pet humanisation, increasing consumer spending and expanding digital discovery. That creates a favourable market backdrop, but it does not eliminate the need for local density and reliable fulfilment.

Correct expansion sequence

City 1 readiness

↓

City 2 demand and supply validation

↓

City 2 closed beta

↓

City 2 quality and contribution gate

↓

City 3 preparation

↓

City 3 closed beta

↓

Network operating-system review

Critical changes to your original plan

1. Launch the City 2 landing page earlier

Do not wait until Week 8.

A waitlist-oriented page should go live during Weeks 2–3 to collect:

Qualified leads

Service requirements

Areas

Preferred schedules

Pet profiles

Price sensitivity

Society information

Week 8 should upgrade the page from a research page into a launch-ready local page with verified services, prices and supported areas.

2. Do not treat 100 sitter applications as the Month 2 success metric

Applications are only top-of-funnel activity.

A healthier target is:

### Table 83

| Supply stage | Month 2 target |
| --- | --- |
| Applications | 50–100 |
| Contact screened | 30–60 |
| Documents verified | 15–30 |
| Practical trial passed | 10–20 |
| Launch-active providers | 8–15 |
| Usable backup providers | 3–5 |

Ten schedulable, trained providers are more valuable than 100 inactive applicants.

3. Do not require ten vet/groomer partners before beta

A better launch pool is:

3–5 reliable groomers

2–5 verified veterinary referral options

2–4 trainers only where training is launching

Boarding providers only after inspections and custody controls pass

The city does not need a large directory. It needs a small, reliable local network.

4. Do not launch subscriptions automatically in Week 11

Subscriptions require:

Successful individual services

Positive ratings

Repeat demand

Stable primary and backup capacity

Credit tracking

Renewal, pause and cancellation controls

In a new city, Week 11 should begin with starter packs. Monthly subscriptions should launch only to customers with established usage and only in clusters with reserved capacity.

5. City 3 must be gated

City 3 preparation should be blocked unless City 2 has:

95%+ service completion

4.6+ rating

Below 1% provider no-show

Below 5% refunds or disputes

Working backup coverage

No unresolved critical incident

Positive or clearly improving contribution

A local operator who can run City 2 without founder firefighting

Privacy requirement

The city waitlist, pet profiles, customer addresses and provider records should use clear notices, purpose-limited access and city-scoped permissions. India’s DPDP Rules were notified in November 2025 with phased commencement, making it prudent to build full data controls before—not after—multi-city scale.

Six-month go/no-go system

Continue expansion when

City 1 remains stable.

City 2 reaches validated-cluster status.

City 3 beta quality is strong.

Each city has independent ownership.

Each city’s P&L reconciles.

Provider payouts remain current.

Central support meets its SLA.

No critical safety or privacy issue remains open.

Slow expansion when

City 2 demand is strong but capacity is weak.

City 3 supply recruitment is delayed.

Support workload rises sharply.

City contributions remain negative but are improving.

Society pilots do not convert.

Subscriptions exceed backup capacity.

Stop new-city launches when

Completion falls below 95%.

Provider no-show exceeds 1%.

Critical incidents are concealed or unresolved.

Partner payouts are delayed.

Customer refunds increase materially.

City finance cannot reconcile payments.

The founder is again personally coordinating daily bookings.

Cross-city data access is uncontrolled.

Final approval

### Table 97

| Proposed month | Decision |
| --- | --- |
| Month 1 readiness | Approved |
| Month 2 City 2 preparation | Approved with smaller active-supply targets |
| Month 3 controlled launch | Approved |
| Subscription launch in Week 11 | Conditional—starter packs first |
| Month 4 City 3 preparation | Only after City 2 passes its gate |
| Month 5 City 3 launch | Approved as controlled beta |
| City 3 subscription push in Week 20 | Replace with retention/starter-pack push initially |
| Month 6 operating-system standardisation | Approved—mandatory |
| Decide next two cities in Week 24 | Evaluate, do not automatically approve |

Phase 10 win condition

At the end of six months, the business has reproduced its operating model in City 2, started proving it in City 3, and can compare demand, quality, supply, subscriptions and contribution across all cities without relying on founder firefighting.

Six-Month Phase 10 Expansion Plan: GO—with sequential city gates, starter packs before subscriptions, and City 3 blocked until City 2 demonstrates stable quality and viable economics.

Phase 10 City Budget, Ownership and Quality-Control System 🌍🐾

Executive decision

Your direction is correct:

Start new cities lean.

Keep the first major cities company-owned.

Use franchise or operating partners only after safety and operating systems are mature.

Standardise quality centrally while allowing controlled local commercial flexibility.

The proposed budget ranges are useful as initial planning estimates, but they currently understate several costs:

Field operations support

Verification and training

Partner-payout working capital

Refund and service-recovery reserves

Legal, accounting and compliance

Insurance

Staff travel and local transport

Central technology and support allocation

The recommended structure is:

Company-Owned City

↓

Lean Closed Beta

↓

Validated Cluster

↓

Controlled Growth Budget

↓

Contribution-Positive City

↓

Premium Scale Budget

Do not begin with the premium budget merely because the city is Bengaluru, Mumbai or Gurugram.

Part I — Lean City Launch Budget

1. Review of the proposed lean budget

### Table 98

| Cost item | Proposed range | Assessment |
| --- | --- | --- |
| City manager/operations | ₹40,000–₹1,00,000 | Plausible for one lead, but may exclude an operations assistant |
| Sitter recruitment | ₹10,000–₹30,000 | Low but possible for referral-led recruitment |
| Local marketing | ₹30,000–₹1,00,000 | Appropriate for one or two micro-markets |
| Society events | ₹20,000–₹75,000 | Appropriate if events are limited and pre-booked |
| Tools/operations | ₹10,000–₹30,000 | May exclude travel, communications and verification |
| Emergency buffer | ₹25,000–₹50,000 | Useful, but should be separated from working capital |
| Proposed monthly total | ₹1.35L–₹3.85L | Bare operating range, not total city funding requirement |

Current salary references show why the city-manager range is broad. Indeed reported an Indian operations-manager average of about ₹32,426 monthly in June 2026, while Glassdoor’s broader self-reported range is considerably higher, reflecting differences in seniority, city, team size and P&L responsibility. A genuine city general manager in Bengaluru or Mumbai will usually require a different budget from a junior launch coordinator.

2. Corrected lean monthly operating budget

A. City team

### Table 99

| Item | Recommended monthly budget |
| --- | --- |
| City manager/launch owner | ₹40,000–₹1,00,000 |
| Operations/onboarding generalist | ₹25,000–₹60,000 |
| Shared central support allocation | ₹10,000–₹30,000 |
| Team subtotal | ₹75,000–₹1,90,000 |

At the earliest stage, one experienced city manager may perform part of the operations role. Once the city exceeds approximately 150–300 monthly services, separate live operations from supply recruitment.

B. Supply recruitment and activation

### Table 100

| Item | Recommended monthly budget |
| --- | --- |
| Recruitment campaigns | ₹8,000–₹25,000 |
| Referral incentives | ₹5,000–₹15,000 |
| Document/background processes | ₹5,000–₹20,000 |
| Training and practical trials | ₹5,000–₹20,000 |
| Supply subtotal | ₹20,000–₹60,000 |

Do not measure recruitment success by applications alone.

The real metric is:

[\text{Cost per activated provider}

\frac{\text{Recruitment and verification cost}}{\text{Providers passing trial and accepting work}}]

A city that spends ₹30,000 and activates ten dependable providers is healthier than one that spends ₹15,000 and produces 100 inactive applications.

C. Local customer acquisition

### Table 101

| Item | Recommended monthly budget |
| --- | --- |
| Local search/social advertising | ₹20,000–₹75,000 |
| Referral and starter offers | ₹5,000–₹25,000 |
| Content and local creative | ₹5,000–₹20,000 |
| Marketing subtotal | ₹30,000–₹1,20,000 |

Paid campaigns should be restricted to:

Supported postcodes

Active service clusters

Available services

Time windows with usable supply

Areas below capacity limits

Do not pay for city-wide demand when operations cover only one locality.

D. Society partnerships and events

### Table 102

| Item | Recommended monthly budget |
| --- | --- |
| Small resident workshop | ₹5,000–₹15,000 |
| Grooming/training event | ₹10,000–₹30,000 |
| Larger society camp | ₹20,000–₹50,000 |
| Printing/announcements | ₹3,000–₹10,000 |

Recommended monthly planning range:

₹20,000–₹75,000

Every event should have a funnel:

Registrations

↓

Attendance

↓

Trial booking

↓

Completed service

↓

Starter pack

↓

Subscription

Do not evaluate events using attendance alone.

AWBI guidance covers pet owners, caregivers, RWAs and apartment associations. Society campaigns should support responsible coexistence and workable gate procedures, rather than allowing local sales teams to promise restrictions or rules inconsistent with national animal-welfare standards.

E. Tools and field operations

### Table 103

| Item | Recommended monthly budget |
| --- | --- |
| Communications and support tools | ₹5,000–₹15,000 |
| Local staff/provider travel | ₹5,000–₹20,000 |
| Verification and report tools | ₹3,000–₹10,000 |
| Coworking/storage, if needed | ₹0–₹20,000 |
| Central software allocation | ₹5,000–₹20,000 |
| Operations subtotal | ₹15,000–₹50,000 |

The platform should avoid a city office during closed beta unless there is a clear operational requirement.

F. Risk, refunds and working capital

These should be separate balances.

Service-recovery reserve

Used for:

Provider replacement

Customer refunds

Goodwill credits

Emergency transport coordination

Immediate operational response

Recommended:

₹25,000–₹75,000 monthly reserve

Partner-payout working capital

Used when:

Partner payouts are due before payment-provider settlement

Refunds are pending

Subscription revenue covers future services

Society invoices have longer payment terms

Recommended:

At least two to four weeks of forecast partner payouts

Do not use the incident reserve to pay ordinary partner earnings.

### Table 104

| Category | Recommended monthly range |
| --- | --- |
| City team | ₹75,000–₹1,90,000 |
| Supply activation | ₹20,000–₹60,000 |
| Local marketing | ₹30,000–₹1,20,000 |

The upper end assumes:

A two-person city team

Multiple society activities

Higher travel or verification costs

A highly founder-assisted launch may operate near ₹1.5L–₹2.5L temporarily, but that should not be mistaken for a scalable city cost.

Budget separately for:

5. Recommended six-month lean-city funding

Using the corrected model:

[\text{Six-month cash requirement}

\text{Setup}+6 \times \text{Monthly burn}+\text{Payout working capital}]

Practical planning envelope

### Table 106

| Launch style | Six-month ring-fenced cash |
| --- | --- |
