| Standard controlled city launch | ₹18L–₹30L |
| Higher-cost metro lean launch | ₹25L–₹40L |

This excludes major central product-development costs.

Spending rule

Do not release the entire budget on Day 1.

Use staged tranches:

20% — Market validation and hiring

25% — Supply activation

25% — Closed beta

20% — Validated-cluster growth

10% — Contingency

Part II — Premium City Launch Budget

6. What “premium launch” should mean

A premium budget should buy genuine capacity and operational capability:

Full city launch team

Multiple active clusters

Strong customer acquisition

Dedicated society sales

Local support

Provider training and quality audits

Events

Backup supply

Faster service recovery

It should not simply mean spending ₹10 lakh monthly on advertising.

7. Review of the premium budget

### Table 107

| Cost item | Proposed range | Assessment |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Full city team | ₹2L–₹5L/month | Plausible for a 5–8-person mixed team |
| Marketing | ₹2L–₹10L/month | Too broad without CAC and capacity gates |
| Events/societies | ₹1L–₹5L | Appropriate only with multiple active clusters |
| Operations buffer | ₹1L–₹3L | Necessary, but should be split |
| Proposed total | ₹6L–₹23L/month | Broadly plausible for growth, not initial validation |

A complete premium budget should also explicitly include:

Provider acquisition

Quality audits

Insurance/compliance

Local support

Payment and refund reserves

Technology allocation

Corrected premium range

₹7L–₹25L per month

Indicative six-month cash envelope

₹50L–₹1.7Cr, including approximately 15–20% contingency.

The top end is justified only when the city already supports multiple dense clusters.

8. Premium-spend unlock gates

Premium spending should begin only after the city demonstrates:

### Table 108

| Requirement | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Monthly completed services | 300–500+ |
| Paying customers | 100–200+ |
| Repeat customers | 25–40% |
| Average rating | 4.6+ |
| Completion rate | 95%+ |
| Provider no-show | Below 1% |
| Refund/dispute rate | Below 5% |
| Active local supply | 10–25 providers |
| Backup coverage | 90–95%+ |
| Direct contribution | Positive or clearly improving |
| Critical unresolved incidents | Zero |

Marketing scale rule

Increase customer-acquisition spending only when:

[\text{Incremental contribution}

\text{Incremental acquisition and fulfilment cost}]

Do not scale advertising when:

Peak slots are full

Provider cancellations are rising

Support SLAs are failing

Customer ratings are falling

Partner payouts are delayed

Subscription capacity lacks backup

Part III — Company-Owned vs Franchise Expansion

9. Company-owned cities

Advantages

Direct control over verification

Consistent safety processes

Better training

Centralised customer experience

Reliable data

Easier pricing control

Direct city P&L visibility

Faster intervention after incidents

Disadvantages

Higher capital requirements

Slower geographic expansion

Greater recruitment burden

Higher central-management complexity

Greater fixed-cost exposure

Best use

Company-owned operations should be used for:

The first three to five cities

Major brand-defining metros

Cities containing new service experiments

Markets with complex routing or high expectations

Cities used to train future regional operators

Recommendation

Keep these company-owned initially:

First/core city

Bengaluru

Pune

Mumbai

Gurugram

Ahmedabad, if it is the core operating base

The exact list should follow the actual expansion sequence rather than an arbitrary population ranking.

10. Traditional franchise cities

Advantages

Lower central capital requirement

Faster local hiring

Local relationship advantage

Local society access

Faster geographic coverage

Risks

Provider-verification shortcuts

Different refund practices

Hidden incidents

Inconsistent training

Poor payment discipline

Unauthorised pricing

Customer-data leakage

Weak sitter payouts

Brand damage spreading nationally

A franchise arrangement in India requires a carefully drafted commercial contract and authorised trademark use. Franchise disputes are expressly recognised among commercial disputes, while the underlying relationship also involves ordinary contract and trademark law.

Franchise operation does not remove the need for consumer grievance handling, fair e-commerce conduct or transparent cancellation and refund rules.

11. Recommended hybrid: Managed City Operator

Before offering a conventional franchise, use a more controlled model:

Managed City Operating Partner

Central company owns

Brand

Technology

Customer accounts and data

Payment collection

Provider payout infrastructure

Verification standards

Final provider approval

Price architecture

Refund rules

Incident classification

Customer-support standards

National marketing

Subscription and credit system

Quality auditing

Local operating partner owns

Field recruitment

Practical trials under central standards

Local availability management

Society outreach

Local events

Partner relationships

Daily schedule coordination

Local service recovery

Local team management

Central company must retain step-in rights

The central team must be able to:

Pause a city

Pause a service

Suspend a provider

Take over customer support

Freeze acquisition

Audit customer and provider records

Replace the operator

Terminate brand access

12. What a franchise or operating partner must never control alone

Do not delegate final authority over:

Critical-incident classification

Deletion of complaints or reviews

Provider identity verification

Safety-policy exceptions

Customer refunds

Customer funds

Partner-payout records

Medical claims

Customer-data exports

Subscription-credit adjustments

City-quality reporting

The DPDP Rules, 2025 have phased commencement, but a multi-operator model should already use purpose-specific notices, role-based access, security safeguards and grievance handling.

Platform-worker and aggregator obligations must also be assessed consistently across company-owned and operator-managed cities. The Code on Social Security recognises gig and platform workers, and 2026 central-rule developments reinforce the need for worker registration, engagement and reporting systems.

13. Franchise-readiness gate

Do not offer franchise rights until:

### Table 109

| Requirement | Minimum condition |
| --- | --- |
| Successfully replicated cities | 2–3 company-owned cities |
| Operating history | 9–12 months of stable data |
| Verification process | Standardised and auditable |
| Training academy | Operational |
| City dashboards | Real-time and standardised |
| City P&L | Reconciled consistently |
| Incident system | Centralised |
| Provider payout system | Centralised |
| Customer support | Central or centrally monitored |
| Brand/trademark protection | Complete |
| Operator agreement | Legally reviewed |
| Audit and step-in rights | Contractual |
| Critical-incident playbook | Tested |
| Data-access system | City scoped |
| Franchise pilot | One controlled territory |

14. Recommended franchise economics

Avoid rewarding an operator on total customer GMV.

An operator should be rewarded for:

Completed services

Positive city contribution

Customer retention

Quality

Society conversion

Provider reliability

Better compensation model

Fixed local management fee

+

Performance share of city contribution

+

Quality and growth bonus

Illustrative structure:

Small fixed operating fee

20–35% share of contribution after direct city costs

Bonus for quality, repeat rate and society renewals

No share on refunded or disputed transactions

Penalties for concealed incidents or payout failures

This is strategically safer than giving a local operator a large percentage of GMV regardless of service quality.

### Table 110

Final recommended expansion ownership model

Cities 1–3

Company owned and directly operated

Cities 4–5

Company owned or tightly managed operating partners

Selective managed operators/franchise pilots

only after central quality systems are mature

Phase 10 budget principle

Spend enough to create local service density and reliable backup capacity, but release growth capital only after demand, fulfilment and contribution have been proven.

Phase 10 ownership principle

Delegate local execution, but never delegate final control over safety, customer funds, verification, incidents, refunds, data or brand standards.

Phase 10 quality-control principle

Every city may speak differently and price differently, but every pet must receive the same minimum safety, verification, documentation and incident-response standard.

Final decision: START LEAN, KEEP THE FIRST MAJOR CITIES COMPANY-OWNED, AND DEFER FRANCHISING UNTIL THE OPERATING SYSTEM HAS BEEN PROVEN ACROSS MULTIPLE CITIES.

Phase 10: Central–Local Operating Model, KPIs and Expansion Risk System 🌍🐾

Executive decision

Your centralisation model is fundamentally correct. The strongest structure is:

Centralise trust, technology, money, data and safety. Localise fulfilment, relationships and market execution.

The corrected operating model is:

### Table 113

| Function category | Operating rule |
| --- | --- |
| Non-negotiable standards | Central |
| Customer funds and financial controls | Central |
| Data, permissions and security | Central |
| Daily service delivery | Local |
| Local relationships and supply | Local |
| Customer recovery and incidents | Hybrid |
| Pricing and marketing | Local within central guardrails |

This avoids two dangerous extremes:

Over-centralisation: local teams cannot respond quickly enough.

Over-localisation: every city develops different safety, refund and verification standards.

Part I — What should be centralised versus local

1. Final responsibility matrix

### Table 114

| Function | Recommended model | Central responsibility | Local responsibility |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Brand | Central | Identity, positioning, claims, design system | Local proof and city messaging |
| Technology | Central | Product, APIs, dashboards, releases | Configuration and operational use |
| Payments | Central | Gateway, invoicing, refunds, reconciliation | Payment follow-up and local exception evidence |
| Data and security | Central | Access model, encryption, audits, retention | Follow access rules and report breaches |
| Safety standards | Central | Verification, risk rules, incident taxonomy | Execute protocols and escalate |
| Training content | Central | Curriculum, assessments, certification language | Training delivery and local coaching |
| City operations | Local | Tools and national SLAs | Scheduling, capacity and fulfilment |
| Society outreach | Local | Contract templates and policy guardrails | RWA relationships, events and pilots |
| Vet/groomer partnerships | Hybrid | Eligibility standards and commercial rules | Recruitment and relationship management |
| Customer support | Hybrid | Central Level 1 support and national policies | Physical recovery and city escalation |
| Emergency response | Hybrid | Incident command and national SOP | Immediate local containment |
| Marketing | Hybrid | Brand assets and claim approval | Channels, language and local campaigns |
| Pricing | Hybrid | Margin floor, price architecture and approvals | Local price recommendations |
| Provider payouts | Central | Ledger, payout rules and reconciliation | Validate service completion |
| Finance and tax | Central | Accounting model, tax controls and city P&L | Local expense evidence and forecasts |
| Quality audits | Central/hybrid | Audit methodology and independent review | Corrective action execution |

2. Brand: central strategy, local evidence

The national team should control:

Brand identity

Verification language

Safety claims

Report-card format

Review methodology

Refund principles

Preferred-caregiver wording

Advertising disclosures

The city team should provide:

Local customer reviews

Local society partnerships

Active caregiver counts

Supported neighbourhoods

City-specific languages

Local service photographs

Local campaign stories

Correct example

National promise

Verified local pet care, documented every visit.

Ahmedabad expression

Trusted local care for Ahmedabad families and apartment communities.

Mumbai expression

Premium caregivers operating within your locality.

The local team may change the message’s emphasis, but not the underlying safety or verification meaning.

3. Technology: central platform, local configuration

Technology should remain centrally owned because multiple city-specific codebases would create:

Inconsistent features

Security gaps

Expensive maintenance

Different credit and refund logic

Reporting incompatibility

Local teams should configure—not rewrite—the system:

Active service zones

Operating hours

Service availability

Provider pools

Capacity limits

Local prices

Society programmes

Notification language

Support contacts

Central product platform

↓

City configuration

↓

Service-zone configuration

↓

Local provider and capacity assignment

No city should be allowed to create an unofficial spreadsheet-based payment, subscription or incident system outside the main platform.

4. Payments: fully centralised

Central payment control should include:

Payment-provider accounts

Subscription mandates

Customer invoices

Partner payout ledger

Refund approval workflow

Failed-payment recovery

Tax records

Reconciliation

Fraud monitoring

Payment-link generation

Local city teams may:

Contact customers about an open invoice

Submit service-failure evidence

Request a refund

Recommend a goodwill credit

Confirm whether a service occurred

They should not:

Mark an unpaid invoice as paid

Collect unofficial cash

Change provider payout records

Issue undocumented refunds

Create local bank or UPI accounts for customer collections

Adjust subscription credits without an audit entry

5. Data and security: central and non-delegable

Multi-city operations process sensitive operational data such as:

Customer addresses

Recurring home schedules

Society gate instructions

Pet behavioural notes

Provider identity documents

Payment references

Incident information

India’s notified DPDP Rules, 2025 establish the current national data-protection framework and include a phased enforcement timeline. City growth should therefore use central data governance, role-based access, security safeguards and auditable grievance handling rather than giving every city broad database access.

Recommended access model

### Table 115

| Role | Permitted access |
| --- | --- |
| Walker | Only assigned service and necessary pet instructions |
| City operations | Scheduling and service-recovery data |
| Customer support | Customer, booking and complaint data |
| Society executive | Society account data, not private pet records |
| City manager | City operations and aggregated performance |
| Finance | Payment and settlement information |
| Safety team | Incidents and required supporting records |
| National administration | Cross-city data according to role |

A Pune team member should not be able to browse Mumbai customer addresses merely because both cities use the same platform.

6. Safety: central standards, local execution

The national safety team should define:

Provider verification levels

Pet-risk classification

Service permissions

Backup requirements

Boarding standards

Grooming hygiene rules

Incident severity

Evidence requirements

Suspension criteria

Root-cause review

Corrective actions

The local team should:

Conduct practical assessments

Verify local availability

Execute emergency containment

Contact the customer

Coordinate local veterinary assistance

Preserve evidence

Remove unsafe providers from active schedules

Implement corrective actions

Incident authority

A city manager can open and escalate an incident, but should not independently:

Reduce the recorded severity

Delete the incident

Close a critical case

Restore a suspended provider

Hide a customer complaint

Critical closure should require central safety approval.

7. Training: central curriculum, local delivery

Centralised training content ensures every city teaches the same fundamentals:

Pet handling

Leash and gate safety

Escape prevention

Customer communication

Service reports

Privacy

Incident reporting

Medication boundaries

Aggression escalation

Cancellation and backup procedures

Local teams may add modules for:

Society access

Local language

Climate and weather

Local travel conditions

City-specific service procedures

Certification language

The platform may issue:

Platform course completed

Practical assessment passed

Dog-walking assessment passed

Cat-care module completed

It should not call internal training a national or government certification unless that status is formally supported.

8. City operations: local ownership

City operations must be local because they require real-time knowledge of:

Traffic

Provider locations

Society entry

Weather disruption

Local events

Provider leave

Customer availability

Emergency providers

The city operations team should own:

Today’s services

↓

Assignment

↓

Check-in

↓

Completion

↓

Report

↓

Customer recovery

Central teams provide software and standards; the city team owns the actual outcome.

9. Society outreach: local within central guardrails

Society acquisition depends on local trust and cannot be managed entirely from a national office.

Local teams should own:

RWA and facility-manager meetings

Resident demand assessment

Gate protocols

Society events

Pilot implementation

Local escalation contacts

Pilot-to-paid conversion

Central teams should supply:

Agreements

Data-sharing terms

Safety presentation

Event SOP

Monthly report format

Commercial boundaries

Brand claims

AWBI publishes national guidance concerning pet owners, caregivers, RWAs and apartment associations. City teams may adapt operational arrangements, but they should not promise society policies that conflict with the national welfare framework.

10. Vet and groomer partnerships: hybrid

Local teams are best placed to find and manage:

Groomers

Trainers

Veterinary clinics

Home-visit professionals

Boarding facilities

Event providers

Central teams must control:

Verification requirements

Service permissions

Contract templates

Scorecards

Customer-data access

Medical-claim restrictions

Payout and refund structures

Veterinary boundary

The platform coordinates access to registered professionals. It does not allow local staff, support agents or AI systems to give final diagnoses.

11. Customer support: hybrid three-level system

Level 1 — Central support

Handles:

Booking questions

Credit balance

Payment status

Subscription changes

Basic cancellations

Report access

General service information

Level 2 — City operations

Handles:

Walker late or missing

Backup assignment

Society gate denial

Customer access issue

Local rescheduling

Physical service recovery

Level 3 — Central specialist escalation

Handles:

Serious incidents

Privacy cases

Large refunds

Legal complaints

Fraud

Medical-boundary issues

Repeated city quality failure

India’s E-Commerce Rules require consumer-facing e-commerce entities to maintain grievance-handling arrangements. A hybrid model works only when the customer has one visible support path and the central system records the entire escalation.

12. Emergency response: local action, central command

The first response must be local because the incident is happening in that city.

Local responsibilities

Secure the pet

Contact the customer

Stop the service

Contact an appropriate clinic

Activate local transport where available

Preserve photographs, location and timeline

Suspend the provider temporarily where required

Central responsibilities

Classify severity

Guide customer communication

Review legal/privacy implications

Approve major refunds

Manage insurers or advisors

Conduct root-cause analysis

Issue network-wide corrective action

Local containment

+

Central governance

=

Effective incident response

13. Marketing: central creative system, local activation

Central marketing should own:

Brand identity

Master campaigns

Safety claims

Creative templates

National website

Analytics framework

Attribution methodology

Local marketing should own:

City language

Local channels

Society promotion

Regional creators

City events

Local testimonials

Neighbourhood-specific search campaigns

Local teams should not independently claim:

“100% safe”

“Guaranteed same sitter”

“Best in the city”

“Police verified”

without centrally approved evidence.

Part II — Correcting the city-level KPIs

14. Autopsy of the proposed KPI table

Your proposed targets contain three important mathematical issues.

1,000 bookings and 500 customers

This equals an average of:

completed services per customer per month.

That may be reasonable for a mixed-service marketplace, but it is low for a walking-subscription-led city. Track customer frequency distribution—not only the average.

1,000 bookings and 50 active sitters

This equals:

services per provider per month.

That may produce weak provider earnings and high supply churn unless many providers are intentionally part-time or provide other services.

₹5 lakh revenue and 1,000 bookings

This equals:

per completed service.

This is meaningful only after defining whether ₹5 lakh means:

Customer GMV

Platform-recognised revenue

Gross profit

City contribution

15. Corrected mature-city KPI scorecard

### Table 116

| KPI | Proposed | Corrected recommendation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Completed services/month | 1,000+ | Approve for a mature city |
| Monthly active paying customers | 500+ | 300–500+ |
| Ninety-day active customers | Not specified | 500–800+ |
| Active providers | 50+ | 15–30 core active; 30–60 verified bench |
| Repeat customer rate | 35%+ | Approve; target 35–50% |
| Rating | 4.6+ | Approve with sufficient review volume |
| Completion rate | Not specified | 95%+ |
| On-time rate | Not specified | 95%+ |
| Provider no-show | Not specified | Below 1% |
| Refund/dispute rate | Not specified | Below 3–5% |
| Recurring revenue | Growing | 20–40% of platform revenue where appropriate |
| Gross margin | 25–35% | Approve only with consistent accounting definition |
| City contribution | Positive | Positive for at least three months |
| Critical incidents | Included in 3% | Zero unresolved |
| Serious incident rate | Included in 3% | Below approximately 0.1–0.3% |
| Minor reportable incident rate | Included in 3% | Below 1% |

16. Bookings KPI

The primary city-volume metric should be:

Completed paid service occurrences

Report separately:

Orders

Scheduled bookings

Completed services

Cancelled services

Refunded services

Subscription credits consumed

A 20-walk subscription may be:

One commercial order

Twenty scheduled bookings

Up to twenty completed services

Do not mix those units.

Stage targets

### Table 117

| City stage | Monthly completed services |
| --- | --- |
| Closed beta | 50–150 |
| Early public launch | 150–350 |
| Validated city | 300–750 |
| Mature city | 1,000+ |
| Scaled city | 2,500+ |

17. Revenue KPI

Every city dashboard must show:

Customer GMV

Total customer spending.

Platform revenue

Commission, platform fees, memberships and B2B fees retained by the company.

City contribution

Direct city costs include:

Local staffing

Payment processing

Customer acquisition

Refunds

Service recovery

Verification

Society events

Local support

Recommended gate

Do not expand the city based on ₹5 lakh of GMV alone.

Require:

Positive contribution per completed service

Positive cluster contribution

Positive city operating contribution for three months before aggressive scaling

18. Active provider KPI

Replace a fixed “50 active sitters” target with:

Active providers

Capacity supplied

Capacity consumed

Provider earnings

Provider retention

Peak-window coverage

Backup coverage

Better calculation

Example:

1,000 services

50 safe services per provider

15% reserve

Approximately 23 active providers may be sufficient, depending on service mix and peak-time concentration.

The target should therefore be:

Enough healthy provider capacity—not the largest possible provider headcount.

19. Repeat-rate KPI

Define repeat rate consistently:

Report:

30-day repeat

60-day repeat

90-day repeat

Subscription renewal

Repeat rate by acquisition channel

A customer acquired one week ago should not be treated as a failed 90-day repeat customer.

20. Subscription KPI

“Subscription MRR growing” is insufficient.

Track:

Active subscribers

New MRR

Upgrade MRR

Downgrade MRR

Churned MRR

Renewal rate

Credit utilisation

Subscription contribution

Backup capacity required

Recommended mature-city target

First renewal: 60–80%

Mature monthly churn: below 10–15%

Recurring platform-revenue share: 25–40%

Credit utilisation: 70–90%

Subscription fulfilment: 95%+

21. Rating KPI

A city’s average should be accompanied by:

Number of reviews

Rating distribution

Percentage of services reviewed

Recent 30-day rating

Low-rating count

Complaint rate

A rating of 4.8 from 15 reviews is less reliable than 4.7 from 1,500 reviews.

Recommended gate:

4.6+ average

At least 50 verified reviews for a validated city

No major service category below 4.4

No unresolved pattern of low ratings

22. Incident KPI

Reject one combined target of “below 3%.”

Use severity-based reporting:

### Table 118

| Category | Example | Target |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Critical | Pet escape, death, serious fraud, major data breach | Zero preventable and zero unresolved |
| Serious | Injury, bite, unsafe handover | Below 0.1–0.3% |
| Minor reportable | Small delay-related welfare concern, minor service issue | Below 1% |
| General complaint | Report quality, communication, timing | Below 3–5% |
| Refund/dispute | Financial/service dispute | Below 3–5% |

A city with 2.5% minor communication incidents is very different from a city with 2.5% pet escapes.

23. Gross-margin KPI

Gross margin is valid only when every city uses the same accounting basis.

If partner payouts are recorded as cost of service:

If the platform recognises only its commission as revenue, the denominator is different.

Therefore:

Use a central finance definition.

Do not allow each city manager to calculate margin independently.

Show contribution alongside gross margin.

Review margin by service, cluster and acquisition channel.

Part III — Correcting the national KPIs

24. Recommended national scorecard

### Table 119

| KPI | Proposed | Corrected interpretation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Active cities | 2–5 | At least one mature and one validated city |
| Total completed services/month | 5,000+ | Strong network target |
| Total MRR | ₹5L–₹50L | Define platform MRR, not subscription GMV |
| Total revenue/month | ₹25L+ | Separate GMV, platform revenue and contribution |
| Average rating | 4.6+ | Volume-weighted; no public city materially below standard |
| Repeat rate | 35–50% | Report by city and customer cohort |
| City profitability | Improving | Count contribution-positive cities |
| CAC payback | Below 3 months | Mature-city target; allow longer launch payback |

25. Active-city KPI

A city should count as active only when it is accepting and completing paid bookings.

Classify cities:

Research

Waitlist

Closed beta

Public limited

Validated

Mature

Paused

Recommended Phase 10 outcome:

### Table 120

| Stage | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Mature cities | At least 1 |
| Validated cities | At least 1 |
| Beta cities | 0–2 |
| Total public cities | 2–5 |

A city landing page does not make the city active.

26. Network-booking KPI

A total of 5,000 monthly completed services can hide concentration.

Report:

Total network services

Services per city

Percentage from largest city

Services per active provider

Services by category

Subscription services

Society-origin services

Concentration warning

If City 1 produces 4,700 bookings and four other cities produce 75 each, the company has not yet built a functioning five-city network.

Recommended additional measure:

No validated city should remain below its minimum stage target without a corrective or exit decision.

27. National MRR KPI

Split recurring revenue into:

Walking subscription platform revenue

Consumer membership revenue

Society subscription revenue

Partner software revenue

Do not include:

One-time prepaid packs

One-time boarding

Future unpaid invoices

Subscription GMV owed to service providers

Better target

Instead of only ₹5L–₹50L, track:

Absolute platform MRR

MRR growth rate

MRR contribution

Recurring revenue as a percentage of platform revenue

MRR concentration by city

A network where 90% of MRR comes from one society contract carries concentration risk.

28. National revenue KPI

Use four separate values:

### Table 121

| Metric | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Network GMV | Total customer spending |
| Platform revenue | Recognised company revenue |
| Contribution | Revenue after direct operating costs |
| Operating result | Contribution after central costs |

A ₹25 lakh GMV target can be commercially weak if provider payouts, marketing and refunds consume most of it.

Recommended national gate

Network contribution positive or improving according to plan

At least one mature city contribution-positive

At least one new city with a contribution-positive cluster

No city scaling through unreconciled discounts

29. National rating KPI

Use a volume-weighted average, but preserve city minimums.

Recommended:

Network weighted rating: 4.6+

Every validated/mature city: 4.6+

No public city below 4.4 for more than one review period

Critical service categories reviewed separately

A strong Bengaluru rating must not hide poor performance in a new city.

30. National CAC payback

Use different expectations by city stage:

### Table 122

| Stage | CAC payback target |
| --- | --- |
| Closed beta | Not yet reliable |
| Early launch | Below 6–9 months or clear improvement |
| Validated city | Below 4–6 months |
| Mature city | Below 2–3 months |
| Society channel | Evaluate using contract and resident contribution |

A universal three-month requirement during launch can cause underinvestment in supply, trust and society partnerships.

Part IV — Expansion risk matrix

31. Corrected risk-control framework

### Table 123

| Risk | Impact | Leading indicator | Prevention | Stop condition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Expansion too fast | High | Support SLA drops, City 1 quality declines | Sequential city gates | Freeze new-city launches |
| Weak city manager | High | Poor reporting, unresolved queues | Structured hiring and probation scorecard | Replace or add operating support |
| Low sitter quality | High | Low ratings, missing reports | Central curriculum and local trials | Stop assigning weak providers |
| Scattered bookings | Medium-high | Travel time and cancellations rise | Small service clusters | Close distant slots |
| High CAC | Medium-high | Payback deteriorates | Society/referral-first acquisition | Pause low-return channels |
| Safety incident | Very high | Near misses and protocol breaches | Central standards and local drills | Immediate affected-service pause |
| Pricing mismatch | Medium | Low conversion or negative contribution | Local price tests within margin floor | Reprice or narrow service |
| Subscription overload | High | Peak capacity exceeds 85–90% | Capacity-controlled checkout | Stop subscription sales |
| Brand inconsistency | Medium | Different claims and policies | Central claim registry | Remove local campaign |
| Franchise failure | High | Audit gaps and incident concealment | Delay franchises and retain step-in rights | Suspend operator |
| Data leakage | Very high | Cross-city access anomalies | Central RBAC and audit logs | Revoke access and investigate |
| Delayed partner payouts | High | Provider complaints and churn | Central payout ledger and reserve | Freeze acquisition and clear arrears |
| Financial misreporting | High | GMV/revenue mismatch | Central definitions and reconciliation | Block scaling decision |
| Society concentration | Medium | One society drives excessive volume | Diversify clusters and channels | Limit dependency |
| Local partner poaching | Medium | Off-platform repeat services | Customer value, agreements and app incentives | Review or remove partner |
| Metric manipulation | High | Suspicious completions or rating patterns | Independent audits | Suspend responsible staff/provider |

32. Expansion-too-fast risk

Early signs

City 1 support response worsens

Founder becomes involved in daily booking assignments

Provider payouts are late

New-city dashboards are incomplete

Training standards are shortened

Unresolved complaints increase

Prevention

One city at a time

Gate-based budget release

Dedicated local ownership

Central capacity review

No City 3 launch until City 2 passes stability gates

33. Weak city-manager risk

A poor city manager can create:

Unsafe provider shortcuts

Inflated booking numbers

Unresolved customer complaints

Society relationship damage

Inaccurate P&L

Hidden incidents

Prevention

Use a balanced scorecard:

### Table 124

| Dimension | Weight |
| --- | --- |
| Safety and quality | 30% |
| Fulfilment and supply | 20% |
| Retention and demand | 20% |
| City economics | 20% |
| Society and strategic growth | 10% |

No growth bonus should be paid after a concealed critical incident or materially unreconciled city P&L.

34. Platform-worker risk

Provider onboarding and worker reporting should not vary casually by city. The Code on Social Security recognises gig and platform workers, and a June 2026 Labour Ministry direction states that covered aggregators must share required worker details electronically under the 2026 Rules. Classification and reporting should therefore be centrally governed even though recruitment is local.

Centralise:

Worker classification policy

Required fields

Engagement records

Earnings and payouts

eShram/reporting workflow where applicable

Document retention

Audit history

Brand

Technology

Payments

Incident governance

Phase 10 win condition

Every city can move quickly in local operations without being allowed to weaken national safety, payment, data, verification or consumer-protection standards.

A healthy multi-city network should demonstrate:

Final Phase 10 decision: APPROVED—with stage-specific KPIs, severity-based incident reporting and central non-negotiable control over money, data and safety.

A quarterly regulatory and city-scorecard review would help keep worker-reporting, privacy and consumer-support controls current.

Phase 10 Final Approval Report

Multi-City Expansion Engine, Deliverables and Phase 11 Gate 🌍🐾

Executive decision

Approve Phase 10—with controlled city gates, cluster-level launches, company-operated early cities, city-level P&Ls and centrally governed safety.

The official Phase 10 operating model should be:

Stable City 1

↓

City-selection scorecard

↓

Demand, supply, operations and safety gates

↓

One or two local clusters

↓

Closed paid beta

↓

Quality and contribution validation

↓

Cluster expansion

↓

City 3 preparation

The business is not ready for Phase 11 merely because multiple city names appear in the application. It is ready when the second city reproduces the first city’s quality, economics and operational discipline without continuous founder intervention.

1. What goes wrong in multi-city expansion

Mistake 1: Expanding before the first city works

Wrong model

100 cumulative bookings

↓

Launch five cities

↓

Demand and supply become fragmented

↓

Quality falls everywhere

A small number of historical bookings does not prove:

Repeatable demand

Stable provider capacity

Subscription retention

Positive contribution

Reliable incident handling

Independent city management

Correct expansion gate

City 1 should demonstrate, for approximately three consecutive months:

### Table 127

| Requirement | Recommended threshold |
| --- | --- |
| Completed paid services/month | 1,000+ |
| Monthly paying customers | 300–500+ |
| Repeat customer rate | 35%+ |
| Active walking subscribers | 50–100+, reconciled with service volume |
| Completion rate | 95%+ |
| On-time rate | 95%+ |
| Provider no-show | Below 1% |
| Average rating | 4.6+ |
| Refund/dispute rate | Below 3–5% |
| City operating contribution | Positive |
| Critical unresolved incidents | Zero |
| Founder-dependent fulfilment | Below 5–10% |

The key requirement is not just volume:

City 1 must operate as a system—not as a founder-managed collection of bookings.

Mistake 2: No accountable city owner

Wrong model

The central team remotely manages:

Partner recruitment

Scheduling

Society entry

Customer complaints

Backup assignments

Local emergencies

This becomes increasingly slow and inaccurate as the company adds cities.

Correct model

Every public city must have a named local owner.

Depending on maturity, that person may be:

### Table 128

| City stage | Ownership model |
| --- | --- |
| Research | City launch owner |
| Closed beta | Accountable launch/operations manager |
| 300–500 monthly services | Dedicated city manager strongly recommended |
| 1,000+ monthly services | Dedicated city manager required |
| Multiple clusters | City manager plus cluster/operations leads |

City manager accountability

The city manager owns:

Local supply

Fulfilment

Backup coverage

Society relationships

Customer recovery

City contribution

Local growth

Safety escalation

However, critical safety classification, recognised financial results, privacy controls and major refunds should remain independently governed by central teams.

Recommended city-manager scorecard

### Table 129

| Dimension | Weight |
| --- | --- |
| Quality and safety | 30% |
| Supply and fulfilment | 20% |
| Demand and retention | 20% |
| City economics | 20% |
| Society and strategic growth | 10% |

A manager should not earn a growth bonus after concealing an incident, using unverified providers or materially delaying partner payouts.

Mistake 3: No area density

Wrong model

“We have launched Mumbai.”

But customers are scattered across Powai, Bandra, Andheri, Worli and Thane, while providers travel long distances between low-value services.

This creates:

Late arrivals

Provider cancellations

Expensive backup

Low provider earnings

Weak contribution

Poor subscription continuity

Correct model

City

↓

Cluster 1

↓

Cluster 1 validated

↓

Adjacent Cluster 2

↓

Cluster 2 validated

↓

Additional local clusters

Example

For Mumbai:

Powai–Chandivali

↓

Bandra–Khar

↓

Andheri West–Juhu

↓

Lower Parel–Worli

These should remain separate provider and capacity pools until real route data proves that they can safely share supply.

Cluster-opening requirements

### Table 130

| Requirement | Recommended target |
| --- | --- |
| Qualified leads | 100–200 |
| Paid trial intent | 20–40 |
| Launch-active providers | 5–8 |
| Peak-time backups | 2–3 |
| Emergency referral network | Verified |
| Local support routing | Ready |
| Prices | Configured |
| Capacity limits | Configured |
| Projected contribution | Viable |

Cluster-validation requirements

### Table 131

| Metric | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Completed beta services | 50–150 |
| Average rating | 4.6+ |
| Completion | 95%+ |
| On-time performance | 95%+ |
| No-show rate | Below 1% |
| Provider cancellation | Below 5% |
| Refund/dispute | Below 5% |
| Critical preventable incidents | Zero |

Mistake 4: Same pricing everywhere

Wrong model

₹149 per walk in Ahmedabad, Bengaluru, Mumbai, Pune and Gurugram.

This ignores differences in:

Partner payouts

Travel time

Building and gate delays

Support expectations

Customer willingness to pay

Competition

Peak-slot scarcity

Backup costs

Society density

Correct pricing formula

Pricing should be configured by:

City

↓

Service cluster

↓

Service package

↓

Time window

↓

Published price version

Existing bookings and subscriptions must retain the pricing terms they accepted. Do not overwrite historical prices when a city publishes a new price.

Mistake 5: Franchising too early

Wrong model

One partially proven city

↓

Sell franchise rights

↓

Local operators change verification and refund rules

↓

Safety and customer experience become inconsistent

Pet care is unusually sensitive to local execution because providers enter homes, handle animals and access recurring customer schedules.

Correct ownership sequence

Cities 1–3

Company-operated

Cities 4–5

Company-operated or tightly managed partners

Later cities

Selective managed-operator/franchise pilots

Traditional franchising should begin only after the company has:

Replicated the model across at least two or three company-operated cities

Standardised verification and training

Centralised payments and provider payouts

Built city-level dashboards

Tested incident-management procedures

Established audit and step-in rights

Operated stable cities for a meaningful period

A safer intermediate structure is a managed city operator model: the local partner handles field execution, while the central company retains control over customer funds, technology, verification, safety, incidents, refunds and data.

2. Phase 10 upgrade decisions

Upgrade 1: Mandatory city-launch gates

Decision: APPROVE—NON-NEGOTIABLE

No public launch should occur until five areas are validated:

Demand

Active supply

Operations

Safety/compliance

Economics

Official gate sequence

Gate 0: City selection

Gate 1: Qualified demand

Gate 2: Active supply

Gate 3: Operational readiness

Gate 4: Closed-beta quality

Gate 5: Cluster economics

Gate 6: Public expansion

A strong demand gate cannot compensate for a failed safety gate.

Upgrade 2: First three to five cities company-operated

Decision: APPROVE WITH FLEXIBILITY

Use “company-operated” rather than requiring one exact legal ownership structure.

The first major cities should remain under direct company operational control because they establish:

Safety standards

Training standards

City staffing ratios

Unit economics

Society sales processes

Support SLAs

Incident patterns

Technology requirements

A tightly managed operator may be tested later, but not before the central operating system is mature.

Upgrade 3: Mandatory area-cluster expansion

Decision: APPROVE—NON-NEGOTIABLE

City-wide availability must not be activated as one Boolean setting.

Every service requires cluster-level control over:

Availability

Partner pool

Price

Capacity

Hours

Backup reserve

Support ownership

Stop-sales threshold

The public landing page and checkout system must read from the same serviceability configuration.

Upgrade 4: Mandatory city-level P&L dashboard

Decision: APPROVE

Track four separate financial layers.

A. GMV

Total customer spending.

B. Platform revenue

The company’s recognised commissions, service fees, memberships, subscriptions, society fees and other retained revenue.

C. City contribution

D. Fully allocated operating result

City contribution after its share of central technology, finance, support and leadership expenses.

Required P&L categories

### Table 132

| Revenue | Direct and operating costs |
| --- | --- |
| Walking | Provider payouts |
| Sitting | Payment processing |
| Boarding | Refunds and service recovery |
| Grooming/training | City team |
| Consumer subscriptions | Local support |
| Society plans | Local acquisition |
| Events | Society events |
| Memberships | Verification and training |
| Partner software/listings | Tools and local travel |

If the platform collects customer consideration for services delivered by third-party providers, its GST e-commerce-operator and TCS treatment depends on the exact transaction and settlement structure. Maintain service-, city- and provider-level ledgers and obtain a written tax position.

G. Compliance and governance gate

The DPDP Rules, 2025 were notified with a phased commencement schedule. Multi-city operations should already use clear processing notices, appropriate security safeguards, role-scoped access and auditable grievance handling for addresses, schedules, pet records and provider documents.

The Code on Social Security recognises gig and platform workers, and the Labour Ministry’s June 2026 aggregator direction states that covered aggregators must electronically share required worker details through the prescribed framework. Obtain a formal classification analysis and implement applicable worker-registration/reporting controls before wider scale.

Consumer flows must provide transparent terms, fair cancellation and an effective grievance mechanism. The CCPA’s dark-pattern guidance specifically identifies difficult or hidden subscription cancellation as a subscription trap, and it issued a 2025 self-audit advisory to e-commerce platforms.

6. Final Phase 10 recommendation

Build:

A city-by-city replication engine that uses mandatory launch gates, small service clusters, accountable city managers, versioned local pricing, society-led demand, independent city economics and centrally governed safety, payments and data.

Do

Prove City 1 before expansion.

Launch one or two clusters per city.

Reserve provider and backup capacity.

Track active supply—not applications.

Measure GMV, platform revenue and contribution separately.

Give every public city one accountable owner.

Keep payments, safety, data and incident rules central.

Use local society and partner relationships.

Expand City 3 only after City 2 passes its gate.

Close weak clusters instead of protecting vanity city counts.

Do not

Launch five cities from 100 bookings.

Call a waitlist page an active city.

advertise city-wide availability from one neighbourhood.

use one national price for every cluster.

guarantee one permanent sitter.

hide city losses inside network totals.

weaken verification to recruit faster.

issue franchise rights before the playbook is proven.

let local teams manage customer money outside the central system.

count all complaints and safety events under one ambiguous incident rate.

Final Phase 10 approval matrix

### Table 150

| Rule | Final decision |
| --- | --- |
| Mandatory city-launch gates | APPROVED—NON-NEGOTIABLE |
| First three to five cities company-operated | APPROVED AS THE DEFAULT STRATEGY |
| Area-cluster expansion | APPROVED—NON-NEGOTIABLE |
| City-level P&L dashboard | APPROVED—MANDATORY |
| Central safety standards plus local operations | APPROVED AS THE OFFICIAL OPERATING MODEL |

Phase 10 win condition

The business launches and validates a second city—and begins a third—using the same documented operating system while maintaining customer ratings, safety, provider reliability, subscription capacity and positive unit economics.

Decision before Phase 11

Move to Phase 11 only when one new city has reached validated status and the platform’s next constraint is genuinely product capability—not unresolved city operations.

Phase 11 should then focus on mobile applications, advanced tracking, matching, loyalty and automation without automating unstable operational behaviour.

Final Phase 10 decision: GO—with all five proposed rules approved and the strengthened Phase 11 gates above.

Phase 11: Mobile App and Advanced Product Phase 📱🐾

Customer App, Sitter App, Tracking, Automation and Product-Scale Architecture

Executive decision

Approve Phase 11, but split it into three controlled product waves:

Wave 1 — Sitter execution app

↓

Wave 2 — Customer mobile app

↓

Wave 3 — Advanced matching, loyalty, health records and automation

The central principle is correct:

Do not build mobile apps to look like a mature startup. Build them when native capabilities solve measurable operational and retention problems.

The strongest Phase 11 outcome is not “two apps published.” It is:

Sitter execution becomes more reliable

Customers receive better visibility and control

Operations require fewer manual interventions

Repeated bookings and subscriptions become easier

Location, payment and personal data remain controlled

Every advanced feature improves contribution or service quality

1. What changes in Phase 11

Until Phase 10, the operating stack consists mainly of:

Website or PWA

Admin dashboard

Sitter web dashboard

WhatsApp and SMS communication

Manual scheduling and escalation

Semi-automated assignment

Browser-based subscriptions and payments

This can work during initial validation because human intervention helps discover the correct operating process.

However, at multi-city scale, several problems usually emerge:

Sitters miss browser notifications

Location tracking stops when the browser closes

Media uploads fail on unstable networks

Service reports are delayed

Admins manually chase check-ins

Customers repeatedly open WhatsApp for updates

Subscription management is fragmented

Booking data and real-world execution drift apart

City teams develop informal workarounds

Phase 11 should turn the proven operating process into a dependable native product system.

2. Should Phase 11 start only at 3,000–10,000 bookings?

The proposed targets are directionally useful, but not absolute

### Table 151

| Requirement | Proposed target | Final recommendation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Active cities | 2–5 | At least one mature city and one validated second city |
| Monthly bookings | 3,000–10,000+ | 3,000+ completed services is a strong customer-app gate |
| Active subscribers | 500+ | Strong target, but sitter app can begin earlier |
| Repeat booking rate | 35–50% | Approve |
| Customer rating | 4.6+ | Approve |
| Sitter reliability | 95%+ | Approve, but track completion, punctuality and no-show separately |
| PWA usage stable | Yes | Must be measurable |
| Admin operations stable | Yes | Mandatory |
| Unit economics positive | Yes | At least original city and one replicated cluster |
| Subscription system working | Yes | Mandatory before advanced mobile subscription controls |

Better approach: separate the sitter-app gate from the customer-app gate

Sitter app can begin earlier

A sitter app may be justified at approximately:

1,000–3,000 completed services per month

20–50 active field providers

Two or more operating clusters

Repeated check-in, GPS, report or notification failures

Meaningful admin time spent monitoring service execution

The sitter app is primarily an operations and quality investment.

Customer app should usually wait longer

A customer app becomes commercially justified when:

Monthly completed services exceed approximately 3,000

Repeat rate is above 35%

A meaningful subscriber base exists

Customers regularly return to track, rebook or manage plans

Push, loyalty and subscription controls can improve retention

Customer acquisition and retention economics support app development

The customer app is mainly a retention, convenience and lifetime-value investment.

3. What “PWA usage stable” should mean

Do not use “the website works” as the definition.

Measure:

### Table 152

| Metric | Healthy signal |
| --- | --- |
| Booking completion rate | Stable or improving |
| Mobile checkout success | 95%+ technical success |
| Login success | 98%+ |
| Crash/error rate | Low and monitored |
| Customer repeat usage | 35%+ |
| Sitter dashboard adoption | 80–90%+ of active sitters |
| Report submission | 95%+ |
| Payment reconciliation | Stable |
| Subscription management | Working |
| Support issues caused by the PWA | Clearly categorised |
| App demand | Customers/sitters repeatedly requesting native capabilities |

Native-app problem test

Build the app when multiple problems genuinely require mobile-native capabilities:

Background or session-based location

Reliable camera and media capture

Offline service reports

Push notifications

Native biometric authentication

Live operational status

Device-level secure storage

Deep links

Faster repeated workflows

Do not build it merely because competitors have apps.

4. Why the sitter app should come first

Your recommendation is correct.

Sitter performance affects every transaction

A customer app can improve booking convenience. A sitter app can improve:

Arrival reliability

Service evidence

Location tracking

Safety escalation

Report submission

Backup handling

Customer communication

Admin visibility

Payout accuracy

If sitter execution fails, a polished customer interface only makes the failure more visible.

Native capabilities provide greater immediate value to sitters

A sitter app can provide:

One-tap accept or reject

Today’s schedule

Navigation

Start-service check-in

Background route tracking during an active walk

Photo/video capture

Offline report drafts

Incident button

Customer handover confirmation

Earnings and payout status

Leave and availability management

These workflows are more difficult to implement reliably in a browser.

5. Important GPS-tracking rule

Do not track sitters continuously

The correct location model is:

Sitter accepts booking

↓

Sitter travels toward service area

↓

Sitter explicitly starts service

↓

Tracking session begins

↓

Walk or visit is completed

↓

Tracking session stops

↓

Route/report saved

Avoid:

Sitter installs app

↓

Platform tracks location all day

Recommended location states

LOCATION_OFF

PRE_SERVICE_OPTIONAL

ACTIVE_SERVICE_TRACKING

PAUSED

SERVICE_COMPLETED

LOCATION_UPLOAD_PENDING

Permission design

Request location only when the sitter activates the relevant feature.

Apple requires location use to be directly relevant to the app’s function and requires notice and consent before location data is collected or used. Apple also advises developers to use background updates only when real-time background information is genuinely needed.

On Android, background location requires a separate permission model; on Android 11 and later, users generally enable “Allow all the time” from system settings. Google Play permits background location only when it is important to the app’s core functionality and requires a declaration, prominent disclosure, privacy policy and review evidence.

Best implementation

Use background location only for an active, user-started walk session.

This produces a defensible core use case:

Tracking the pet’s service route while the sitter performs the booked walk, including when the screen is locked.

Do not use location for:

Advertising

General sitter monitoring

Attendance outside booked services

Unrelated analytics

Persistent employee surveillance

Battery and accuracy

Background location can materially affect battery life, and Android may throttle background updates. Use:

Moderate update intervals

Distance-based updates

Automatic timeouts

Immediate stop after service completion

Upload batching

Location-quality indicators

Offline storage with later synchronisation

Android’s official guidance recommends removing updates when no longer needed and setting reasonable timeouts to prevent continued battery consumption.

7. Offline-first sitter architecture

The sitter app must remain usable when mobile connectivity is weak.

Features that should work offline

View cached booking details

Start and stop an assigned service

Capture location points locally

Take photographs

Draft report

Trigger emergency call actions

Record incident evidence

Synchronisation states

LOCAL_ONLY

SYNC_PENDING

SYNCING

SYNCED

SYNC_FAILED

CONFLICT_REVIEW

Important controls

Encrypt local sensitive data

Store only currently relevant bookings

Delete cached access details after the service retention window

Retry uploads automatically

Show whether the report was actually submitted

Prevent duplicate completion events

Reconcile GPS timestamps on the server

Never mark the booking completed solely because the device displays success

9. Push-notification architecture

Push notifications should improve timeliness, but must not become the system of record.

Recommended events

Customer

Booking confirmed

Sitter assigned

Sitter en route

Walk started

Service completed

Report available

Schedule changed

Payment failed

Renewal upcoming

Credit expiring

Safety/support update

Sitter

New booking

Acceptance deadline

Upcoming booking

Travel reminder

Check-in overdue

Report pending

Backup request

Payout processed

Document expiring

Support message

Firebase Cloud Messaging provides cross-platform message delivery for Android, iOS, web and Flutter clients. Apple uses APNs for remote notification delivery.

Notifications still require user permission. Android 13 and later require the POST_NOTIFICATIONS runtime permission for most notifications, while iOS requires notification authorisation before alerts, sounds or badges are shown.

Notification design rules

Ask permission after explaining the value

Let users control marketing separately

Do not include sensitive home or pet-health details in lock-screen text

Deep-link to the relevant booking

Store every important event in the app inbox

Use WhatsApp/SMS or support calls for critical operational fallback

Never assume push delivery alone proves the user was informed

10. AI-assisted sitter matching

Correct use of AI

AI should recommend and rank candidates.

It should not autonomously assign every pet without operational constraints.

Booking request

↓

Hard eligibility filters

↓

Capacity and geography filters

↓

Risk and skill filters

↓

AI/rules-based ranking

↓

Recommended sitters

↓

Automatic or human confirmation based on risk

Hard filters before AI scoring

A sitter must first satisfy:

Active verification

Correct service permission

Correct city/zone

Available time

Travel limit

Pet-size capability

Behaviour-risk eligibility

Society access

Capacity

No quality suspension

AI must never rank an ineligible sitter above an eligible one.

Suggested ranking factors

### Table 153

| Factor | Example weight |
| --- | --- |
| Pet compatibility | 20 |
| Previous relationship | 20 |
| Reliability | 15 |
| Distance/travel time | 15 |
| Availability fit | 10 |
| Customer preference | 10 |
| Rating and report quality | 5 |
| Backup availability | 5 |

High-risk cases requiring human approval

Aggressive pet history

Senior/medical care

Medication

First boarding

Previous incident

Multiple large pets

Special-access home

Emergency booking

Very young animals

Explainability

The operations team should see reasons such as:

Recommended because Riya has completed seven services with Bruno, has 98% punctuality, is 1.2 km away and has matching morning availability.

Avoid an unexplained “AI score: 92.”

11. Loyalty system

Do not begin with complex points and tiers.

Start with behaviour-linked benefits

Reward:

Completed repeat bookings

Subscription renewal

Referrals after completed service

On-time customer handover

Honest reviews

Society programme participation

Early loyalty model

Care Credits

- Earn after completed eligible service

- Apply to future service

- Non-transferable

- No cash value

- Expiry clearly disclosed

- Refund and reversal rules visible

Avoid

Points for app opens

Gamifying emergency or medical care

Unlimited liability

Confusing conversion rates

Hidden expiry

Rewards before service completion

Referral abuse

12. Wallet versus service credits

Use the word credits rather than “wallet” in the initial release.

Recommended design:

Non-transferable

No cash withdrawal

No peer-to-peer transfer

Used only for eligible platform bookings

Issued for subscription entitlement, refund, promotion or service recovery

Separate balances by credit type

Full transaction history

RBI distinguishes closed-system instruments used only to buy the issuer’s own goods or services from broader prepaid payment instruments. Because your marketplace may facilitate third-party service providers, a reloadable customer-funded balance can create additional regulatory questions. Obtain payments counsel before creating cash-like stored value, transferability or third-party merchant acceptance.

Recommended Phase 11 approach

Launch:

Subscription service credits

Promotional credits

Refund credits with customer choice where lawful

Loyalty credits

Defer:

User-funded cash wallet

Peer transfer

Cash-out

Cross-merchant usage

Stored balances unrelated to an active service obligation

13. Pet health records

The app may become the owner-controlled record layer for:

Vaccinations

Allergies

Medication instructions

Veterinary consultation documents

Lab reports

Medical conditions

Surgery history

Follow-up reminders

Access design

### Table 154

| Role | Access |
| --- | --- |
| Pet parent | Full record |
| Assigned sitter | Only care information necessary for current service |
| Vet partner | Records explicitly shared for the consultation |
| Support | No routine clinical access |
| Society/RWA | No access |
| Marketing | No access |
| Admin | Restricted and audited |

Safety rules

AI does not provide final diagnosis

Support does not interpret medical reports

Sitters see only operationally necessary instructions

Every document share is logged

Owners can revoke access

Records have retention and deletion policies

Urgent symptoms route to a vet, not an automated diagnosis

Location history, home schedules, provider records and pet-health documents require clear notices, access controls, security safeguards and grievance/deletion handling under the Indian data-protection framework. The DPDP Rules, 2025 were notified with phased commencement.

14. Account and privacy controls

Both apps should include:

Download personal data

Update profile information

Communication preferences

Location-permission status

Active devices

Logout from other devices

Delete account

Request data correction

Request support

Privacy policy

Retention explanation

Apple requires apps that support account creation to let users initiate account deletion inside the app. Google Play requires a readily discoverable deletion option inside the app and a corresponding external web deletion path.

The deletion system must distinguish:

Deleting the app

Deactivating the account

Cancelling a subscription

Deleting the account and eligible associated data

These are different actions.

15. Do not build a basic wrapper

The proposed “fast app build in 2–3 months” is acceptable only for:

Internal testing

Sitter beta

Limited native proof of concept

It is not a strong public app strategy if the result is just the website inside a mobile shell.

Apple’s App Review Guidelines state that apps should provide features, content and UI beyond a repackaged website.

A public mobile app should add real native value

At minimum:

Native push

Offline workflows

Native camera/media capture

Secure device sessions

Native location during active services

Deep linking

Biometric unlock

Smooth repeated booking

Background upload/retry

App-specific accessibility and navigation

16. Recommended technology strategy

One backend, multiple clients

Core API platform

├── Admin web application

├── Customer web/PWA

├── Customer mobile app

└── Sitter mobile app

Do not create separate business logic in every client.

Keep server-side:

Pricing

Eligibility

Matching rules

Capacity

Booking status transitions

Credit ledger

Payment verification

Refund rules

Incident state

Access control

Subscription lifecycle

The apps should not be trusted to decide:

Whether a payment succeeded

Whether a sitter is eligible

How many credits remain

Whether a booking is completed

Whether a refund is owed

Whether an incident can be closed

17. Cross-platform versus native

Cross-platform is appropriate when

The team is strong in TypeScript/Dart

Most features share common UI and logic

Faster simultaneous iOS/Android delivery matters

Native bridges for GPS, camera, push and background jobs are tested carefully

Fully native may be justified when

Background location is highly complex

Device-level performance is critical

The team already has strong iOS and Android specialists

Platform-specific workflows differ significantly

Reliability outweighs initial development speed

Practical recommendation

Use a well-supported cross-platform framework for both apps, but treat these as native-critical modules:

Location

Background service/session

Push

Camera and uploads

Secure storage

Biometrics

Offline database

Deep links

Do not choose the framework solely from developer familiarity. Run a technical proof of concept for:

Screen locked during a 45-minute walk

Location retained with expected battery use

Network lost and restored

Photo/report upload retry

Push received across device states

App killed and reopened

Permission denied and later enabled

### Table 158

22. Final decisions

### Table 159

| Proposal | Decision |
| --- | --- |
| Build full customer app | Approve after repeat-use gate |
| Build sitter app first | Approve—recommended |
| Advanced GPS tracking | Approve only during active services |
| Push notifications | Approve with permission and fallback design |
| AI-assisted matching | Approve as recommendation system first |
| Loyalty system | Approve after core app adoption |
| Wallet | Defer cash-like wallet; use service credits |
| Advanced subscription management | Approve |
| Pet health records | Approve as owner-controlled record layer |
| App phase at 2–5 cities | Approve when one new city is validated |
| Require 500 subscribers before any app work | Modify—customer app gate, not sitter app gate |
| 4–6 month duration | Approve for production MVPs |
| 2–3 month wrapper | Not recommended for public launch |
| PWA replacement | Reject; retain PWA as a supported channel |

Final Phase 11 recommendation

Build:

A sitter-first mobile operating system that captures reliable check-ins, active-service location, offline reports, incidents and payouts—followed by a customer app focused on booking, live visibility, subscriptions, credits and pet-care records.

Do not:

Build two giant apps simultaneously

Track sitters continuously

Request every permission during onboarding

Put business rules only in the apps

Let AI override eligibility and safety controls

Build a cash-like wallet without regulatory analysis

Hide account deletion

abandon the website/PWA

launch loyalty before core fulfilment works

treat app downloads as the primary success metric

Phase 11 win condition

Mobile products reduce operational effort and service failures while increasing customer retention, without weakening privacy, battery performance, payment accuracy or sitter adoption.

Phase 11 status: GO—start with the sitter app, validate it operationally, then launch the customer app to repeat customers and subscribers.

Phase 11 Product Priority and Mobile Architecture 📱🐾

Executive decision

Approve the sitter-first strategy and React Native with Expo recommendation.

However, the proposed priority list needs two changes:

Advanced GPS is not a separate fourth-stage product. Session-based tracking is part of the sitter-app foundation.

Credits, loyalty and wallet must be separated. Service credits can launch early; loyalty comes later; a cash-like wallet should be deferred.

Corrected build order

0. Shared mobile platform foundation

↓

1. Sitter app + active-service GPS

↓

2. Advanced admin command centre

↓

3. Customer mobile app

↓

4. Rules-based matching → AI-assisted matching

↓

5. Service credits → loyalty

↓

6. Pet health timeline

↓

7. Society and corporate mobile features

The sitter app and command centre should be developed together because they are two sides of the same operating workflow:

Sitter app creates live operational events

↓

Admin command centre monitors and resolves them

1. Phase 11 main principle

Wrong approach

Build attractive customer app

↓

Add animations and loyalty points

↓

Customers book more

↓

Sitters still miss bookings and reports

↓

Operational failures increase

A polished customer application amplifies demand. It does not repair weak field execution.

Correct approach

Strengthen sitter execution

↓

Strengthen admin monitoring

↓

Reduce fulfilment failures

↓

Improve customer visibility

↓

Increase repeat usage

↓

Add intelligence and loyalty

For this marketplace, the sitter application is not a secondary partner portal.

The sitter app is the field operating system for every physical service.

It controls the transition between a digital booking and a real service:

Booking accepted

↓

Sitter arrives

↓

Pet handover confirmed

↓

Service begins

↓

Service is documented

↓

Incident reported if necessary

↓

Service completed

↓

Customer report delivered

↓

Provider payout released

2. Priority 0: Shared mobile foundation

Before building app screens, strengthen the common platform.

Required shared capabilities

Versioned APIs

Mobile authentication

Device registration

Push-token management

Booking event model

Offline synchronisation protocol

Media-upload pipeline

Location-session APIs

Feature flags

Analytics taxonomy

Error monitoring

App-version enforcement

Remote configuration

Role-based access

Audit logs

Server-authoritative model

The backend—not either mobile app—must determine:

Whether a booking is valid

Whether a sitter is eligible

Whether capacity exists

Which price applies

Whether a payment succeeded

How many credits remain

Whether a service can be completed

Whether a refund is due

Whether an incident can be closed

The applications should collect actions and display state. They should not become independent sources of business truth.

3. Priority 1: Sitter mobile application

Primary objective

Improve service completion, punctuality, evidence quality, safety and provider communication.

P0 sitter modules

↓

↓

↓

↓

↓

Cannot start too far from the service location without reason

Cannot complete before minimum duration without explanation

Duplicate completion blocked

Server reconciles conflicting events

GPS limitations shown honestly

↓

↓

↓

↓

The sitter should not have to complete a long form before receiving urgent instructions.

Earnings and payouts

Show:

Financial transparency improves provider trust and reduces support contacts.

Availability and leave

Sitters should manage:

Subscription schedules should warn operations before a primary sitter’s leave creates a coverage gap.

4. Advanced GPS belongs inside the sitter-app foundation

Correct tracking model

Track only during a user-initiated service session:

Sitter taps “Start Walk”

↓

Tracking begins

↓

App enters background if necessary

↓

Location points are stored locally

↓

Points sync to the server

↓

Sitter taps “Complete”

↓

Tracking stops

Expo supports background tasks and background-location updates through its TaskManager and Location APIs, but operating-system constraints still apply. Expo’s documentation notes, for example, that background location stops if the user terminates the app and resumes only after reopening it.

Never use continuous workforce surveillance

Do not track:

Outside an accepted booking

Throughout the sitter’s day

During leave

After service completion

For advertising

Without clear disclosure

GPS session schema

location_sessions

- id

- booking_id

- sitter_id

- device_id

- started_at

- stopped_at

- status

- tracking_reason

- consent_version

- point_count

- distance_meters

- quality_score

location_points

- id

- session_id

- recorded_at

- latitude

- longitude

- accuracy_meters

- speed

- heading

- source

- sync_status

GPS quality indicators

The customer should not see an apparently precise route when the location signal is poor.

Possible statuses:

Accurate

Approximate

Temporarily unavailable

Device offline

Upload pending

Route incomplete

GPS acceptance criteria

Tracking starts only after explicit sitter action

Tracking stops automatically after completion

Offline points sync later

Duplicate points are handled

Poor-accuracy points are filtered or marked

Battery impact is measured on real devices

No location remains active after the booking

Customer sees only the relevant booking route

7. Priority 4: Matching engine before “AI matching”

Do not begin by training a complex model.

Build matching in three stages.

Stage 1: Deterministic eligibility

Remove every sitter who fails a hard requirement:

Wrong city or zone

Outside travel limit

Unavailable

No required service permission

Incompatible pet-risk level

Suspended

No society access

Capacity exhausted

Required documents expired

Stage 2: Rules-based ranking

Rank remaining sitters using explainable factors:

### Table 160

| Factor | Suggested weight |
| --- | --- |
| Previous relationship with pet | 20 |
| Pet/skill compatibility | 20 |
| Travel time | 15 |
| Reliability | 15 |
| Schedule fit | 10 |
| Customer preference | 10 |
| Rating/report quality | 5 |
| Backup availability | 5 |

Stage 3: AI-assisted ranking

Once sufficient historical data exists, a model can estimate:

Acceptance probability

On-time probability

Completion probability

Customer satisfaction

Repeat-sitter likelihood

Cancellation risk

The model should generate recommendations—not bypass safety filters.

Explainable output

Operations should see:

Recommended because Riya has completed eight services with Bruno, has 98% punctuality, is 1.4 km away and has matching weekday availability.

Not:

AI score: 94.

Human approval required

Behaviourally high-risk pets

Medication

Senior or medical care

First boarding

Previous incident

Emergency request

Multiple large pets

Unusual property access

8. Priority 5: Credits, loyalty and wallet

These are three different products.

A. Service credits — early

Used for:

Walking-plan entitlements

Starter packs

Service recovery

Eligible refunds

Promotions

Required ledger:

GRANT

RESERVE

CONSUME

RELEASE

RESTORE

EXPIRE

ROLLOVER

Service credits should already exist before the customer app because subscription fulfilment depends on them.

B. Loyalty — later

Reward meaningful behaviour:

Completed repeat service

Subscription renewal

Successful referral

Society participation

Long-term retention

Do not reward app opens or encourage unnecessary pet services.

C. Cash-like wallet — defer

Do not initially build a customer-funded, transferable stored-value wallet.

Use:

Non-transferable service credits

No cash withdrawal

No peer-to-peer transfer

Clear expiry

Full transaction history

Usage only for eligible platform services

This reduces product complexity and financial-regulatory risk.

9. Priority 6: Pet health timeline

Objective

Create an owner-controlled continuity record, not an automated diagnostic product.

Timeline events

Vaccination

Deworming

Allergy

Medication instruction

Veterinary consultation

Test or report

Surgery

Grooming concern

Injury or incident

Follow-up reminder

Access controls

### Table 161

| User | Access |
| --- | --- |
| Pet parent | Full record |
| Assigned sitter | Current-service care instructions only |
| Vet partner | Explicitly shared records |
| Customer support | Minimal operational access |
| Society/RWA | No access |
| Marketing | No access |

AI boundaries

AI may:

Extract a vaccination date from a document

Organise records

Generate reminders

Summarise owner-provided notes

Suggest contacting a veterinarian

AI must not:

Diagnose

Prescribe

Interpret an emergency as safe

Replace a registered professional

Keep them web-based during Phase 11

A separate society or corporate mobile app is unlikely to justify the maintenance cost initially.

Society web dashboard

Complaint status

Monthly report

Eligible employees

Budget remaining

Service categories

Renewal report

Do not expose individual employee or resident care information to organisations.

Build native B2B features only when

Users return frequently

Approvals occur regularly

Web experience is demonstrably limiting adoption

A dedicated B2B product owner exists

Phase 11

### Table 162

| Product | Platform | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |

Later phase
