12. React Native with Expo vs Flutter

Option A: React Native with Expo

Advantages for your context

TypeScript and React knowledge transfers directly

Easier alignment with a Next.js team

Shared domain types and API clients

Faster hiring from JavaScript/React talent pools

Expo provides an integrated framework and native-device APIs

Native modules can still be added where required

React Native’s own setup documentation recommends using a framework for new applications, and Expo provides a React Native framework with routing, build tooling and native-device modules.

Risks

Background-location behaviour still varies by operating system

Some native libraries require development builds or custom configuration

Poorly selected third-party packages can cause upgrade friction

JavaScript knowledge does not remove the need to understand iOS and Android lifecycle behaviour

Expo’s current tooling supports React Native’s newer architecture, but third-party-library compatibility should still be checked before adoption.

Option B: Flutter

Advantages

Strong UI consistency

Highly controlled rendering

Single Dart codebase

Mature architecture guidance

Excellent for custom interfaces and motion-heavy applications

Flutter officially positions itself as a framework for natively compiled multiplatform applications from one codebase and publishes detailed scalable-app architecture guidance.

Costs for your context

Requires Dart expertise

Less direct code and knowledge reuse from Next.js/React

Separate frontend hiring profile

Shared TypeScript domain packages cannot be consumed directly

Web and mobile teams may diverge more quickly

Final framework decision

Use React Native with Expo when:

Your existing stack is React/Next.js

Your team is strongest in TypeScript

Speed and shared engineering practices matter

You are prepared to test native-critical modules thoroughly

Use Flutter when:

You have a dedicated Flutter team

Visual consistency is the dominant requirement

You accept a separate language and mobile architecture

You are not optimising for React/TypeScript reuse

For your current full-stack profile, React Native with Expo is the stronger default.

13. Corrected React Native stack

Mobile framework

React Native

Expo with development builds

Expo Router

TypeScript

Expo Router provides file-based routing and native, deep-linkable navigation across Android, iOS and web.

Do not rely only on Expo Go for production development. Features such as production push notifications require development builds, and native/background behaviour must be tested in real app binaries.

Backend

Existing Node.js/Next.js API layer

PostgreSQL

Existing payment and subscription services

Background workers/queues

Object storage

WebSocket or event-stream layer where justified

Authentication

Use the same identity system, but model mobile sessions separately:

user

device

mobile_session

push_token

refresh_token

last_seen_at

app_version

Store small secrets or tokens in secure device storage. Expo SecureStore provides encrypted local key-value storage; it should not be used as a general database for large payloads.

Navigation

Expo Router

Role-separated route groups

Deep links from notifications

Auth-protected layouts

Offline database

Expo SQLite

Local outbox queue

Conflict-resolution layer

Expo SQLite persists its database across app restarts and is suitable as the local persistence layer for offline-first workflows.

Use it for:

Assigned booking cache

Pending service events

Location points

Unsynced reports

Media-upload queue

Do not store highly sensitive records indefinitely on the device.

Push notifications

Recommended starting choice:

expo-notifications

Expo Push Service initially

Direct FCM/APNs later if more control is required

Expo’s notifications API is push-service agnostic, so the client can later use native device tokens for direct FCM/APNs delivery.

Location and background tasks

expo-location

expo-task-manager

Development-build testing

Active-service sessions only

Maps

Evaluate:

Google Maps

Mappls

Possibly separate maps for display and address intelligence

Mappls currently offers React Native mapping and live-location SDK documentation, making it a credible India-focused candidate. Selection should be based on address accuracy, route quality, geocoding, pricing, support and compatibility with your chosen Expo development-build workflow.

Run a proof of concept using real launch areas:

Whitefield

Baner

Powai

Gurugram sectors

Bopal

Vesu

Do not select a map provider from screenshots alone.

Media

Use:

Native camera/image picker

Local compression

Temporary file queue

Signed uploads

Object storage such as S3-compatible storage or Cloudinary

Expo provides camera, image-picker and filesystem modules suitable for capturing and temporarily storing media before upload.

Analytics

Recommended:

PostHog for product analytics, funnels and feature flags

Firebase Analytics as an alternative or supplementary acquisition layer

Strict event redaction

PostHog’s React Native SDK supports Expo projects, custom events and feature flags.

Do not send:

Customer addresses

Access codes

Detailed health notes

Raw GPS routes

Payment credentials

Incident narratives

to general analytics tools.

Error and performance monitoring

Sentry

Release tracking

Source maps

Navigation performance

User/session correlation with privacy safeguards

Sentry provides dedicated React Native and Expo integrations, including Expo Router instrumentation.

Final Phase 11 product doctrine

First digitise the work that creates trust. Then digitise the convenience that retains customers. Only after both work should the platform add intelligence, loyalty and deeper records.

Official Phase 11 focus

Sitter application

+

Active-service tracking

+

Admin command centre

+

Customer application

Everything else should follow measured adoption and operational benefit.

Phase 11 Product Priority: GO—with React Native and Expo development builds, sitter-first execution, admin visibility in parallel, and advanced features unlocked only after the operational core is stable.

Phase 11 Products 1–2: Mobile Apps and Advanced GPS System 📱🐾

Executive decision

Both product definitions are strong, with four important corrections:

GPS tracking belongs inside the sitter-app P0, not as a later standalone feature.

The sitter must not see more customer data than the current booking requires.

The emergency button must use multiple escalation channels—not push or WhatsApp alone.

Route and timing alerts should open operational reviews, not automatically accuse a sitter of misconduct.

The complete system should operate as:

Sitter app

↓ emits verified service events

Operations command centre

↓ monitors exceptions

Customer app

↓ displays trusted service state

Central backend

↓ controls bookings, payments, credits and incidents

Product 1: Sitter Mobile App

1. Product goal

The sitter app should make field delivery:

More punctual

More documentable

Easier to monitor

Safer during incidents

Less dependent on WhatsApp

More transparent for sitters

Easier to reconcile financially

The sitter application is not merely a provider profile or job board.

It is the field-operations application through which a scheduled digital booking becomes a verified physical service.

6. Booking request screen

Before acceptance, show enough information to make an informed decision without exposing unnecessary customer data.

Before acceptance

Show:

Approximate locality

Service type

Date and time

Duration

Pet species, size and broad risk category

Required skills

Estimated travel time

Expected payout

Society-entry requirement

Number of pets

Do not expose before acceptance:

Exact flat number

Personal access codes

Full customer phone number

Detailed health files

Unrelated customer notes

After confirmed assignment

Show:

Exact service address

Relevant owner instructions

Handover process

Required pet-care notes

Emergency contact method

Society gate instructions

Operations support

Navigation

This follows data-minimisation principles: location, home access and pet records should be available only to people who need them for the current service. India’s DPDP framework requires digital personal data to be processed for lawful purposes with appropriate protections, and the notified 2025 Rules have phased commencement.

7. Booking accept/reject logic

Acceptance checks

Before confirming acceptance, the backend should verify:

Sitter remains active

Documents remain valid

No time conflict exists

Travel time is feasible

Capacity remains available

Pet risk is compatible

Sitter has correct service permission

Another sitter has not already accepted

Structured rejection reasons

Schedule conflict

Too far away

Pet size/behaviour mismatch

Society access issue

Payout mismatch

Personal emergency

Temporarily unavailable

Other

These reasons should feed:

Pricing analysis

Capacity planning

Matching improvements

Recruitment gaps

Sitter-support decisions

A rejection should not automatically damage the sitter’s reliability score when it occurs promptly and for a valid reason.

Identity and task

10. Arrival and start-service flow

Recommended start flow

Sitter approaches location

↓

App checks permission and GPS quality

↓

Sitter taps “I’ve Arrived”

↓

System compares position with service geofence

↓

Handover confirmed

↓

Sitter taps “Start Service”

↓

Active tracking and timer begin

Start-service preconditions

Booking is assigned to this sitter

Current time is within allowed range

Required location permission exists

Device has acceptable location signal

Handover is confirmed

No conflicting active service exists

Safety instructions acknowledged

Starting outside the geofence

Do not automatically block every legitimate case.

Allow an exception with a reason:

Customer requested another meeting point

Society entrance differs from residence

GPS is inaccurate

Admin authorised start

Emergency relocation

The exception should be logged and surfaced for review.

Android geofencing uses a latitude, longitude and radius to identify entry or exit from an area, but geofence events and background location are subject to platform timing and accuracy constraints.

11. GPS session architecture

Session-based tracking only

Booking assigned

↓

No continuous location collection

↓

Sitter starts eligible service

↓

Tracking session activated

↓

Location points collected during service

↓

Service ends

↓

Tracking stopped automatically

Do not track sitters continuously throughout the working day.

Apple permits background services only for their intended purposes and requires location use to be directly relevant to app functionality. Android requires appropriate location permissions, and apps targeting Android 14 or later must declare the correct foreground-service type for a location foreground service.

Important Expo limitation

Expo supports background location through its Location and TaskManager APIs, but background updates remain subject to platform constraints. Expo states that background location stops if the user terminates the application and resumes after the app is restarted.

Therefore, your product must detect and report:

App terminated

Background permission removed

Location disabled

Tracking task stopped

Network unavailable

Points pending upload

Do not falsely label these situations as a completed, continuously tracked route.

12. Adaptive tracking strategy

Do not hard-code one aggressive interval for every device and service.

Use adaptive profiles:

Walking profile

Higher frequency while moving

Lower frequency while stationary

Distance-based updates

Batch upload during weak connectivity

Immediate upload for safety events

Sitting profile

Continuous route tracking is usually unnecessary.

Use:

Arrival verification

Periodic check-ins

Service start/end

Media and report evidence

Boarding profile

Do not continuously track the host.

Use:

Check-in/check-out

Scheduled care updates

Media

Incident reporting

Grooming profile

Use:

Arrival

Start

Before media

End

After media

Report

Android recommends limiting location work to what the feature needs because background location affects battery usage; Google’s location stack includes battery-optimised fused-location and geofencing capabilities.

14. Offline tracking and report sync

Local outbox

The sitter app should locally queue:

Start-service event

Location points

Media files

Observations

End-service event

Report submission

Incident evidence

Sync states

LOCAL_ONLY

QUEUED

UPLOADING

SYNCED

FAILED_RETRYABLE

FAILED_PERMANENT

CONFLICT_REVIEW

Server rules

Events require unique idempotency keys

Duplicate completion must be ignored

Device timestamps must be preserved

Server-received timestamps must also be stored

Conflicts should not silently overwrite records

Media upload failure must not erase the written report

Earnings should remain pending until completion is validated

15. Photo and video upload

Capture rules

Compress media before upload

Strip unnecessary metadata where appropriate

Use signed upload URLs

Store upload progress

Retry failed uploads

Show pending versus uploaded status

Obtain customer consent for routine media

Avoid showing unrelated people or private home areas

Suggested limits

A small number of meaningful photographs

Short video clips only when useful

No forced high-resolution upload

Media retained according to a documented policy

Location routes, customer homes and pet-related health or behaviour notes should not be sent to general-purpose analytics or error logs.

16. End-service validation

When the sitter taps End Service, evaluate:

Minimum expected duration

GPS/session health

Distance where relevant

Required observations

Required media

Open incident state

Customer handover confirmation

Early-end flow

Early end detected

↓

Sitter selects reason

↓

Operations notified

↓

Service remains “Pending Review”

↓

Customer receives neutral update

↓

Admin validates completion and credit/payout

Do not immediately accuse the sitter or automatically release full payout.

Valid reasons may include:

Pet became unwell

Weather emergency

Customer requested early return

Unsafe animal encountered

Service changed by operations

GPS malfunction

Incident opened

On the active-service screen

On the incident screen

Sitter injured or unsafe

Medical concern

Customer unavailable

Property/security issue

Other urgent issue

Sitter taps Emergency Help

↓

Large category choices appear

Multi-channel escalation

The system should:

Create an incident through the backend

Send a critical operations alert

Trigger an in-app alarm/queue

Offer direct phone calling

Send push notifications

Use SMS/WhatsApp fallback where configured

Escalate automatically if not acknowledged

FCM is a cross-platform push service, but push should not be your only emergency mechanism; operational state must be persisted on the backend and paired with direct calling/escalation.

Nearest-vet display

Clinic name

Distance

Phone

Available capabilities

Navigation

Do not promise:

Guaranteed admission

Guaranteed open status

Guaranteed response

Medical suitability without verification

Advanced GPS Tracking

31. Correct GPS architecture

Sitter initiates service

↓

Permission and device-state check

↓

Server creates location session

↓

Foreground/background location begins

↓

Points collected adaptively

↓

Points stored locally

↓

Near-real-time batches sent to backend

↓

Customer and admin receive session state

↓

Alerts evaluate session events

↓

Service completion requested

↓

Backend validates and closes session

↓

Route summary generated

On Android, active background location normally requires the correct location foreground-service configuration and visible foreground-service behaviour; Android 13 and later also introduced runtime notification permission.

On iOS, the app must request appropriate location authorisation and configure background location for the actual use case.

32. Permission UX

Do not request background location immediately after login.

Recommended sequence

Sitter accepts first tracked walk

↓

App explains why location is needed

↓

Foreground permission requested

↓

Background-service explanation shown

↓

Background access requested/configured

↓

Test confirms tracking readiness

For notifications:

User sees value explanation

↓

System permission requested

Android 13+ uses the POST_NOTIFICATIONS runtime permission, while Apple requires explicit notification authorisation through UNUserNotificationCenter.

33. GPS alert engine

Alerts should use:

Static rules

Contextual thresholds

Historical baselines

GPS quality

Service type

Pet requirements

Human review

Do not trigger based on a single noisy GPS point.

Alert state model

DETECTED

OBSERVING

CONFIRMED

NOTIFIED

ACKNOWLEDGED

RESOLVED

FALSE_POSITIVE

ESCALATED

Trigger inputs

35. Wrong-location alert

Recommended logic

Open a review when:

Start occurs outside the configured radius

GPS accuracy is acceptable

No authorised meeting-point exception exists

Several points confirm the location

Avoid false positives from

Apartment entrance versus tower

Indoor GPS drift

Dense urban buildings

Customer-requested park meetup

Inaccurate device location

The app should first ask the sitter to confirm:

You appear to be outside the expected start area. Are you meeting the customer at another location?

36. Route-anomaly alert

“Unusual route” requires careful definition.

Possible signals

Route goes far outside expected service zone

Sudden impossible movement

Repeated circular GPS noise

Service leaves approved boundaries

Route deviates materially from historical pattern

Pet-risk rule prohibits certain area

Response

A route anomaly should:

Be labelled as a review signal

Prompt sitter confirmation

Show GPS-quality context

Notify operations only after persistence

Avoid alarming the customer prematurely

Never send:

Your sitter is taking your dog somewhere unsafe.

based only on an automated route deviation.

37. Long-pause alert

Context matters

No movement may be acceptable when:

Pet rests

Sitter gives water

Traffic crossing is delayed

Customer requested a stationary outdoor break

GPS signal is weak

Service is pet sitting rather than walking

Recommended rule

Use:

No significant movement

For a sustained period

During a walking service

With acceptable GPS accuracy

Without a pause reason

First prompt the sitter:

You have been stationary for several minutes. Is everything okay?

Escalate only when the sitter fails to respond or another risk signal exists.

38. Early-end alert

Trigger

Completed before minimum acceptable duration

No approved exception

GPS/session is valid

No incident explains the end

Workflow

Early completion requested

↓

Reason required

↓

Booking marked Pending Review

↓

Operations evaluates

↓

Credit and payout resolved

Do not automatically deduct the sitter’s payout or customer credit.

39. Tracking-lost alert

Possible causes

Permission revoked

GPS disabled

App force-closed

Device battery exhausted

Operating-system restriction

Network unavailable

Expo background task stopped

App crash

Required response

Attempt local tracking continuity.

Notify sitter in-app if possible.

Record session degradation.

Alert admin after threshold.

Inform customer neutrally if live tracking is materially interrupted.

Preserve service evidence from timer, media and reports.

Customer copy:

Live location is temporarily unavailable. The service remains in progress, and operations has been notified.

Not:

Sitter stopped tracking.

40. Emergency alert

Emergency Help should override ordinary alert handling.

Immediate actions

Create critical incident

Attach last known location

Lock the booking from normal completion

Notify city operations

Notify central incident team

Offer direct call

Display safety instructions

Start acknowledgement timer

Escalate to backup contacts

If the device is offline, the app should:

Store the incident locally

Present direct call options

Attempt SMS/phone fallback

Sync evidence when connectivity returns

41. Admin live monitor

The operations command centre should show:

### Table 173

| Booking | Pet | Sitter | Status | GPS | Alert | Action |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| BKG-21 | Bruno | Riya | In progress | Healthy | None | View |
| BKG-22 | Max | Aman | Late | Available | Late start | Contact |
| BKG-23 | Simba | Neha | In progress | Lost | Tracking | Review |

Map privacy

Admin access should be:

Role-controlled

Limited to active operational sessions

Logged

Disabled for marketing/society roles

Hidden after the operational retention period

Product doctrine

The sitter app should prove what happened, support the person delivering the service and surface exceptions early. The customer app should translate that operational evidence into confidence, visibility and convenient repeat care.

Phase 11 Product 1–2 win condition

At least 95% of eligible services are digitally acknowledged, started, monitored and reported, while customers gain live visibility without creating continuous worker surveillance, excessive battery use or unsafe dependence on automated alerts.

| Service credits | Approve |
| Referral and promotional credits | Approve with fraud and reversal controls |

Product 3: AI-Assisted Sitter Matching

1. Product objective

The matching system should reduce:

Manual search time

Poor distance assignments

Skill mismatches

Schedule conflicts

Repeated sitter changes

Last-minute cancellations

Subscription fulfilment risk

It should not remove human accountability.

NIST’s AI-risk guidance recommends defining where human oversight is necessary, documenting system limitations, providing understandable outputs and continuously monitoring performance and harmful outcomes. That is especially relevant here because matching affects both pet safety and sitter earning opportunities.

2. Why full auto-assignment is premature

A pet-care assignment contains context that may not be completely represented in structured data:

The dog recently became reactive

The customer distrusts a particular replacement

A society gate has changed its rules

A sitter has accepted too many consecutive walks

A medical or behavioural note is ambiguous

A location appears close geographically but is difficult to reach

The sitter is technically available but is the only backup for another subscription

An automated system may produce a mathematically plausible match that is operationally unsafe.

Recommended responsibility

### Table 179

| Actor | Responsibility |
| --- | --- |
| System | Filters ineligible sitters |
| Ranking engine | Orders eligible candidates |
| Admin | Reviews context and confirms |
| Sitter | Accepts the assignment |
| Customer | Confirms where appropriate |
| Quality team | Audits results and failures |

3. Three-stage matching architecture

Stage 1: Hard eligibility filters

Before any AI scoring, eliminate sitters who do not satisfy mandatory conditions.

Hard filters

Sitter account is active

Required documents are current

Correct service permission exists

Correct city and service zone

Available at requested time

Travel time is feasible

Daily capacity is available

Pet-risk level is permitted

Sitter has required skills

Society access is valid

No overlapping booking

Sitter is not paused or under investigation

Required backup coverage remains available

All sitters

↓

Hard safety and operational rules

↓

Eligible candidate pool

The AI should never “outscore” a failed safety rule.

Stage 2: Candidate ranking

Rank only the eligible pool.

Recommended factors

### Table 180

| Factor | Suggested importance |
| --- | --- |
| Previous relationship with pet | Very high |
| Pet-risk and skill compatibility | Very high |
| Availability fit | Very high |
| Travel time | High |
| Reliability | High |
| Service-specific experience | High |
| Customer preference | Medium-high |
| Society access familiarity | Medium-high |
| Rating and report quality | Medium |
| Price/payout compatibility | Medium |
| Backup implications | Medium |

Example scoring model

Weights should vary by service.

For example:

Dog walking: distance, punctuality and previous pet relationship matter strongly.

Boarding: environment, trial history and household compatibility matter more.

Senior care: relevant skill and medical-instruction reliability dominate.

Grooming: package skill, pet size and equipment matter more than distance alone.

4. Do not call an arbitrary score a probability

Avoid:

Riya — 92% probability of being the right sitter.

unless your model has been calibrated against real outcomes and 92% has a statistically validated meaning.

Use:

Riya — Match score 92/100

or:

Excellent operational match

Suggested bands

### Table 181

| Score | Label |
| --- | --- |
| 90–100 | Excellent match |
| 80–89 | Strong match |
| 70–79 | Acceptable match |
| 60–69 | Manual review required |
| Below 60 | Do not recommend automatically |

5. Admin recommendation interface

Recommended output

Recommended sitters

1. Riya — 92/100 · Excellent match

1.2 km from the customer

Previously completed seven walks with Bruno

Qualified for large-dog handling

Available at 7:00 AM

98% on-time rate

Approved for this society

Backup remains available

2. Aman — 86/100 · Strong match

1.8 km away

No previous history with Bruno

Experienced with Labradors

96% completion rate

Available at requested time

Would require a first-service introduction

3. Neha — 82/100 · Strong match

2.4 km away

Previously handled Bruno once

Strong customer rating

Available within a 30-minute flexible window

Admin actions

Assign

View complete profile

Compare candidates

Request sitter acceptance

Select another candidate

Record override reason

Escalate for specialist review

6. Admin override system

Whenever the admin ignores the top recommendation, request a structured reason:

Customer requested another sitter

Existing pet relationship

Risk information not represented

Society access issue

Schedule/travel concern

Backup-capacity concern

Sitter workload concern

Quality concern

Customer gender preference where legitimately applicable

Other

This information becomes valuable training data.

Do not automatically treat the administrator’s choice as “correct.” Compare it with the actual outcome.

7. Matching workflow

↓

↓

↓

↓

Candidates ranked

↓

↓

Admin selects sitter

↓

Sitter accepts or rejects

↓

Customer notified

↓

Service completed

↓

Outcome recorded

↓

Ranking performance evaluated

Outcome signals

Sitter accepted

Sitter arrived on time

Service completed

Report submitted

Customer rating

Customer requested same sitter again

Complaint occurred

Sitter cancelled

Backup was required

Incident occurred

10. Matching fairness and worker controls

Matching can affect how many earning opportunities each sitter receives. Therefore, avoid creating a system that permanently sends all work to a small number of already successful providers.

Track:

Recommendations per sitter

Assignments per sitter

Acceptance rate

Earnings distribution

New-sitter exposure

Geographic distribution

Admin overrides

False exclusions

Rating bias

Customer-preference effects

NIST recommends defining human roles, measuring errors and negative effects, monitoring performance over time and maintaining processes to correct systems that exceed acceptable risk levels.

New sitter cold-start policy

A new sitter cannot have:

Past relationship history

Many ratings

Large completed-service volume

Create a controlled trial mechanism:

Low-risk first assignments

Supervised or monitored first services

Explicit “new approved sitter” status

No automatic penalty for limited history

Progressive capacity increase

| Metric | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| Admin recommendation acceptance | Recommendation usefulness |

| Override rate | Model/rule limitations |
| Incident rate | Safety |

| Matching-related support tickets | Operational clarity |

Initial targets

Top-three recommendation contains final selected sitter: 80%+

Admin accepts top recommendation: 50–70% initially

Eligible-candidate false exclusions: near zero

Human review: 100% initially

High-risk booking auto-assignment: zero

Product 4: Loyalty, Credits and Referral System

12. Separate the five balance types

Do not combine every balance into one number called “wallet.”

### Table 183

| Balance | Purpose | Recommended status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Subscription service credits | Included walks/services | Launch |
| Referral credits | Earned from eligible referrals | Launch |
| Promotional credits | Marketing campaigns | Launch carefully |
| Society benefit credits | Resident/company benefits | Launch where applicable |
| Refund/service-recovery credits | Customer-selected or goodwill credit | Controlled |
| Customer-funded cash wallet | Stored monetary value | Defer |

13. Why the “cash wallet” should be deferred

RBI defines a closed-system instrument as one used to purchase goods or services from the issuing entity only; it cannot be used to settle third-party services and does not permit cash withdrawal. A marketplace using independent sitters, groomers or other providers should not automatically assume that a customer-funded wallet qualifies as an unregulated closed system.

Therefore, avoid initially building:

User-reloadable money balances

Peer-to-peer transfers

Cash withdrawal

Transferable balances

Payments outside your platform

Cross-merchant acceptance

Wallet-to-bank settlement

Before introducing those features, obtain specialist Indian payments advice regarding PPI and marketplace-payment treatment.

14. Recommended early credit system

Use non-transferable ledgered credits.

Credit categories

SUBSCRIPTION

REFERRAL

PROMOTIONAL

SOCIETY_BENEFIT

SERVICE_RECOVERY

REFUND_ELECTED

MANUAL_ADJUSTMENT

Credit states

AVAILABLE

RESERVED

CONSUMED

RELEASED

EXPIRED

REVERSED

FROZEN

Core rule

Never store only:

customer.wallet_balance = 450

Use an immutable ledger and calculate the balance from transactions.

16. Refund-credit rule

Do not automatically replace every monetary refund with platform credit.

Recommended options:

Refund to original payment method

Customer voluntarily selects faster platform credit

Goodwill/service-recovery credit added separately

Contractually valid non-cash benefit where clearly disclosed

The customer should see:

Original amount

Refund method

Credit amount

Expiry

Eligible services

Whether the decision can be changed

Indian dark-pattern guidance prohibits deceptive design, including subscription traps and hidden or unnecessarily complex cancellation practices. The same product principle should apply to credit expiry and refund choice: terms must be visible before acceptance and not designed to trap value.

17. Referral programme

Recommended offer

Give ₹150, Get ₹150

But define the ₹150 as an eligible platform credit—not withdrawable cash.

End-to-end flow

Existing customer receives referral code

↓

Customer shares code

↓

Friend signs up through code

↓

Friend completes first eligible paid service

↓

Refund/reversal window passes

↓

Friend discount is finalised

↓

Referrer receives ₹150 referral credit

Do not grant the reward merely because:

The friend created an account

The friend entered a code

A payment attempt occurred

A booking was cancelled

A fully refunded service was completed

The same person created another account

18. Referral eligibility rules

Recommended conditions:

New paying customer

First eligible completed service

Minimum booking value

No full refund

Unique verified phone/account

Referral code applied before checkout

One referrer per new customer

Referrer’s account remains active

Reward issued after the fraud/reversal window

Anti-abuse signals

Repeated devices

Repeated payment instruments

Repeated addresses

High cancellation patterns

Circular referrals

Unusual referral velocity

Multiple accounts with the same identity evidence

These signals should trigger a review rather than automatic punishment. Collect only the data required for fraud prevention and explain material account restrictions.

REFERRED_USER_REGISTERED

FIRST_BOOKING_CREATED

FIRST_SERVICE_COMPLETED

UNDER_REVIEW

QUALIFIED

REWARD_GRANTED

REVERSED

REJECTED

20. Promotional-credit rules

Every promotional offer should specify:

Value

Eligibility

Minimum spend

Applicable services

Applicable city

Applicable users

Expiry date

Whether it combines with other offers

Maximum usage

Refund treatment

Avoid vague copy such as:

₹500 added to your wallet!

Use:

₹500 promotional credit for eligible dog-walking bookings in Ahmedabad, valid until 31 August 2026.

21. Society credits

Society credits may come from:

Society management sponsorship

Resident launch campaign

Event participation

Corporate or RWA care benefit

Resident subscription

They need an identifiable funding source.

society_credit_grant

- programme_id

- amount

- eligible_services

The society dashboard should display aggregate utilisation, not individual resident pet-care activity.

22. Loyalty system

Start with one simple loyalty mechanism:

Complete eligible services and earn Care Credits.

Example

Earn one Care Point for every ₹100 of eligible platform spend

100 points = ₹100 promotional credit

Points unlock only after service completion

Refund reverses corresponding points

Points expire after a clearly disclosed period

No cash value

No transfer

Better early rewards

Instead of only discounts, loyalty can offer:

Priority booking

Early access to peak slots

Flexible rescheduling

Grooming benefit

Referral boost

Society-event benefit

Avoid excessive discounting that damages unit economics.

Product 5: Pet Health Timeline

24. Product objective

The health timeline should make the app useful between bookings while improving care continuity.

It should become:

A pet-parent-controlled record of care events, documents, observations and reminders.

It should not become:

An automated veterinary diagnostic system.

25. Timeline categories

### Table 185

| Category | Example |
| --- | --- |
| Vaccination | Rabies vaccination |
| Preventive care | Deworming or tick treatment |
| Vet consultation | Skin-allergy consultation |
| Medication | Allergy medicine schedule |
| Grooming | Full grooming completed |
| Weight | 28 kg |
| Activity | Monthly walking summary |
| Behaviour | Pulling on leash |
| Training | Recall improvement |
| Incident/observation | Vomiting observed |
| Document | Lab report or prescription |
| Reminder | Vaccination due next month |

Use factual language.

Prefer:

Vomiting observed twice during sitting service.

Avoid:

Gastroenteritis diagnosed.

unless a registered veterinarian supplied that diagnosis.

26. Timeline sources

Every timeline entry needs a visible source:

PET_PARENT

SITTER_REPORT

GROOMER_REPORT

VET_DOCUMENT

VET_PARTNER

SYSTEM_ACTIVITY_SUMMARY

ADMIN_CORRECTION

Source examples

Rabies vaccinationSource: Document uploaded by pet parent

Weight: 28 kgSource: Recorded during vet visit

Pulled strongly on leashSource: Sitter report

Vaccination due 12 SeptemberSource: System reminder generated from uploaded record

27. Health-record workflow

Pet parent uploads record

↓

Document stored securely

↓

Metadata entered or extracted

↓

Customer reviews extracted information

↓

Timeline event created

↓

Reminder scheduled

↓

Customer chooses whether to share with vet

If AI extracts details from a document:

Mark them as machine-extracted

Show the source document

Require owner confirmation

Allow correction

Preserve the original

Do not treat extracted text as verified medical advice

| Groomer | Relevant allergy/handling details |
| Customer support | Minimal operational information |
| Marketing | No access |
| Society/RWA | No access |
| General city admin | No routine clinical access |

The DPDP Act and notified Rules require lawful-purpose processing, clear notices, safeguards and mechanisms for exercising data rights. Pet records linked with an identifiable owner, address or account should therefore be governed as part of the customer’s personal-data environment.

31. Sharing records with a vet

Recommended flow:

Customer selects documents

↓

Customer sees recipient and purpose

↓

Time-limited access grant created

↓

Vet accesses selected records

↓

Access recorded in audit log

↓

Grant expires or customer revokes it

Do not give every partner vet access to every customer’s complete pet history.

32. Health reminders

The system can safely provide reminders such as:

Vaccination record expires soon

Customer-entered medicine reminder

Grooming interval reached

Follow-up date recorded in vet document

Weight has not been updated recently

Wording should distinguish reminders from medical recommendations.

Use:

Bruno’s uploaded vaccination record shows the next date as 12 September. Confirm the schedule with your veterinarian.

Avoid:

Bruno must receive this vaccine on 12 September.

33. Timeline-derived recommendations

Recommendations should use clear rules and transparent reasons.

Examples:

Grooming

Bruno’s last recorded grooming was 10 weeks ago. View available grooming services.

Training

Several walk reports mention leash pulling. Consider an introductory training consultation.

Vet follow-up

The uploaded consultation document includes a follow-up date of 25 August.

Senior care

Bella’s profile identifies her as a senior dog. Review senior-care support options.

These are service recommendations—not diagnosis.

34. Pet health timeline privacy controls

Customers should be able to:

Add an event

Correct an event

Delete an owner-created event

Download their records

Revoke sharing

View access history

Set reminder preferences

Request account/data deletion

Control which carers see specific instructions

Separate:

Essential current-service instructions

Historical records

Veterinary documents

Marketing recommendations

A sitter should not automatically receive a pet’s entire medical history because they are assigned one walk.

35. Pet health timeline KPIs

### Table 188

| Metric | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| Profiles with timeline events | Adoption |
| Vaccination documents uploaded | Utility |
| Reminders created | Engagement |
| Reminder completion | Usefulness |
| Records shared with vets | Coordination value |
| Owner corrections | Extraction quality |
| Access revocations | Privacy control |
| Grooming reminder conversion | Commercial value |
| Training-consult conversion | Cross-sell |
| Support/privacy complaints | Trust risk |

Avoid optimising solely for:

Number of health alerts

Number of veterinary bookings

Number of medical-related notifications

Too many reminders can create anxiety and destroy trust.

36. Recommended implementation order

Wave 1: Matching foundation

Hard eligibility filters

Rules-based top-three ranking

Explanation UI

Admin approval

Override logging

Outcome tracking

Wave 2: Credits and referrals

Immutable credit ledger

Subscription credits

Service-recovery credits

Referral programme

Promotional-credit rules

Fraud-review queue

Wave 3: Loyalty

Care Points

Non-cash benefits

Referral tiers

Contribution monitoring

Wave 4: Health timeline MVP

Manual timeline entries

Document uploads

Reminders

Owner-controlled sharing

Activity summaries

Wave 5: Advanced intelligence

Calibrated prediction models

Matching optimisation

Document extraction

Personalised service recommendations

Health-timeline summaries

Final approval matrix

### Table 189

| Proposal | Final decision |
| --- | --- |
| AI recommends top three | APPROVE |
| Admin selects final sitter | APPROVE—MANDATORY INITIALLY |
| Human review for every assignment | APPROVE during initial rollout |
| Use “92% match” | CHANGE TO 92/100 unless calibrated |
| Explain matching reasons | MANDATORY |
| Hard safety filters before AI | MANDATORY |
| High-risk auto-assignment | REJECT |
| Cash wallet | DEFER |
| Service credits | APPROVE |
| Referral credits | APPROVE after eligible completed service |
| Promotional credits with undisclosed expiry | REJECT |
| Wallet transfers | DEFER/REJECT EARLY |
| Customer cash withdrawal | REJECT EARLY |
| Force monetary refunds into credit | REJECT |
| Pet health timeline | APPROVE |
| Owner-controlled record sharing | MANDATORY |
| AI-generated diagnosis | REJECT |
| Sitter access to entire medical history | REJECT |
| Health-based service recommendations | APPROVE with transparent reasoning |

Phase 11 product doctrine

AI should improve the administrator’s decision—not hide the decision. Credits should reward repeat care—not become an unregulated cash substitute. Pet records should improve continuity—not become an automated medical authority.

Win condition

The three systems succeed when:

Admin assignment time decreases

Matching quality and repeat relationships improve

Sitter opportunities remain explainable and auditable

Credits increase profitable repeat usage

Referral acquisition remains fraud-controlled

Pet parents maintain useful care histories

Medical, payment and privacy boundaries remain intact

AI Matching, Loyalty Credits and Pet Health Timeline: GO—with human approval, immutable ledgers, transparent scoring and owner-controlled records.

Phase 11 Advanced Command Center and B2B Dashboards 🧠📊🐾

Executive decision

Approve all four product directions, with several safeguards:

### Table 190

| Product | Decision |
| --- | --- |
| Advanced admin command center | Approve—highest priority with sitter app |
| Booking risk score | Approve as explainable rules first |
| Subscription churn intelligence | Approve as support-assistance, not pressure automation |
| Society web dashboard | Approve; do not build a native app yet |
| Partner web dashboard | Approve; use role-specific modules |

The command center should not become a screen full of charts. It should be an exception-management system:

Operational events

↓

Rules and risk evaluation

↓

Actionable queue

↓

Assigned human owner

↓

Resolution

↓

Audit trail and learning

Product 3: Advanced Admin Command Center

1. Core objective

The command center should help the team answer five questions immediately:

What is happening now?

What is likely to fail?

Who must act?

How long do they have?

Was the issue resolved correctly?

It should combine:

Live operations

Booking-risk management

Safety and incidents

Supply and capacity

Subscription retention

Customer support

City and financial performance

Use consistent application events, metrics, traces and logs rather than building every dashboard from unrelated manual spreadsheets. OpenTelemetry’s observability model similarly separates telemetry into traces, metrics and logs so teams can understand system behaviour from multiple connected signals.

2. Command-center information architecture

National command center

Used by:

Founders

National operations

Safety team

Finance

Central support

Expansion leaders

Main sections

Network Overview

Cities

Live Operations

Bookings at Risk

Safety and Incidents

Sitters and Partners

Subscriptions

Customer Support

Capacity

Finance

Societies

System Health

City command center

Used by:

City manager

Operations coordinator

Local support

Supply manager

Society executive

It should automatically scope data to the staff member’s assigned city and zones.

PostgreSQL row-level security can restrict which rows a role may read, insert, update or delete. It is appropriate as a defence-in-depth layer for isolating city, society, booking and incident data, alongside application-level authorisation.

3. Live operations map

Purpose

Display operationally relevant live services—not the continuous location of every sitter.

Show

Services in progress

Sitters en route to accepted bookings

Late arrivals

Tracking interruptions

Backup assignments

Active incidents

Unassigned urgent bookings

Do not show

Sitters who are off duty

Sitters outside an active or imminent booking

Historical home locations

Location to marketing or society users

Precise routes after the operational retention period

Map states

### Table 191

| State | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Healthy | Service progressing normally |
| Watch | Minor risk signal |
| Action required | Operations intervention needed |
| Critical | Safety or fulfilment escalation |
| Offline | Live data temporarily unavailable |
| Completed | Service finished |

Example card

Bruno · Dog Walk

Sitter: Riya

City: Ahmedabad

Status: In progress

Started: 7:34 AM

Tracking: Healthy

Elapsed: 22 min

Risk: Low

[Open Booking] [Contact] [View Route]

Location, addresses, schedules and incident information should be access-limited, purpose-specific and auditable under the Indian digital-data protection framework. The notified DPDP Rules, 2025 have phased commencement, but the product should implement those controls before expanding access across cities and partner roles.

4. City health score

A city health score can be useful, but one number must never hide a safety failure.

Recommended score

### Table 192

| Dimension | Weight |
| --- | --- |
| Safety and quality | 30% |
| Fulfilment and supply | 20% |
| Retention and demand | 20% |
| Economics | 20% |
| Support and compliance | 10% |

Example

Ahmedabad City Health: 87/100 · Approved

Safety: 28/30

Fulfilment: 18/20

Retention: 16/20

Economics: 17/20

Support: 8/10

Hard overrides

The city cannot be labelled “healthy” when any of these is true:

Unresolved critical incident

Partner payouts materially overdue

Critical documents expired

Cross-city data exposure

Booking completion below minimum

Finance unreconciled

Safety incidents deliberately misclassified

Recommended health bands

### Table 193

| Score | Status | Action |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 90–100 | Excellent | Eligible for controlled growth |
| 80–89 | Healthy | Normal operation |
| 70–79 | Monitor | Restrict expansion |
| 60–69 | Intervention | Freeze new clusters |
| Below 60 | Critical | Consider service/city pause |

5. Sitter risk list

Rename this module:

Sitter Quality Review Queue

“Risk list” can become an unfair blacklist if it combines weak, noisy or outdated signals.

Signals

Repeated late arrivals

Sitter-initiated cancellations

No-shows

Missing reports

Unexplained GPS interruptions

Low recent ratings

Incident involvement

Expired documents

Unresolved payout dispute

Abnormal workload

Repeated customer complaints

Context required

Each signal should show:

Severity

Frequency

Date

Service volume denominator

Whether the concern was verified

Whether the sitter appealed

Corrective training completed

Current review status

A sitter with one complaint across 500 bookings should not be treated the same as a sitter with three complaints across ten bookings.

Review states

NORMAL

WATCH

COACHING_REQUIRED

BOOKING_LIMITED

TEMPORARILY_PAUSED

INVESTIGATION

REINSTATED

REMOVED

Required protections

Clear reasons

Human review

Appeal/dispute mechanism

No automatic lifetime penalty

Separate verified incidents from allegations

Exclude customer/admin-caused failures

Model version and audit history

6. Delayed-service alerts

A delayed-service alert should create an actionable operational queue.

Alert progression

Booking approaching start

↓

Sitter not en route

↓

Sitter reminder

↓

Grace period exceeded

↓

Operations notified

↓

Backup checked

↓

Customer proactively updated

Alert record

Booking

Scheduled time

Current sitter

Latest acknowledgement

Travel estimate

Customer communication status

Backup availability

Assigned operations owner

SLA deadline

Prometheus guidance recommends alerting on actionable symptoms and avoiding alerts where there is no meaningful response. The command center should therefore suppress duplicates, group related alerts and always present a next action.

7. Incident heatmap

The heatmap should help identify recurring operational causes—not publicly label neighbourhoods or sitters as dangerous.

Dimensions

City and service zone

Society

Service type

Time of day

Incident category

Pet-risk classification

Provider-experience level

Root cause

Severity

Incident categories

### Table 194

| Category | Examples |
| --- | --- |
| Access | Gate denial, wrong address |
| Pet handling | Escape risk, aggression |
| Medical | Pet distress, injury |
| Scheduling | No-show, late arrival |
| Customer handover | Customer unavailable |
| Technology | Tracking/app failure |
| Partner quality | Hygiene or service concern |
| Security/privacy | Wrong data or access exposure |

Heatmap safeguards

Minimum sample-size threshold

No personally identifying labels

Separate verified incidents from reports under investigation

Show exposure rate, not just raw counts

Compare against booking volume

Retain drill-down only for authorised safety roles

Example:

Wrong:

Powai has 15 incidents.

Better:

Powai had 15 reportable events across 4,200 services:

0 critical, 2 serious and 13 minor.

The financial module must distinguish:

### Table 195

| Metric | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| GMV | Total customer spending |
| Platform revenue | Retained commission and fees |
| Partner payouts | Amount payable to providers |
| Direct contribution | Revenue minus direct costs |
| City operating contribution | Contribution minus local fixed costs |
| Fully allocated result | After central cost allocation |

Required views

City

Cluster

Service

9. Capacity planning module

Purpose

Prevent the company from selling services or subscriptions that the city cannot deliver.

Show by city, zone and time window

Gross provider capacity

Existing booking commitments

Subscription reservations

Backup reserve

Leave impact

Remaining sellable capacity

Stop-sales threshold

Capacity statuses

### Table 196

| Utilisation | Status |
| --- | --- |
| Below 70% | Healthy |
| 70–85% | Controlled selling |
| 85–90% | Restrict acquisition |
| Above 90% | Stop sales |
| No backup | Block high-frequency plans |

Command-center actions

Pause a time slot

Pause a plan

Move demand to another window

Recruit providers

Increase backup reserve

Waitlist customers

Open an adjacent zone only after gate approval

Queues

Metrics

### Table 197

| Metric | Purpose |
| --- | --- |

### Table 198

| Severity | Example | Target |
| --- | --- | --- |

Booking Risk Score

11. Product objective

Identify bookings that require additional preparation before they fail.

The score should:

Be calculated before confirmation

Update when conditions change

Explain every risk factor

Recommend actions

Remain reviewable by humans

NIST’s AI Risk Management Framework recommends documenting human oversight, system limitations, explanations and monitoring of unexpected behaviour or performance degradation.

12. Corrected risk factors

### Table 199

| Factor | Recommended treatment |
| --- | --- |
| First-time customer | Low–medium context signal |
| First booking for pet | Medium |
| New sitter | Medium; do not automatically penalise heavily |
| High pet-risk classification | High |
| Long travel time | High |
| No approved backup | High/critical for subscriptions |
| Peak-time booking | Medium |
| Unpaid booking | Block confirmation rather than score it |
| Previous complaint | Depends on severity, recency and resolution |
| Boarding | Higher custody exposure |
| Medication/senior care | High or specialist review |
| Society access incomplete | High |
| Tight back-to-back schedule | High |
| Expired sitter document | Ineligible, not merely risky |
| Open sitter investigation | Ineligible or senior review |

Important correction

Some conditions should be hard blockers, not score factors:

Payment not verified

Required document expired

Sitter not qualified

No service capacity

Sitter under suspension

Service unavailable in zone

A 95/100 match must not override an expired document or failed payment.

13. Booking-risk score design

Use an explainable point score first.

Example

### Table 200

| Factor | Points |
| --- | --- |
| First booking for pet | +8 |
| New sitter | +8 |
| Yellow pet-risk level | +15 |
| Red/specialist pet | +30 |
| Travel above target | +15 |
| No backup | +20 |
| Peak-time slot | +8 |
| Prior unresolved complaint | +20 |
| Boarding service | +15 |
| Medication required | +20 |
| Gate approval missing | +15 |

Mitigating factors

### Table 201

| Mitigation | Adjustment |
| --- | --- |
| Previous successful pet-sitter relationship | −20 |
| Experienced specialist sitter | −10 |
| Named approved backup | −15 |
| Successful trial completed | −10 |
| Same society/locality sitter | −5 |
| Flexible time window | −5 |

Levels

### Table 202

| Score | Level | Action |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 0–19 | Low | Normal workflow |
| 20–39 | Medium | Operations visibility |
| 40–59 | High | Backup and admin review |
| 60+ | Critical | Senior approval or reject |
| Hard blocker | Blocked | Cannot confirm |

Do not call this a probability unless it has been calibrated against real booking outcomes.

14. Risk-score output

Booking Risk: 47/100 · High

Reasons

• First service with this pet: +8

• Yellow behavioural risk: +15

• Travel estimate 31 minutes: +15

• Peak-time booking: +8

• Approved backup available: −15

• Experienced large-dog sitter: −10

Required actions

✓ Admin review

✓ Confirm introduction notes

✓ Keep backup reserved

15. Dynamic risk updates

Risk should be recalculated when:

Sitter changes

Customer updates pet instructions

Backup cancels

Payment fails

Weather or route conditions affect travel

Society gate approval changes

Primary sitter reports leave

A provider investigation begins

Store risk snapshots instead of overwriting history.

booking_risk_snapshots

- booking_id

- score

- level

- factor_scores

- blockers

- recommended_actions

- model_version

- calculated_at

16. Risk-score governance

Track:

High-risk false positives

Low-risk bookings that failed

Admin override rate

Outcome by risk band

Outcome by city

New-sitter treatment

Pet-risk classification accuracy

Score drift

Model/rule version

NIST recommends regular post-deployment monitoring to identify degradation, near-misses and unexpected behaviour.

Subscription Intelligence

17. Churn-risk objective

Identify subscribers who may need product or service recovery before renewal.

The goal is not:

Find users who can be pressured into paying.

The goal is:

Detect service-plan mismatch and offer a relevant remedy.

18. Corrected churn signals

### Table 203

| Signal | Likely interpretation |
| --- | --- |
| Unused credits | Plan may be oversized |
| Credits exhausted early | Upgrade opportunity |
| Low service rating | Quality problem |
| Repeated sitter changes | Continuity problem |
| Payment failure | Payment-method issue |
| Repeated missed walks | Timing mismatch |
| Multiple support complaints | Unresolved dissatisfaction |
| Repeated pauses | Seasonal or poor plan fit |
| Drop in app/report usage | Lower engagement |
| Cancellation-page visit | Strong churn intent |
| Preferred sitter unavailable | Trust risk |
| Travel dates | Temporary pause opportunity |

Do not use sensitive pet-health information for marketing or churn pressure without a clearly justified purpose and suitable customer controls.

19. Churn-risk model

Begin with rule-based scoring:

### Table 204

| Signal | Example points |
| --- | --- |
| Credit utilisation below 40% | +20 |
| Last rating below 4 | +25 |
| Two or more sitter changes | +20 |
| Payment failure | +25 |
| Two support complaints | +20 |
| Pause more than twice | +15 |
| Preferred sitter unavailable | +15 |
| Customer opened cancellation flow | +30 |

Levels

### Table 205

| Score | Level | Recommended action |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 0–19 | Healthy | No intervention |
| 20–39 | Watch | In-app education |
| 40–59 | High | Support review |
| 60+ | Critical | Human retention outreach |

20. Remedy engine

Do not immediately offer discounts.

Match the intervention to the cause:

### Table 206

| Churn cause | Best response |
| --- | --- |
| Low utilisation | Downgrade or pause |
| Travel | Skip cycle or temporary pause |
| Sitter changes | Primary/backup review |
| Timing issue | Reschedule recurring slot |
| Payment failure | Update method/manual link |
| Low rating | Service recovery |
| Too expensive | Smaller plan |
| Credits expiring | Reminder or limited rollover |
| Pet health change | Pause without sales pressure |

Workflow

Risk detected

↓

Cause ranked

↓

Support reviews context

↓

Relevant action offered

↓

Customer chooses

↓

Outcome recorded

21. Retention-message controls

Customer communication preferences respected

No repeated daily pressure

Separate service messages from marketing

Clear opt-out controls

No false urgency

Cancellation remains available

Discounts require authorised limits

Every manual offer is recorded

22. Churn dashboard

Show:

Subscribers by risk level

Renewal date

Primary churn drivers

Credits used

Sitter continuity

Failed-payment state

Support owner

Last contact

Recommended action

Outcome

KPIs

Renewal rate

Churn rate

Save rate

Downgrade rate

Pause-to-reactivation

Payment recovery

Discount cost

Contribution retained

Complaint rate from retention outreach

Do not optimise only for “saved subscriptions.” A forced save followed by a refund is not success.

Web dashboard is sufficient for Phase 11.

Society stakeholders generally need periodic reporting, gate lists and event management—not constant native-app engagement.

Partner Dashboard Upgrade

26. Product decision

Keep groomers, trainers, vets and other specialist partners on a responsive web dashboard during Phase 11.

Build a native partner app only when:

Partners receive frequent daily bookings

Push responsiveness becomes a bottleneck

Offline reporting is necessary

Web adoption materially damages completion

The business can support another mobile release cycle

| Live operations map | Approve—active services only |
| City health score | Approve with safety overrides |
| Sitter risk list | Rename Quality Review Queue |
| Booking risk predictor | Approve as explainable rules first |
| Subscription churn risk | Approve with human-reviewed remedies |
| Delayed-service alerts | Approve |
| Incident heatmap | Approve with severity and volume context |
| Revenue by city/service | Approve with GMV/revenue/contribution separation |
| Capacity planning | Mandatory |
| Support SLA dashboard | Mandatory |
| First-time customer as major risk | Reduce importance |
| Unpaid booking as risk | Treat as confirmation blocker |
| No backup sitter | High/critical risk |
| Senior approval for critical booking | Approve |
| Society native app | Defer |
| Society web dashboard | Approve |
| RWA access to resident details | Reject; aggregate data only |
| Partner web dashboard | Approve |
| Partner package editing | Approve with versioned approval workflow |
| Native partner app | Defer |

Phase 11 command-center doctrine

Predict only what can be explained, alert only when somebody can act, expose only the data a role needs and keep humans accountable for high-impact decisions.

Win condition

The system succeeds when:

Operations intervene before bookings fail

Safety alerts are faster and less noisy

City performance is directly comparable

Subscription remedies match actual churn causes

Societies receive useful aggregate reports without resident surveillance

Partners manage bookings and payouts independently

Every override and sensitive action remains auditable

Advanced Admin Command Center: GO—with event-driven operations, explainable risk scores, human review and strict role-based data access.

Phase 11 Database, API, Security and Location-Privacy Architecture 🔐📱🐾

Executive decision

Your proposed foundation is good, but it needs several production-grade corrections:

Separate devices, push tokens and authenticated sessions.

Separate tracking sessions, GPS points and operational events.

Store matching recommendations as versioned runs with explanations and admin decisions.

Replace the mutable wallet.balance model with an immutable service-credit ledger.

Split pet timeline events, documents, reminders and sharing permissions.

Make all APIs object-authorized, idempotent and role-scoped.

Treat live location as a temporary, purpose-limited operational data stream.

The corrected architecture should be:

Mobile device

├── authenticated session

├── push token

└── app/version state

Booking

├── service session

├── tracking session

├── tracking points

├── operational events

├── service report

└── incidents

Customer

├── credit accounts

├── immutable credit ledger

├── sitter preferences

└── pet timeline

1. Device and push-token schema

Problem with the current table

device_tokens

- user_id

- device_type

- token

- app_version

This combines three different concepts:

The physical/app device installation

The push-notification token

The authenticated user session

One user may have several devices, multiple users may sequentially use the same device, and push tokens can rotate or become invalid. Firebase recommends storing a timestamp with registration tokens, refreshing token records periodically and removing tokens that are stale or rejected as invalid.

Recommended tables

mobile_devices

- id

- user_id nullable

- installation_id

- app_type // CUSTOMER, SITTER

- platform // IOS, ANDROID

- device_model

- os_version

- app_version

- build_number

- locale

- timezone

- status

- first_seen_at

- last_seen_at

- created_at

- updated_at

push_tokens

- id

- device_id

- provider // FCM, APNS, EXPO

- token_encrypted

- environment // DEVELOPMENT, PRODUCTION

- status // ACTIVE, INVALID, REVOKED

- notifications_enabled

- registered_at

- last_validated_at

- last_sent_at

- invalidated_at

- created_at

- updated_at

mobile_sessions

- id

- user_id

- device_id

- refresh_token_family_id

- refresh_token_hash

- status

- issued_at

- last_used_at

- expires_at

- revoked_at

- compromise_detected_at

- last_ip_hash

- created_at

Important rules

Use a stable app-generated installation_id.

Keep one push-token record per app installation and provider.

Never treat a push token as an authentication credential.

Encrypt the usable token at rest because the server must retrieve it to send notifications.

Do not write push tokens into application logs.

Mark tokens invalid after permanent provider errors.

Detach or revoke the token when a user logs out of a shared device.

Refresh tokens should use rotation: each refresh issues a new refresh token and invalidates the previous one while retaining the token-family relationship for replay detection. This is the current OAuth security best practice.

On the device, refresh tokens should be stored in secure platform-backed storage, such as Expo SecureStore, rather than AsyncStorage or ordinary local files. Expo SecureStore is intended for small secrets such as tokens and keys.

2. Tracking schema

Problem with one tracking_events table

This table mixes:

High-frequency GPS samples

Service lifecycle events

Alerts

Tracking state

Arbitrary metadata

tracking_events

- booking_id

- event_type

- lat

- lng

- metadata

Continuous location points and business events have different volume, retention, indexing and access requirements.

Corrected structure

Tracking session

tracking_sessions

- id

- booking_id

- sitter_id

- device_id

- service_session_id

- status

- permission_state

- consent_notice_version

- started_at

- ended_at

- last_point_at

- termination_reason

- point_count

- distance_meters

- duration_seconds

- tracking_quality

- created_at

- updated_at

Statuses:

PENDING

ACTIVE

OFFLINE

PERMISSION_LOST

APP_TERMINATED

COMPLETED

ABORTED

UNDER_REVIEW

GPS points

tracking_points

- id

- tracking_session_id

- sequence_number

- location geography(Point, 4326)

- recorded_at_device

- received_at_server

- accuracy_meters

- altitude_meters nullable

- speed_mps nullable

- heading_degrees nullable

- battery_percent nullable

- source

- is_mock_location_suspected

- created_at

PostGIS supports geography(Point,4326) for longitude/latitude coordinates and GiST spatial indexes, which is preferable to repeatedly processing separate unindexed lat and lng values.

For high-volume production use:

Partition tracking_points by time once volume justifies it.

Index tracking_session_id, recorded_at_device.

Add a GiST index on location.

Batch-upload points rather than making one HTTP request per point.

Use sequence_number and idempotency keys to recover offline data safely.

Store raw tracking data separately from derived distance and route summaries.

Operational tracking events

tracking_events

- id

- tracking_session_id

- booking_id

- event_type

- severity

- event_time_device

- received_at_server

- metadata_json

- created_by

- idempotency_key

- created_at

Event types:

TRACKING_STARTED

TRACKING_PAUSED_SYSTEM

TRACKING_RESUMED

LOCATION_PERMISSION_LOST

GPS_DISABLED

APP_TERMINATED

NETWORK_OFFLINE

NETWORK_RESTORED

GEOFENCE_ARRIVAL

ROUTE_ANOMALY_DETECTED

LONG_PAUSE_DETECTED

TRACKING_ENDED

The event does not need latitude and longitude unless the event itself requires a location snapshot.

Problem with the proposed table

matching_recommendations

Which algorithm produced the score

Which other sitters were eligible

Whether the admin overrode the recommendation

The eventual service outcome

matching_decisions

- selected_candidate_id nullable

matching_outcomes

- customer_rating

- customer_requested_repeat

- sitter_cancelled

- backup_used

- incident_id nullable

- recorded_at

Scoring rule

Use:

Riya — Match score 92/100

Do not label it “92% probability” unless the model is statistically calibrated and validated as a probability.

Hard eligibility filters must execute before ranking:

Current verification

Valid documents

Correct service permission

Availability

Pet-risk compatibility

Capacity

Service-zone access

No suspension

No schedule conflict

The admin should see explanations and retain final approval during the initial rollout.

4. Replace the wallet with a credit ledger

Problem with this schema

wallets

- user_id

- balance

A mutable balance does not provide a reliable audit trail and can become inconsistent during concurrent bookings, refunds and retries.

There is also a regulatory distinction between internal service credits and a cash-like prepaid wallet. RBI states that a closed-system instrument can only purchase the issuer’s own goods or services, cannot support cash withdrawal and cannot settle third-party services. A marketplace involving independent service providers should therefore obtain payments advice before creating reloadable cash balances.

Recommended early model

Name the feature Credits, not Wallet.

credit_accounts

- id

- customer_id

- credit_type

- currency

- status

- created_at

- updated_at

Credit types:

SUBSCRIPTION

REFERRAL

PROMOTIONAL

SOCIETY_BENEFIT

SERVICE_RECOVERY

REFUND_ELECTED

credit_ledger_entries

- id

- credit_account_id

- booking_id nullable

- subscription_id nullable

- referral_id nullable

- entry_type

- amount_minor

- source_reference

- reason_code

- expires_at nullable

- idempotency_key

- created_by_type

- created_by_id

- created_at

Entry types:

GRANT

RESERVE

CONSUME

RELEASE

RESTORE

EXPIRE

REVERSE

ADMIN_ADJUSTMENT

Balance calculation

Available credits

=

grants

+ releases

+ restores

− reservations

− consumption

− reversals

− expiry

A cached available_balance_minor may be maintained for performance, but the immutable ledger remains the financial source of truth.

Controls

Store money in minor units, such as paise.

Require idempotency keys for every adjustment.

Every admin adjustment needs a reason.

Require dual approval above a configured threshold.

Do not allow customer transfers or cash withdrawal.

Do not force eligible cash refunds into platform credits without customer choice.

UNIQUE(customer_id, pet_id, sitter_id, preference_type)

Use customer-facing language such as Preferred sitter, not guaranteed sitter.

A DO_NOT_MATCH preference should be tightly access-controlled because it may contain complaint or safety context.

8. Corrected mobile authentication APIs

Use a versioned API namespace.

POST /v1/mobile/auth/login

POST /v1/mobile/auth/refresh

POST /v1/mobile/auth/logout

GET /v1/mobile/auth/sessions

DELETE /v1/mobile/auth/sessions/{sessionId}

Login response

{

"access_token": "...",

"access_token_expires_at": "...",

"refresh_token": "...",

"session_id": "...",

"device_id": "..."

}

Security rules

Short-lived access token

Rotating, per-device refresh token

Refresh-token reuse detection

Server-side session revocation

Rate limiting and OTP abuse protection

Logout from one device or every device

MFA for national and city administrators

Separate mobile and admin session policies

OWASP identifies broken authentication and broken object-level authorization as major API risks. Every endpoint accepting an object identifier must verify that the authenticated actor is authorized to access that particular object.

9. Device-token APIs

Replace generic token endpoints with device-scoped endpoints:

PUT /v1/mobile/devices/{deviceId}/push-token

DELETE /v1/mobile/devices/{deviceId}/push-token

PATCH /v1/mobile/devices/{deviceId}

Example registration:

{

"provider": "FCM",

"token": "...",

"app_type": "SITTER",

"platform": "ANDROID",

"environment": "PRODUCTION",

"app_version": "2.3.0",

"build_number": "147"

}

The deviceId must belong to the current authenticated session.

10. Push-notification APIs

Do not expose this publicly

POST /api/notifications/push

A general client-callable push endpoint could be abused to send arbitrary notifications.

Use an internal event-driven service:

POST /internal/v1/notifications/events

POST /internal/v1/notifications/send

Only trusted backend services or tightly authorized admins should invoke it.

Customer-facing APIs:

GET /v1/me/notification-preferences

PATCH /v1/me/notification-preferences

GET /v1/me/notifications

POST /v1/me/notifications/{notificationId}/read

Store critical booking state in the backend and in-app inbox. Push is a delivery mechanism, not proof that the user received or read an operational update.

11. Advanced tracking APIs

Corrected endpoints

POST /v1/sitter/bookings/{bookingId}/tracking-sessions

POST /v1/tracking-sessions/{sessionId}/points:batch

POST /v1/tracking-sessions/{sessionId}/events

POST /v1/tracking-sessions/{sessionId}/end

GET /v1/customer/bookings/{bookingId}/live-tracking

GET /v1/customer/bookings/{bookingId}/route-replay

GET /v1/admin/bookings/{bookingId}/tracking

Why batch points?

Instead of:

POST /api/tracking/point

for every coordinate, send small batches:

{

"batch_id": "uuid",

"points": [

{

"sequence": 101,

"recorded_at": "...",

"latitude": 23.01,

"longitude": 72.52,

"accuracy_meters": 12

}

]

}

This is more resilient to weak connectivity and lowers network and battery overhead.

Required controls

The booking must be assigned to the sitter.

The service must permit tracking.

Only one active session is allowed per booking.

Start and end calls require idempotency keys.

Points outside the active session are rejected.

Customer access is limited to their booking.

Admin access is limited to operations/safety roles.

Route replay omits pre-service and post-service travel.

Expo supports location subscriptions and background-task integration, but background execution remains subject to mobile-platform restrictions, so the backend must explicitly represent interrupted or incomplete tracking rather than fabricating a continuous route.

12. Matching APIs

GET /v1/admin/bookings/{bookingId}/matching-recommendations

POST /v1/admin/bookings/{bookingId}/matching-recommendations:refresh

POST /v1/admin/bookings/{bookingId}/assignments

Assignment payload:

{

"matching_run_id": "uuid",

"sitter_id": "uuid",

"candidate_id": "uuid",

"decision_type": "RECOMMENDED",

"override_reason": null,

"expected_booking_version": 14

}

Use optimistic concurrency through expected_booking_version so that two admins cannot assign different sitters simultaneously.

13. Credit APIs

Read APIs

GET /v1/me/credits

GET /v1/me/credit-transactions

Do not use a separate client-controlled apply endpoint

POST /api/wallet/apply-credit

Credit reservation and use should be part of the atomic booking checkout:

POST /v1/bookings/quote

POST /v1/bookings

Example:

{

"pet_id": "...",

"service_package_id": "...",

"scheduled_at": "...",

"payment_method": "SERVICE_CREDITS",

"credit_account_id": "...",

"idempotency_key": "..."

}

The backend must:

Lock or safely reserve the available credit.

Create the booking.

Record the ledger reservation.

Roll back the entire operation if any step fails.

Admin API:

POST /v1/admin/customers/{customerId}/credit-adjustments

Required fields:

Credit type

Amount

Reason code

Case or booking reference

Idempotency key

Approver where required

14. Health-timeline APIs

GET /v1/pets/{petId}/health-timeline

POST /v1/pets/{petId}/health-timeline

PATCH /v1/pets/{petId}/health-timeline/{eventId}

DELETE /v1/pets/{petId}/health-timeline/{eventId}

Your proposed delete endpoint contains a typo:

:eventide

It should be:

:eventId

Additional endpoints:

POST /v1/pets/{petId}/health-documents/upload-url

POST /v1/pets/{petId}/health-reminders

PATCH /v1/pets/{petId}/health-reminders/{reminderId}

POST /v1/pets/{petId}/record-access-grants

DELETE /v1/pets/{petId}/record-access-grants/{grantId}
