An update should usually use PATCH, because clients commonly change only selected fields.

15. Feedback API

Add the missing endpoint:

POST /v1/mobile/feedback

GET /v1/admin/mobile-feedback

PATCH /v1/admin/mobile-feedback/{feedbackId}

The mobile client should send only explicitly allowlisted diagnostic fields.

16. Security requirements

Authentication

Rotating refresh tokens

Short-lived access tokens

Per-device sessions

Session revocation

OTP and login rate limits

MFA for administrators

Reauthentication for high-risk actions

No tokens in URLs or logs

Authorization

Use RBAC plus contextual checks:

Role

+ city scope

+ service-zone scope

+ booking relationship

+ action permission

Examples:

A sitter can read only currently assigned bookings.

A customer can read only their own pets and bookings.

A city manager can access only assigned cities.

A society user can see aggregate society information.

A vet can access only explicitly shared pet records.

PostgreSQL Row-Level Security can restrict which rows a database role may select or modify and provides a default-deny posture when enabled without an applicable policy. Use it as defence in depth, not as a replacement for application authorization.

Device integrity

Consider Play Integrity for high-risk Android actions such as:

Login from a new device

Credit adjustment attempts

Suspicious tracking submissions

Referral qualification

High-value booking activity

Play Integrity allows the backend to assess whether requests originate from the genuine app installed through Google Play on a recognized device. It should provide a risk signal—not automatically block every unsupported device.

Secure local storage

Store in SecureStore or platform key storage:

Refresh token

Device-session identifier

Small encryption keys

Do not store there:

Large pet records

GPS history

Media

Complete booking databases

Media uploads

OWASP recommends allowlisting file types, validating content rather than trusting the filename or HTTP content type, limiting sizes, generating safe filenames, storing files outside executable web roots and applying malware scanning where appropriate.

Required controls:

Signed uploads

MIME and magic-byte validation

Size and duration limits

Image/video transcoding

Malware scanning

Randomized storage keys

No public buckets for private records

Expiring download URLs

Metadata stripping where appropriate

Logging

Never log:

Refresh/access tokens

Push tokens

Gate codes

Exact health documents

Full GPS payloads

Payment data

Customer access instructions

Log:

Actor

Action

Target entity

Result

Timestamp

City

Device/session ID

Correlation ID

Security reason

Forced updates

Support:

minimum_supported_version

recommended_version

update_mode

Modes:

OPTIONAL

RECOMMENDED

REQUIRED

SECURITY_BLOCK

Do not force an update during an active walk unless the existing app version presents an immediate security or safety risk. Allow completion of the live service, then block further bookings.

17. Location privacy policy

Mandatory rules

Track only during a sitter-initiated eligible service.

Stop tracking automatically when the service ends.

Do not track during off-duty periods.

Explain foreground and background use before requesting permission.

Show tracking status visibly to the sitter.

Give customers access only to their active or historical booking.

Restrict admin access to authorized operations and safety roles.

Log every sensitive administrative access.

Define and enforce route-data retention.

Delete or de-identify raw points after the retention purpose expires.

Retain incident-related routes separately under a documented legal or investigation hold.

Do not send raw routes to general analytics tools.

Android requires developers to accurately declare location-data collection and sharing, and background-location access is treated distinctly from ordinary foreground location.

The DPDP Act requires consent requests to be presented clearly and in plain language, while the notified Rules establish the current implementation framework. Location notices should therefore explain the purpose, duration, recipients, controls and grievance contact in straightforward language.

Recommended data separation

Exact GPS points

→ restricted tracking store

Derived distance/duration

→ ordinary booking report

Service zone and city

→ operational analytics

De-identified aggregate patterns

→ capacity and route analysis

Exact customer coordinates and access codes should not be available to marketing, finance or society roles.

18. User-facing location wording

Short permission explanation

Location during active servicesYour location is collected only while you start and perform an active pet-care service. It helps verify arrival, share live service updates, document the service route and provide safety support. Tracking stops when the service ends.

Detailed wording

When you start an eligible pet-care service, the app may collect your location while the app is open and, where required for route continuity, while it runs in the background. The pet parent can view location updates only for their active booking. Authorized operations and safety staff may access the information when needed to manage the service, investigate an incident or provide support.

The app does not track your location outside an active service. Route data is retained only for the period described in our privacy notice and is then deleted or de-identified unless it is required for an open safety, support or legal matter.

Interrupted tracking message for customers

Live location is temporarily unavailable. The service remains in progress, and our operations team has been notified.

Avoid blaming the sitter when the cause may be:

Network loss

Permission change

Device shutdown

Operating-system restriction

GPS inaccuracy

19. Final approval matrix

### Table 211

| Proposal | Decision |
| --- | --- |
| Single device_tokens table | Split devices, tokens and sessions |
| Store token timestamp/version | Approve |
| One tracking_events table | Split sessions, points and events |
| Raw latitude/longitude columns | Use PostGIS geography where available |
| One request per GPS point | Replace with batch ingestion |
| Matching score and reasons | Approve with algorithm versioning |
| accepted_by_admin Boolean | Replace with decision record |
| Mutable wallet balance | Reject as source of truth |
| Immutable credit ledger | Mandatory |
| User-funded cash wallet | Defer pending payment review |
| Favourite sitter table | Approve with preference status |
| Single health-event table | Split events, documents, reminders and access grants |
| Mobile feedback table | Approve with diagnostic metadata and redaction |
| Rotating refresh tokens | Mandatory |
| Public push-send endpoint | Reject |
| Object-level authorization | Mandatory on every ID endpoint |
| Media validation | Mandatory |
| Admin MFA | Mandatory for privileged roles |
| Tracking only during service | Mandatory |
| Customer access to another booking’s tracking | Reject |
| Unlimited location retention | Reject |
| Forced update capability | Approve with active-service safeguards |

Phase 11 technical win condition

Every mobile action is attributable to a device and session, every service has a defensible event history, every credit change is auditable, every record is role-scoped and location is collected only for a clearly defined active-service purpose.

Phase 11 database, API, security and privacy architecture: GO—with the corrected normalized schemas, immutable ledgers, rotating sessions and purpose-limited tracking model above

Phase 11 Six-Month Execution Plan 📱🐾

Executive verdict

Approve the six-month roadmap, with five structural corrections:

Prototype background GPS in Month 1, rather than discovering technical limitations in Week 9.

Build the admin command centre alongside the sitter app, because it must receive and act on sitter events.

Replace “wallet” with an immutable service-credit ledger. Defer customer-funded cash balances.

Launch rules-based, explainable matching first; advanced AI comes after sufficient outcome data exists.

Security, privacy, physical-device QA and app-store preparation must run throughout the programme—not only during Week 23.

The corrected delivery sequence is:

Product and technical validation

↓

Sitter app foundation

↓

Tracking + emergency + admin operations

↓

Customer app

↓

Subscriptions + credits + referrals

↓

Matching assistance + hardening

↓

Controlled store launch

Expo background location and remote push notifications require development builds rather than relying only on Expo Go. Expo also documents that background location stops if the user terminates the application, so this limitation must be tested and represented truthfully in the operational state model from the first month.

Month 1: Product Planning and Architecture

Primary objective

Freeze what Phase 11 will solve, validate the technically risky features and prepare a production-grade delivery system.

Month 1 should not produce only wireframes. It should prove that the hardest mobile workflows are technically viable on real devices.

Week 1: Scope freeze and mobile PRD

Goal

Define the exact Phase 11 boundaries before designers and developers begin building screens.

Required product decisions

Sitter app P0

Authentication and device registration

Accept/reject

Booking and pet instructions

Arrival and handover

Start/end service

Active-service GPS

Offline execution

Media uploads

Report cards

Emergency escalation

Earnings

Push notifications

Customer app P0

Authentication

Pet profiles

Booking and payment

Active service status

Report cards

Reviews

Subscription management

Remaining service credits

Notifications

Support and account controls

Admin P0

Live bookings

Delayed-service queue

Tracking-health states

Incident queue

Missing reports

City and role scoping

Explicit non-goals

Keep these outside the initial release:

Continuous sitter tracking

Week 3: API readiness and backend-gap analysis

Goal

Ensure the backend can support authoritative, offline-safe mobile workflows.

API audit

Versioning

The booking lifecycle should be explicit:

REQUESTED

ACCEPTED

CONFIRMED

EN_ROUTE

ARRIVED

HANDOVER_CONFIRMED

IN_PROGRESS

COMPLETION_REQUESTED

COMPLETED

REPORT_SUBMITTED

CANCELLED

INCIDENT_OPEN

The backend must reject invalid transitions. For example:

A sitter cannot start an unassigned booking.

A customer cannot view another customer’s route.

A completed service cannot be started again.

An expired document cannot be overridden by a high matching score.

Android background location requires platform-specific permissions, and Android 11+ generally routes “Allow all the time” access through system settings. Android 13+ also introduced runtime notification permission, which matters for visible foreground-service notifications.

Test scenarios

Screen locked

App backgrounded

App force-closed

GPS disabled

Permission revoked

Device offline

Device reboot

Low battery

Poor GPS accuracy

Duplicate point upload

Service ends without network

Acceptance criteria

Tracking occurs only during eligible active services.

The app visibly indicates tracking state.

Tracking stops after service completion.

App termination creates an interrupted state, not a false continuous route.

Customer sees only their booking.

Admin access is role-scoped and audited.

Apple requires apps that offer account creation to allow users to initiate account deletion within the app. Google Play also requires deletion of account-associated data when a valid account-deletion request is completed, subject to clearly disclosed legitimate retention requirements.

| Soft launch in Week 24 | Approve as a staged controlled release |
| Immediate forced migration from PWA | Reject |

Phase 11 six-month win condition

The sitter app reliably captures and proves service execution, the command centre surfaces failures before they affect customers, and the customer app increases self-service and retention—without introducing unsafe tracking, credit errors or uncontrolled operational complexity.

Final Phase 11 execution decision: GO—with GPS validation moved forward, admin operations developed in parallel, service credits replacing the cash wallet, and staged store release after continuous QA and security verification

Phase 11 App Launch, Store Preparation and City Rollout Strategy 📱🐾

Executive decision

Approve the staged app-launch model. Do not release the sitter and customer apps to every city and user simultaneously.

The recommended rollout is:

Internal alpha

↓

Sitter closed beta

↓

Customer closed beta

↓

Single-city production launch

↓

Selected-cluster expansion

↓

Multi-city rollout

Each stage must have a measurable exit gate. Progress should depend on product reliability, operational performance and safety—not merely the scheduled launch date.

1. End-to-end app launch strategy

Before team testing, validate the highest-risk capabilities on physical devices:

Login and refresh-token rotation

Push-notification delivery

Deep links

Start/end service idempotency

GPS with the screen locked

Media-upload retry

This stage uses development and staging environments, not real customer bookings.

For new personal Google Play developer accounts, Google currently documents a production-access requirement involving a closed test with at least 12 opted-in testers maintained for 14 continuous days. Confirm whether the specific developer account falls under that requirement before setting the launch date.

Stage 4: Single-city production launch

Recommended launch city

Select the city with:

The strongest city operations team

Stable sitter-app adoption

4.6+ service rating

Strong support coverage

Working subscription operations

Manageable cluster geography

Reliable incident escalation

Launch only in configured service clusters—not automatically across the entire city.

Rollout controls

City-level feature flag

Customer eligibility flag

Percentage-based invitation cohorts

Supported app-version policy

Tracking kill switch

Mobile-booking kill switch

PWA fallback

Rollback procedure

First production cohorts

10% of eligible repeat users

↓

25%

↓

50%

↓

100% of eligible users in launch clusters

Google Play’s staged rollout feature applies to updates rather than an app’s first production publication. For the initial release, use closed testing, controlled country availability, invitations and backend feature flags. Later updates can be released to percentages of production users.

Apple allows manual release after approval, which gives the team control over the exact production-launch moment.

| Screenshots | Required | Show real application functionality |
| App description | Required | Accurate product explanation |
| Short description | Required on Google Play | Concise store summary |
| Feature graphic | Required/relevant on Google Play | Store promotional asset |
| Privacy policy | Required | Must match actual data practices |
| Terms of Service | Operationally required | Service, booking, tracking and credit terms |
| Support contact | Required | Apple support URL; Google contact email |
| Demo/reviewer account | Conditional but usually needed | Required when reviewers cannot access the app normally |
| Age rating | Required | Store content classification |
| Permission explanations | Required for sensitive permissions | In-app and store disclosures |
| Apple privacy details | Required | Declared data collection and use |
| Google Data Safety form | Required outside internal-only testing | Declared collection, sharing and protection |
| Account-deletion flow | Required for apps with account creation | In-app and applicable web controls |
| Background-location declaration | Required on Google Play when used | Includes disclosure and review materials |
| Reviewer notes | Strongly required | Explain test scenarios and background tracking |
| Export-compliance answers | Conditional | Apple encryption-related submission questions |

Apple requires a privacy-policy URL, description, support URL and age rating as part of its app information. It also requires app screenshots for the product page, while a preview video is optional.

Google Play requires a contact email and uses assets such as the icon, screenshots, feature graphic and descriptions in the store listing.

App icon

Prepare separate icons for:

Customer app

Friendly pet-parent identity

Clear brand mark

Simple silhouette

Legible at small size

Sitter app

You may use the same national brand with a controlled visual distinction, such as:

Sitter submark

Different internal accent

Small “Pro” or caregiver identifier

Avoid creating two unrelated brands.

Apple’s icon is packaged with the application build and appears across the Home Screen, search, settings, notifications and TestFlight.

Splash/launch screen

The splash screen is not a separate store-listing requirement, but it remains important product work.

It should:

Load quickly

Use a simple brand mark

Avoid advertising

Avoid fake progress bars

Transition smoothly

Respect light/dark appearance if supported

Do not make the user wait for animation before accessing an urgent active-service screen.

Store screenshots

Create screenshots around benefits, not generic menus.

Customer app screenshot sequence

Book verified local pet care

Track an active walk

Receive detailed report cards

Manage walking subscriptions

Keep every pet profile organised

Access support when needed

Sitter app screenshot sequence

Manage today’s bookings

Start and document a service

Submit professional care reports

Access emergency support

Track earnings transparently

Manage availability

Do not use fake screens or functionality unavailable in the submitted build. Google’s store-listing guidance requires descriptions and assets to represent the app accurately and avoid misleading claims.

Description and claims

Customer app positioning

Book verified local pet-care services, follow active service updates, receive care reports and manage your recurring pet-care plans.

Sitter app positioning

Manage assigned pet-care bookings, document services, submit care reports, access safety support and track approved earnings.

Avoid claims such as:

100% safe

Guaranteed sitter

Always accurate GPS

Instant veterinary emergency response

Best pet-care app

Government-approved sitters

Claims should correspond to measurable, available functionality.

Privacy policy

The policy must explain:

Account information

Pet-profile information

Addresses and society-access details

Location collection

Media uploads

Booking and payment data

Health-document storage

Push notifications

Analytics and crash reporting

Data sharing with assigned providers

Retention periods

Customer rights and deletion

Security and grievance contact

Apple requires privacy details that account for data collected by the app and integrated third-party SDKs. Google similarly requires the Data Safety form for apps on closed, open and production tracks.

Terms and service policies

Include:

Booking confirmation

Provider assignment

Preferred-sitter limitations

Cancellations

Refunds

Subscription credits

Expiry and rollover

GPS limitations

Media consent

Emergency-coordination limitations

Veterinary boundaries

Pet-parent responsibilities

Sitter responsibilities

Incident handling

Account suspension

Governing law and dispute process

Apple supplies a standard EULA unless the developer provides a custom agreement, but this does not replace your marketplace-specific booking and service terms.

Demo accounts and review instructions

When the app requires authentication, provide:

Customer demo account

Sitter demo account

Admin-assisted test booking

Test pet profile

Test subscription or credits

Instructions to start a simulated walk

Explanation of background location

Test emergency flow

Review contact

Apple states that review demo-account credentials should remain valid and not expire. Google Play provides a section for special app-access instructions for reviewers.

Do not provide reviewers with access to real customer or sitter records.

3. Permission strategy

Main principle

Request a permission at the moment the user activates the related feature—not during first launch.

Android recommends associating runtime permission requests with the specific user action requiring them. Google Play also restricts sensitive permissions to current, disclosed and genuinely necessary features.

Location

Sitter-app use

Location is required for:

Arrival confirmation

Active-walk tracking

Route proof

Safety support

Customer active-service updates

Rules

Request when the sitter prepares for the first tracked service

Track only between service start and end

Show an active tracking indicator

Stop automatically after completion

Store routes for a documented period

Do not use routes for marketing

Do not track general sitter availability

Allow operations access only when required

Google Play requires background location to be core to the app, visibly disclosed in-app, documented in the privacy policy and supported by a permissions declaration and demonstration for review.

Recommended sitter disclosure

Location during active servicesThe app uses your location only while you perform an eligible pet-care service. This verifies arrival, provides service-route updates and supports safety assistance. Tracking stops when the service is completed.

Customer-app location

The customer app usually does not require continuous location permission.

Use:

Address entry

Map pin

Optional one-time current-location selection

Do not request persistent customer location merely to make booking slightly faster.

Camera

Use for:

Pet photos

Service-proof images

Report cards

Incident evidence

Health-document capture

Recommended disclosure

Camera access lets you capture pet-care updates, service reports and documents when you choose.

Request permission only when the user taps:

Take photo

Record update

Scan document

Photo library

Use for:

Uploading existing pet photos

Selecting vaccination records

Adding service-proof media

Uploading profile images

Prefer a system media picker and limited selection rather than asking for unrestricted library access where platform capabilities permit it.

Disclosure

Photo access lets you select specific pet photos and care documents to upload.

Notifications

Operational notifications include:

Booking request

Assignment

Sitter en route

Service started

Report available

Payment failed

Renewal due

Emergency update

Ask after explaining the value:

Enable notifications for booking assignments, service updates, reports and urgent support messages.

Separate:

Operational notifications

Subscription reminders

Product announcements

Promotions

A user who disables marketing should still be able to receive necessary service communications through permitted channels.

Microphone and video

Keep these optional.

Use for:

Short pet-care video update

Incident evidence

Optional sitter introduction

Do not request microphone access for ordinary photo capture.

Disclosure

Microphone access is used only when you record a video or audio update.

Request the permission only after the user explicitly starts recording.

4. City-specific Phase 11 strategy

National product rule

Do not build six separate applications.

Use:

One customer app

One sitter app

One backend

One design system

↓

City-specific configuration

Localise:

Services

Prices

Languages

Landing content

Plans

Support channels

Operational radii

Society benefits

App feature flags

Google Play supports custom store listings with different names, descriptions and graphic assets for selected audiences, while Apple supports localised app information. Use these carefully for regional language or segment campaigns rather than producing separate apps.

Bengaluru

Product focus

Reliable sitter execution

Polished customer experience

Real-time walk visibility

Walking subscriptions

Explainable matching

Corporate benefit tools later

Operational features

Tight schedule-conflict detection

Peak-window capacity

Travel-time-aware matching

Preferred and backup sitter system

Strong push-notification flows

Fast self-service subscription management

Bengaluru recorded a 74.4% congestion level in TomTom’s 2025 data, supporting smaller operational zones and travel-time-aware scheduling rather than broad city-radius matching.

Release approach

Sitter beta: Whitefield/Brookefield cluster

Customer beta: subscribers and repeat users

Expand to HSR as a separate capacity zone

Corporate features only after consumer operations remain stable

Main risk

A visually polished application can increase bookings faster than the provider network can fulfil them.

Pune

Product focus

Monthly walking-plan management

Recurring sitter scheduling

Leave and backup management

Society resident benefits

Transparent value and credit use

Sitter-specific tools

Student/part-time availability

Exam and leave calendar

Restricted maximum schedules

Morning/evening commitment tracking

Backup assignment

Provider-earnings transparency

Pune’s 2025 congestion level was reported at 71.1%, with an average 10 km journey exceeding 33 minutes. The product should therefore treat Baner–Balewadi, Wakad, Kharadi and other clusters as distinct capacity zones where necessary.

Main risk

Presenting a city-wide schedule while sitters are actually concentrated in only a few apartment clusters.

Mumbai

Product focus

Hyperlocal matching

Travel-time and radius warnings

Late-arrival prediction

Same-locality caregiver preference

Building and gate instructions

At-home grooming workflows

Essential controls

Peak-time operational radius

Maximum travel-time filter

Building-access records

Automatic impossible-schedule detection

Local backup pools

Stale-location warnings

Mumbai recorded a 63.2% average congestion level in 2025, with a 10 km trip averaging nearly 29 minutes. Matching should therefore use route time and cluster boundaries—not only straight-line distance.

Main risk

A sitter who appears geographically close may still be operationally unavailable because of traffic, building entry and route direction.

Gurugram

Product focus

Verification evidence

Society gate list

Emergency Help

Premium support

Grooming/training packages

Society subscription features

Safety badge design

Show exact verification facts:

Identity checked

Documents reviewed

Practical assessment completed

Society access approved

Background check type and date

Do not show:

100% safe

Government guaranteed

Permanently verified

Society features

Time-limited gate entries

Approved sitter roster

Resident benefits

Monthly aggregate report

Event registrations

Gate-incident escalation

Main risk

Decorative badges that imply a stronger safety guarantee than the underlying verification process supports.

Ahmedabad

Product focus

App and WhatsApp hybrid

Assisted first booking

Simple 8/12-walk plans

Society events

Family-oriented reports

Hindi and Gujarati support based on measured demand

Product configuration

Prominent call/WhatsApp support

Manual-payment-link fallback

Fewer onboarding steps

Large, clear booking actions

Simple credit explanation

Human-supported subscription setup

Local-language notification templates

Ahmedabad’s 2025 congestion level was 49%, lower than Bengaluru, Pune and Mumbai but still operationally meaningful, with an average 10 km journey of approximately 29 minutes. Cluster-based scheduling remains necessary.

Main risk

Building an entirely self-service experience before local customers and providers consistently use it.

Surat

Product focus

Simple booking

Family-care updates

Pet-sitting and boarding report cards

Society event registration

Assisted support

Reliable offline sitter workflows

Product strategy

One dominant home-screen action

Starter plans rather than complex subscriptions

Strong report cards and photos

Human-supported first booking

Society/referral onboarding

Gujarati/Hindi communication where validated

Controlled boarding workflows

Treat assumptions about slower app-first adoption as hypotheses. Validate them through:

Assisted versus self-service conversion

WhatsApp-to-app activation

Repeat booking behaviour

Language selection

Support contacts per booking

Subscription conversion

Main risk

Overbuilding native functionality before sufficient repeat demand exists.

5. What goes wrong in app phases

Mistake 1: Building apps before product-market and operational fit

Wrong

Low repeat usage

Unstable city operations

Few subscriptions

↓

Large mobile investment

Correct

Build mobile products after recurring workflows exist and native capabilities solve measurable friction.

Gate

Repeat rate above 35%

Stable admin operations

Active provider network

Subscription lifecycle working

App-specific operational need documented

Mistake 2: Customer app before sitter execution

Wrong

Customers book through a polished interface, while sitters still:

Receive assignments through WhatsApp

Submit reports manually

Miss schedule changes

Lack safety escalation

Have unclear earnings

Correct

Sitter app

↓

Operational command centre

↓

Customer app

Customer trust comes from field performance, not animation quality.

Mistake 3: Fully automated AI assignment

Wrong

The model assigns high-risk pets without human review.

Correct

Hard eligibility rules

↓

Top-three recommendation

↓

Reasons and warnings

↓

Admin approval

Keep senior approval for:

High-risk pets

Medication

First boarding

Previous incidents

Multiple large pets

Unusual property access

Mistake 4: Tracking without privacy boundaries

Wrong

The sitter is tracked:

Before the service

Between bookings

During breaks

After the service

Without visible status

Correct

Track only during an explicitly started eligible service, disclose the purpose, limit access and enforce retention.

Google Play requires sensitive permissions to be necessary for disclosed current features and requested in context.

Mistake 5: Building every advanced product simultaneously

Wrong scope

Two apps

AI auto-matching

Cash wallet

Insurance

E-commerce

Corporate benefits

Social feed

Pet health diagnosis

Correct scope

Sitter execution

↓

Admin operations

↓

Customer booking and subscriptions

↓

Service credits and referrals

↓

Matching assistance

↓

Health timeline

↓

Advanced B2B features

Mistake 6: Treating store approval as a final-week task

Background location, privacy disclosures, demo accounts, screenshots and reviewer instructions need preparation before submission.

Google Play’s background-location review can require:

Permission declaration

Prominent disclosure

Privacy policy

Video demonstration

A store listing that visibly describes the core location feature.

Mistake 7: Removing the PWA immediately

The PWA should remain available for:

Customers who do not install apps

Unsupported devices

Booking during store outages

New customers

Emergency fallbacks

Search acquisition

Society-specific links

The app is an additional high-retention channel—not the only access channel.

Phase 11 launch doctrine

Release to the smallest group that can prove the next level of product reliability, then expand only after the data shows that service quality, privacy and operations remain stable.

Phase 11 launch win condition

Sitters execute most services through the mobile workflow, customers receive reliable live visibility and self-service controls, and each new city can activate the apps without increasing incidents, support burden or operational failure.

Final app-launch decision: GO—with progressive cohorts, city-level feature flags, contextual permissions, complete store disclosures and no forced migration from the web platform.

Phase 11 Final Approval Report

Mobile Apps, Advanced Product, Risks and Phase 12 Readiness 📱🐾

Executive decision

Approve Phase 11 with all five proposed rules.

The official build sequence should be:

Sitter app

↓

Operational command centre

↓

Customer app

↓

Service credits and referrals

↓

Explainable AI matching

↓

Pet health timeline and advanced features

The phase succeeds only when mobile products improve fulfilment, customer retention and operational control. Publishing two apps is not itself a successful outcome.

1. Phase 11 risk register

Corrected risk matrix

### Table 230

| Risk | Impact | Prevention | Stop condition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Development delays | High | Sitter-first scope, vertical releases, strict P0/P1 separation | Delivery repeatedly misses critical milestones |
| GPS battery drain | High | Adaptive intervals, movement detection, session-only tracking | Material battery complaints or device overheating |
| Location privacy concerns | Very high | Explicit disclosure, active-service-only tracking, limited retention | Any off-service tracking or unauthorised route access |
| Bugs during live bookings | Very high | Closed beta, feature flags, rollback and PWA fallback | Duplicate bookings, incorrect credits or failed emergency flow |
| Low sitter adoption | High | Training, simple UX, device support and gradual enforcement | Adoption remains below 80% after remediation |
| Customer app unused | Medium-high | Target repeat users and subscribers first | App performs worse than PWA without operational benefit |
| Incorrect AI recommendations | High | Hard eligibility filters and admin approval | Unsafe recommendation or systematic false exclusion |
| Credit disputes | High | Immutable ledger, visible expiry and adjustment history | Unreconciled balance or repeated duplicate consumption |
| App-store rejection | Medium-high | Early permission review, disclosures and reviewer accounts | Background location or privacy requirements remain unresolved |
| Offline-sync failure | High | Local outbox, idempotency and conflict resolution | Lost service evidence or duplicate completion |
| Push-notification failure | High | Token hygiene, in-app inbox and SMS/call fallback | Critical workflow depends on push alone |
| Security or data exposure | Very high | OWASP MASVS verification, RBAC and secure storage | Any cross-user or cross-city data exposure |
| Forced-update disruption | Medium | Graceful upgrade policy and active-service protection | Update blocks a live service |
| Mobile demand overload | High | Capacity-gated checkout and city feature flags | App growth reduces completion or punctuality |
| Third-party SDK leakage | High | SDK inventory, data-flow review and privacy declarations | Undeclared collection or sensitive-data leakage |

Risk 1: App development delays

Root causes

Building sitter and customer apps simultaneously

Adding AI, loyalty and health features before the core workflow works

Discovering GPS limitations late

Rebuilding backend logic inside the app

Treating every feature as P0

Prevention

Use four controlled release units:

Sitter service-execution vertical slice

Admin exception-management workflow

Customer booking and live-status workflow

Retention and intelligence features

The first production-quality vertical slice should cover:

Login

→ Assigned booking

→ Arrival

→ Start service

→ GPS/session evidence

→ End service

→ Report submission

→ Admin visibility

Do not begin customer loyalty or advanced AI while this flow remains unreliable.

Leading indicators

Increasing unfinished work each sprint

More than 20–25% of sprint work carried forward

Backend contracts changing after mobile development begins

Native-location prototype still unproven after Month 1

P0 defect count growing rather than shrinking

Risk 2: GPS battery drain and unreliable tracking

A single aggressive location interval is not appropriate for every service. Use adaptive collection based on movement, accuracy, battery condition and service type.

Expo supports background-location tasks through development builds, but its documentation notes that updates stop if the user terminates the app and resume when the app is reopened. The product must represent this as an interruption rather than showing a falsely continuous route.

Required controls

### Table 231

| Control | Requirement |
| --- | --- |
| Tracking period | Active eligible service only |
| Update strategy | Time- and distance-adaptive |
| Offline behaviour | Queue points locally |
| End behaviour | Stop automatically |
| App terminated | Mark tracking interrupted |
| Customer map | Display freshness and quality |
| Sitting/grooming | Arrival and check-ins, not continuous routes |
| Battery monitoring | Test real devices and OEMs |

Stop condition

Pause tracking rollout when:

Tracking continues after service completion

Battery or thermal issues materially affect devices

More than 5% of eligible sessions fail without a recovery path

Location points are silently lost

Staff cannot distinguish network loss from deliberate tracking disablement

Risk 3: Privacy concerns

The official rule must be:

Location is collected only during an explicitly started active pet-care service and is stopped when that service ends.

Google Play requires background-location access to support an important core feature, accompanied by prominent in-app disclosure, a privacy policy and an accurate permissions declaration. Apple also requires location collection to be directly relevant to app functionality and accurately disclosed.

The Indian DPDP framework requires digital personal data to be processed for lawful purposes with clear notices and appropriate safeguards. The 2025 Rules use phased commencement, so product controls should be built before wider enforcement and scale.

Mandatory safeguards

No off-duty or between-booking tracking

Visible tracking indicator

Customer access only to their booking

Admin access limited to operations and safety roles

Access audit history

Defined raw-route retention

Incident-related legal hold handled separately

Raw routes excluded from ordinary analytics

Sitter can see permission and tracking status

Privacy notice states purpose, access and retention

Approved user-facing wording

Location during active servicesYour location is collected only while you perform an eligible pet-care service. It helps verify arrival, provide service updates, document the route and support safety assistance. Tracking stops when the service is completed.

Risk 4: Bugs during real bookings

A mobile defect can cause more than inconvenience. It can affect pet custody, customer access, payment, provider earnings and incident handling.

Required protections

Internal alpha

→ Sitter closed beta

→ Customer closed beta

→ One-cluster production

→ One-city production

→ Multi-city rollout

Use independent feature flags for:

Mobile booking

GPS

Route sharing

Credit payments

AI recommendations

Emergency workflows

City and service-zone availability

Keep the PWA operational as a fallback.

Apple TestFlight supports up to 100 internal testers and up to 10,000 external testers. Google recommends moving from internal testing to a controlled closed test; certain newer personal Play developer accounts must complete a closed test with at least 12 testers opted in for 14 continuous days before applying for production access.

Release blockers

Duplicate service start or completion

Cross-customer access

Incorrect credit consumption

Incorrect payout calculation

Unrecoverable offline report

Emergency alert not delivered to the backend

Tracking outside active service

Crash during the main service flow

Missing account-deletion path

Risk 5: Low sitter adoption

The sitter app will fail if it feels like surveillance, adds unnecessary steps or works poorly on common Android devices.

Adoption strategy

### Table 232

| Stage | Approach |
| --- | --- |
| First 5–10 sitters | Guided pilot and direct observation |
| 20–50 sitters | Training plus structured feedback |
| One city | App becomes default, with support |
| Stable production | App required for eligible services |

Product principles

Three-tap access to the next booking

Large Start, Emergency and Complete actions

Offline support

Earnings transparency

Clear tracking status

No hidden scoring

Training in local languages where needed

No reliability penalty for verified app outages

No requirement for premium personal devices

Adoption metrics

Weekly active sitter adoption

Booking acknowledgement

Check-in/out completion

Report submission

Support contacts per sitter

Time required to submit a report

Device-specific failure rate

Risk 6: Customer app remains unused

A customer app should initially target:

Repeat customers

Active subscribers

Society-plan users

Customers who regularly track services

Multi-pet households

The strongest value proposition is not “we now have an app.” It is:

Faster repeat booking

Live service status

Report history

Subscription self-service

Remaining-credit visibility

Preferred-sitter management

Better support

Do not force installation. Continue to support the PWA, deep links and assisted booking.

Risk 7: Incorrect AI matching

AI matching must remain an admin decision-support tool.

Hard eligibility rules

↓

Explainable candidate ranking

↓

Top three recommendations

↓

Admin approval

↓

Sitter acceptance

The model must never override:

Expired documentation

Pet-risk incompatibility

Sitter suspension

Service-zone restrictions

Capacity limits

Society-access requirements

Schedule conflicts

Required governance

Display a match score, not an uncalibrated probability

Explain positive and negative factors

Record algorithm version

Record admin override reasons

Monitor new-sitter exposure

Audit assignment and earnings concentration

Require human approval for all assignments initially

Never auto-assign high-risk cases

Initial success targets

### Table 233

| Metric | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Top-three list includes selected sitter | 80%+ |
| Human approval | 100% |
| High-risk auto-assignments | Zero |
| False eligibility exclusions | Near zero |
| Recommendation outcome audit | Monthly |

Risk 8: Wallet and credit disputes

The early product should be called Credits, not Wallet.

Use:

Subscription service credits

Referral credits

Promotional credits

Society benefits

Service-recovery credits

Do not initially support:

Customer-funded cash storage

Transfers between users

Cash withdrawal

Open merchant usage

RBI distinguishes closed-system instruments used only for the issuer’s own services from broader prepaid instruments, and closed systems cannot be used for third-party settlement or cash withdrawal. A marketplace with independent providers should obtain specialist payments advice before introducing a cash-like stored-value product.

Ledger requirements

Every credit movement must be append-only:

GRANT

RESERVE

CONSUME

RELEASE

RESTORE

EXPIRE

REVERSE

ADMIN_ADJUSTMENT

Every transaction needs:

Idempotency key

Reason

Source booking/subscription

Actor

Expiry

Timestamp

Reversal link where applicable

Risk 9: App-store rejection

Store preparation should start while the apps are being built—not in the final week.

Common rejection areas

Background location lacks a core use case

Permission disclosure is vague

Screenshots show unavailable features

Demo account fails

Privacy policy does not match actual SDK behaviour

Account deletion is missing

App crashes or has incomplete flows

Data Safety or App Privacy declarations are inaccurate

Apple requires apps supporting account creation to let users initiate account deletion inside the app. Google Play requires an in-app deletion path and an external web resource for account-deletion requests.

Apple requires developers to disclose data collected by the application and integrated third-party partners. Google Play similarly requires accurate Data Safety declarations and may take enforcement action when app behaviour conflicts with the declaration.

Risk 10: Push notifications are treated as guaranteed delivery

Push is useful but should not be the only operational channel.

Firebase recommends storing token timestamps, refreshing tokens and removing stale or invalid registrations.

For critical events use:

Backend event

+ in-app inbox

+ push

+ operations queue

+ SMS/call fallback where necessary

Do not define “push reliability” only as messages submitted to FCM or APNs.

Measure:

Active-token coverage

Provider acceptance

Application receipt where measurable

User acknowledgement

Fallback activation

Stale-token rate

2. Phase 11 upgrade decisions

Upgrade 1: Build the sitter app before the customer app

Decision: APPROVE — NON-NEGOTIABLE

The sitter app directly improves:

Booking acknowledgement

Arrival reliability

Service evidence

GPS tracking

Report quality

Incident response

Payout transparency

The customer app should follow after the sitter workflow and command centre are operationally stable.

Upgrade 2: Use React Native with Expo

Decision: APPROVE AS THE OFFICIAL DEFAULT STACK

As of 2026, the official React Native documentation recommends using a framework such as Expo for new applications and describes Expo as a production-grade React Native framework.

Approved stack

### Table 234

| Layer | Decision |
| --- | --- |
| Mobile framework | React Native with Expo development builds |
| Language | TypeScript |
| Navigation | Expo Router |
| Backend | Existing Node.js/Next.js APIs |
| Database | PostgreSQL/PostGIS |
| Push | Expo Notifications with FCM/APNs |
| Secure token storage | Platform-backed secure storage |
| Offline queue | SQLite/local outbox |
| Error monitoring | Sentry |
| Product analytics | PostHog or Firebase with redaction |
| Build/release | EAS or equivalent CI/CD |

Condition

Run native proofs for:

Background GPS

App termination

Push deep links

Offline media

Battery impact

Android OEM behaviour

iOS background execution

Expo development builds are required for background location and other production native behaviour that Expo Go does not support.

Upgrade 3: AI remains admin-approved

Decision: APPROVE — MANDATORY FOR THE INITIAL RELEASE

AI should recommend candidates and explain the ranking. It should not autonomously assign every booking.

Human approval is especially required for:

High-risk pets

Medication

Senior or specialist care

First boarding

Previous incidents

Multiple large pets

Unusual home access

No established backup

Upgrade 4: Track location only during active service

Decision: APPROVE — NON-NEGOTIABLE

The official policy should state:

No active booking

→ no tracking

Service explicitly started

→ tracking permitted

Service completed or aborted

→ tracking stops

This must be enforced technically, not only written in the privacy policy.

Upgrade 5: Beta-first app launch

Decision: APPROVE — NON-NEGOTIABLE

Official progression:

### Table 235

| Stage | Users | Exit purpose |
| --- | --- | --- |

6. Final Phase 11 recommendation

Build:

A sitter-first mobile operating system connected to an advanced command centre, followed by a customer application for repeated booking, live service visibility, subscriptions, credits and preferred-caregiver continuity.

Do

Build sitter execution first.

Use React Native with Expo development builds.

Validate GPS on real Android and iOS devices early.

Keep AI recommendations explainable and admin-approved.

Use an immutable service-credit ledger.

Launch through controlled cohorts.

Keep the PWA operational.

Maintain kill switches and rollback controls.

Treat security and privacy testing as continuous engineering work.

Do not

Track sitters outside active services.

Build a cash-like wallet without regulatory analysis.

auto-assign high-risk bookings.

depend on push alone for emergency communication.

expose exact customer data before assignment.

publish both apps to every city simultaneously.

add social, insurance or e-commerce distractions.

call app downloads the primary success metric.

Final approval matrix

### Table 250

| Phase 11 rule | Decision |
| --- | --- |
| Build sitter app before customer app | APPROVED |
| Use React Native with Expo | APPROVED |
| AI matching remains admin-approved | APPROVED |
| Location tracking only during active service | APPROVED — NON-NEGOTIABLE |
| App launch remains beta-first | APPROVED — NON-NEGOTIABLE |

Phase 11 win condition

Sitters execute services more reliably, operations detect failures earlier, customers gain trustworthy visibility and self-service controls, and repeat users adopt the apps without increasing safety incidents, privacy exposure or financial disputes.

Phase 11 final decision

GO — with all five rules approved.

Move to Phase 12: Brand, Community and Content Engine only after the sitter app, customer app, GPS system, credit ledger, notifications and command centre pass the measurable gates above.

## Page text map

### Page 1

Phase 10: Multi-City Expansion
Controlled City Replication, Operations and Phase 11 Gate
Executive decision
Approve Phase 10 with one major condition: launch the two new cities sequentially—
not simultaneously.
Recommended six-month sequence:
Proven City
↓
City 2: controlled cluster launch
↓
City 2 reaches stability gate
↓
City 3: controlled cluster launch
↓
Network operating system validated

### Page 2

The strategic objective is not to display more city names on the website. It is to prove that
the complete marketplace system can be reproduced in another geography:
• Demand acquisition
• Sitter recruitment
• Partner verification
• Scheduling
• Subscription fulfilment
• Society partnerships
• Payments and payouts
• Incident response
• Customer support
• Positive city-level contribution
India’s broader pet-care market is expanding rapidly, but market growth does not remove
fulfilment risk. IBEF estimated the wider Indian pet-care industry at approximately ₹30,434
crore in CY2024, while projecting substantial growth through 2032. This makes expansion
attractive, but operational reliability—not market size—will determine whether a multi-city
service marketplace survives.
1. Phase 10 principle
Wrong expansion model
Launch six cities
↓
Recruit large numbers of applicants
↓
Run ads everywhere
↓
Accept bookings before local density exists
↓
Partners travel long distances
↓

### Page 3

Bookings fail
↓
Support and refunds increase
↓
Brand trust declines nationally
Correct expansion model
Select one city
↓
Select two or three micro-markets
↓
Validate demand before public launch
↓
Build verified local supply
↓
Run a closed beta
↓
Reach quality and contribution gates
↓
Expand inside the city
↓
Replicate in the next city
The actual expansion unit is not a city.
The expansion unit is a dense service cluster containing customers, societies, sitters,
backup supply and local support.
Examples:
• Bengaluru → HSR–Koramangala
• Pune → Baner–Balewadi
• Mumbai → Powai–Chandivali
• Gurugram → Golf Course Road societies

### Page 4

• Ahmedabad → Thaltej–Bodakdev
• Surat → Vesu–VIP Road

3. First-city replication readiness

### Page 5

The first city must prove that its performance comes from a repeatable system—not
founder intervention.
Minimum stability period
Require 8–12 consecutive weeks of stable performance.

Supply gate
• Active sitter pool is stable.
• Backup coverage exists by cluster.
• No single sitter controls an excessive share of fulfilment.
• Partner payouts are current.

### Page 6

• Founder is not manually assigning most bookings.

• Positive contribution per subscriber

contribution.

Active cities: 2–5
Approve as the long-term Phase 10 range.
For the first six months:
• Minimum: two active cities

Monthly bookings per city: 1,000+
This is appropriate for a mature city, but not for every newly launched city.

6. Why cluster operations are mandatory
Indian metro traffic creates substantial fulfilment risk. TomTom’s 2025 India index reported
Bengaluru at a 74.4% average congestion level, with Pune and Mumbai also among the
country’s most congested operating environments. This makes city-wide sitter pools,
cross-city assignments and broad exact-time guarantees operationally unsafe.
Correct city structure
City
├── Cluster A
│ ├── Societies
│ ├── Sitters
│ ├── Backup sitters
│ └── Local partners
├── Cluster B
│ ├── Societies
│ ├── Sitters
│ └── Local partners
└── Cluster C
A sitter should normally operate within one cluster during a service block.
Cluster launch criteria
Each launch cluster should have:
• 300–1,500 addressable pet households
• Three or more target societies or dense neighbourhood zones
• Five to ten active sitters or walkers
• Two or more backup sitters

### Page 11

• Local grooming/training partners where those services are live
• One veterinary referral network
• One operations owner
• Maximum travel-time rules
• Local service pricing
• Capacity limits by time window
7. Multi-city operating model
Central team responsibilities
The central team should own:
• Product and engineering
• Brand standards
• Verification framework
• Payment infrastructure
• Subscription architecture
• Customer-support platform
• Partner scorecard
• Incident classification
• Finance and reconciliation
• Training material
• Privacy and security standards
• National performance reporting
City team responsibilities
The local team should own:
• Sitter recruitment
• Document collection
• Practical assessments
• Society acquisition
• Local partnerships

### Page 12

• Daily fulfilment
• Backup activation
• City-specific pricing recommendations
• Local complaints
• Event operations
• Supply and capacity planning
Principle
Centralise standards and technology; decentralise fulfilment and local relationships.
Do not allow every city to invent different:
• Verification badges
• Incident definitions
• Refund policies
• Rating formulas
• Subscription-credit rules
• Pet-safety protocols
Localise only what genuinely varies:
• Price
• Travel radius
• Language
• Society access
• Partner availability
• Event format
• Customer-acquisition channel
8. City launch lifecycle
Stage 0: Market validation
Duration: two to four weeks.
Actions
• Create city waitlist page.

### Page 13

• Run small-area acquisition tests.
• Interview pet parents.
• Interview sitter candidates.
• Meet RWAs and facility managers.
• Validate price ranges.
• Map competitors.
• Assess traffic and service radii.
Gate
Proceed when:
• 100–300 qualified customer leads exist.
• 30–60 sitter/partner applicants exist.
• At least three societies show interest.
• Two target clusters are identifiable.
• Early unit economics are viable.
Stage 1: Supply build
Duration: three to six weeks.
Minimum target
Supply measure Target
Sitter applicants 30–60
Document-verified 15–30
Trial-completed 10–20
Launch-ready active sitters 8–15
Backup-capable sitters 3–5
Groomer/trainer partners 3–8 as relevant
Vet referral partners 2–5
Do not celebrate applicant count. Measure active partners who accept and complete work.

### Page 14

Stage 2: Closed beta
Duration: four to six weeks.
Audience
• Waitlisted customers
• Existing customers relocating to the city
• Friendly societies
• Employee or founder networks
• Limited invited neighbourhoods
Beta targets
• 50–150 completed bookings
• 30–75 paying customers
• 4.6+ rating
• 95%+ booking completion
• Less than 5% complaint/refund rate
• No critical incident
• Positive or improving booking contribution
Stage 3: Cluster public launch
Open:
• Specific pin codes
• Specific time windows
• Specific services
• Capacity-controlled subscriptions
Do not advertise “Now available across Mumbai” when only Powai is operational.
Use:
Available in Powai, Chandivali and selected neighbouring societies.
Stage 4: City stabilisation
Before opening a second cluster, require:

### Page 15

• 300–500 monthly bookings
• 100+ repeat customers
• 25–50 subscribers
• 4.6+ rating
• 95% fulfilment
• Positive cluster contribution
• Stable city support
• Backup pool
• No serious unresolved incident

Concentration control
Do not let one sitter become responsible for too much city revenue.
Suggested limits:
• No sitter handles more than approximately 10–15% of city bookings.

### Page 18

• Every recurring schedule has backup coverage.
• Every cluster has at least two independently viable primary providers.
• High-risk services have specialist alternatives.
These are operational safeguards rather than universal legal thresholds.
11. Demand architecture
Every city should begin with the channels most likely to generate dense, high-trust
demand.
Recommended order
1. Existing customer referrals
2. Society and apartment partnerships
3. Local veterinary/grooming referrals
4. Resident ambassadors
5. Search demand
6. Hyperlocal digital campaigns
7. Corporate pilots after fulfilment works
Do not begin with broad city advertising
Broad advertising creates:
• Scattered customers
• Long travel distances
• Low density
• High partner cancellation
• Expensive backup
• Poor contribution
Advertising should be restricted to active:
• Pin codes
• Clusters
• Time windows
• Service types

### Page 19

• Capacity levels
12. Society replication
Society relationships are city infrastructure—not merely marketing.
AWBI maintains guidance for pet owners, caregivers, RWAs and apartment associations.
The platform should therefore standardise gate protocols and resident coexistence
practices while adapting them to each property’s reasonable operational needs.
Society launch pack
Every new city should have:
• Society proposal
• 30-day pilot agreement
• Resident announcement templates
• Gate protocol
• Partner-entry roster
• Event SOP
• Complaint escalation
• Monthly report
• Data-sharing notice
• Pilot-to-paid conversion proposal

• Societies contacted
• Meetings
• Pilots
• Paid societies
• Resident leads
• Resident bookings
• Subscriptions
• Event conversion

City maturity economics
A city should not be scaled merely because GMV is increasing.

### Page 23

Scale when:
• Contribution per booking is positive.
• CAC is declining or controlled.
• Repeat rate is improving.
• Partner utilisation is healthy.
• Refund and support costs are stable.
• Fixed city costs have a credible payback path.
15. City manager scorecard
A city manager should be accountable for more than booking volume.
Dimension Metric
Demand Completed bookings and repeat customers
Supply Active providers and backup coverage
Quality Rating, complaints and incidents
Reliability Completion and on-time rates
Economics Contribution and CAC payback
Society Pilots, conversions and RWA satisfaction
People Partner retention and earnings
Compliance Documents, reporting and grievance handling
Avoid incentive distortion
Do not pay bonuses solely for:
• Number of new sitters
• GMV
• Number of city launches
• App downloads
Use a balanced city score:
City score = growth+quality+reliability+contribution+safety

### Page 24

A critical preventable safety incident should override growth bonuses.
16. Multi-city compliance system
Each city should have a compliance register covering:
• Legal entity and state registrations
• Local establishment requirements
• Partner classification
• Partner documents
• Veterinary professional controls
• Transport requirements if taxi launches
• Local event permissions
• Tax registration and invoicing
• Customer grievances
• Privacy and data access
• Insurance
• Incident reporting
Gig and platform workers
The Code on Social Security defines platform work, and a June 2026 Labour Ministry
communication states that the Social Security Rules, 2026 require aggregators to share
details of gig and platform workers electronically. A pet-care marketplace should obtain a
formal opinion on whether it is an in-scope aggregator and implement eShram
onboarding/reporting where applicable.
Privacy
The notified DPDP Rules, 2025 have phased commencement. Multi-city expansion
increases the number of employees and partners accessing addresses, recurring
schedules, pet-care instructions and health-related records, so role-based access, city-
level permissions, processing notices and audit trails should be operational before
expansion.
Consumer support
India’s E-Commerce Rules require consumer-facing grievance mechanisms. New cities
must not launch until customers have a visible escalation channel and central support
knows which local team owns each case.

### Page 25

GST and marketplace settlement
Where the platform collects customer consideration for supplies made through third-party
providers, e-commerce-operator and TCS treatment depends on the actual transaction
structure. Maintain city-, partner- and service-level ledgers and obtain a written tax position
before expanding settlement flows across states.

18. City maturity classification
Use standard stages.
Stage Definition
C0 Research Demand and supply study
C1 Pre-launch Recruitment and waitlist
C2 Closed beta Invitation-only services
C3 Cluster launch Public in limited areas
C4 Validated city Repeatable demand and positive cluster contribution
C5 Mature city 1,000+ monthly bookings with stable operations
C6 Scaled city Multiple stable clusters and local management
Cities should not be marketed internally as “launched” merely because a landing page
exists.

### Page 27

19. Main Phase 10 failure modes
Failure 1: Copying pricing without copying economics
A Mumbai price cannot automatically be used in Ahmedabad or Pune.
Correct control:
• City pricing versions
• Local partner payouts
• Travel assumptions
• Support costs
• Contribution review
Failure 2: Hiring city teams before demand
Correct control:
• Launch owner first
• Contractor/partner-led operations
• Dedicated hiring after validated volume
Failure 3: Running nationwide advertising
Correct control:
• Cluster-specific campaigns
• Capacity-aware budget
• Waitlists for inactive areas
Failure 4: Counting applicants as supply
Correct control:
Track active, accepting and completing partners.
Failure 5: Inconsistent quality standards
Correct control:
One national verification and incident framework.
Failure 6: Opening services without backups
Correct control:
No subscription or high-frequency service without cluster backup coverage.

### Page 28

Failure 7: Hiding weak cities inside network totals
Correct control:
Publish city-level retention, quality and P&L.
Failure 8: Founder becoming every city manager
Correct control:
Named local accountability and escalation ownership.
Failure 9: Expanding after GMV growth but before contribution
Correct control:
City contribution and payback gates.
Failure 10: Changing too many variables
Do not simultaneously test:
• New city
• New service
• New pricing
• New subscription
• New partner model
• New payment provider
Each city should launch with the simplest proven service stack.

21. Phase 11 go/no-go criteria
Move to Phase 11 only when all critical gates pass.
Replication gate
• At least one new city has reached C4 Validated City status.
• The city launch was not dependent on continuous founder intervention.
• The playbook produced similar quality to the original city.
• Sitter recruitment and activation are repeatable.
• Society acquisition is repeatable.
• Local support ownership is working.
Quality gate
• Every public city has a 4.6+ rating.
• Completion is 95%+.
• On-time performance is 95%+.
• Critical unresolved incidents are zero.
• Refund and dispute rate is below 5%.
Economics gate
• Original city is contribution-positive.
• At least one new city has a contribution-positive cluster.
• City CAC and payback are known.
• City P&Ls reconcile.
• Expansion is not funded through uncontrolled discounting.

### Page 31

Subscription gate
• Network has at least 200 active subscribers.
• Renewal remains 60%+.
• Recurring platform-revenue share has a credible path to 25–40%.
• Subscriptions remain within local sitter capacity.
Management gate
• Every public city has a named accountable operator.
• Mature cities have a dedicated city manager.
• Central quality and finance teams can compare cities consistently.
• Incident escalation works across city boundaries.
Compliance gate
• City compliance registers are complete.
• Partner documentation is current.
• Worker-reporting obligations have been assessed.
• Privacy access is city-scoped.
• Customer grievances have clear ownership.
• Tax and payout structures are documented.

Phase 10 Expansion Readiness and City Sequence
Executive decision
Your principle is correct:
Do not launch City 2 until City 1 functions as a repeatable operating system rather
than a founder-managed project.
However, the proposed readiness table contains several internal inconsistencies. The
biggest is this:
• 100 active walking subscribers receiving 12–20 walks each would generate
approximately 1,200–2,000 monthly service fulfilments.
• Therefore, a target of only 1,000 total paid bookings per month cannot coexist with
100 walking-plan subscribers unless “booking” means a multi-walk order rather
than an individual completed walk.
• Similarly, 50–100 sitters capable of 60–100 walks each would represent roughly
3,000–10,000 walks of theoretical monthly capacity, which is excessive for a city
completing only 1,000 walks.
Before expansion, define precisely:
Order = one commercial purchase
Booking = one scheduled service

### Page 36

Fulfilment = one completed walk/visit/session
Customer = one paying account
Subscriber = one active recurring plan
Final recommendation
• Approve the first-city stability requirement.
• Modify the subscriber, sitter and revenue thresholds.
• Reject one fixed national city order.
• Choose either a Gujarat-first or metro-first expansion thesis and follow it
deliberately.
• Add one adjacent city first, then one structurally different city to prove that the
playbook is genuinely transferable.
1. When City 1 is genuinely ready
The first city should meet readiness standards for at least three consecutive months, not
merely during one unusually strong month.

A large verified database is not the same as usable supply.

Recommended first-city readiness:
• 40–80 verified providers

If 50 active sitters each safely fulfil 60 monthly walks, the city has approximately 3,000-
walk capacity. Maintaining that supply against only 1,000 completed walks may produce
weak partner earnings and high partner churn.
Partner societies: 10–25

### Page 40

Decision: Separate live partners from pipeline
Use:
• 5–10 paid or operationally active societies
• 10–25 qualified societies in the pipeline
• At least three societies generating repeat resident bookings
• At least two societies producing positive contribution
A logo on a “partner society” page does not count unless the relationship generates:
• Resident registrations
• Paid bookings
• Events
• Subscriptions
• Operational access
• Repeat activity
AWBI maintains specific guidance covering pet owners, caregivers, RWAs and apartment
associations, so the city playbook should include standard society-access, resident-
responsibility and grievance procedures.
Monthly revenue: ₹5 lakh–₹20 lakh
Decision: Replace “revenue” with three metrics
At 1,000 bookings:
• ₹5 lakh represents an average customer payment of ₹500 per booking.
• ₹20 lakh represents an average customer payment of ₹2,000 per booking.
Both may be possible depending on whether the service mix is dominated by walking,
grooming, boarding or training. But the range is too broad to prove expansion readiness.
Track:
City GMV
Total customer spending.
Platform revenue
Commission, fees and subscription income retained by the platform.
City contribution

### Page 41

Platform revenue minus direct city costs:
• Partner payouts where applicable
• Local support
• Refunds
• Payment costs
• City acquisition
• Verification
• Local operations
• Service recovery
• Events
Expansion should require:
Positive city contribution for three consecutive months.
Not merely:
₹5 lakh or ₹20 lakh received from customers.
Published Indian pet-care estimates differ significantly depending on whether they include
food, products, healthcare and services, reinforcing why internal city economics are more
useful than headline market-size figures.
Sitter no-show below 5%
Decision: Tighten
A 5% no-show rate means five completely missed arrivals per 100 assignments, which is
too high for a trust-sensitive care marketplace.

Approved backup fulfilment 90%+
Overall completion 95%+

Gate A — Demand

4. What is wrong with one universal expansion order
The proposed list is useful as a candidate pipeline, but it should not be treated as a fixed
national sequence.
City selection depends on:
• Founder network
• Existing waitlist
• Local operations leader
• Sitter pipeline
• Society access
• Customer pricing power
• Travel friction
• Competition
• Local compliance
• Cross-city management cost
The correct next city is not necessarily the largest market.
It is:
The city with the highest probability of producing a dense, contribution-positive
launch cluster using the fewest new assumptions.
5. Current city-operating signals
Ahmedabad
Ahmedabad’s 2025 average congestion level was 49%, lower than Bengaluru, Pune,
Mumbai, Hyderabad, Chennai and Jaipur in TomTom’s current city data. That does not
eliminate travel problems, but it can make controlled route operations more manageable.
Ahmedabad Municipal Corporation also operates an official pet-dog registration system.

### Page 46

Existing dog-walking subscriptions are already publicly advertised in Ahmedabad, so the
category is not completely undeveloped; differentiation must come from local trust, better
reliability and society integration.
Best role: controlled first city or regional operating base.
Bengaluru
Bengaluru offers strong subscription-validation potential, but it is operationally difficult.
TomTom recorded a 74.4% average congestion level in 2025 and approximately 36 minutes
for a 10 km drive. Existing providers already market monthly, GPS-tracked walking
subscriptions, demonstrating both customer familiarity and active competition.
Best role: strongest digital product-market-fit test, but only through micro-market clusters.
Pune
Pune recorded 71.1% average congestion in 2025, so cross-city sitter assignment would be
risky. Current providers also advertise subscription-based walking, meaning value,
continuity and society density—not merely subscription availability—must differentiate the
offer.
Best role: balanced second metro and strong society/subscription market.
Hyderabad
Hyderabad had a 55.5% average congestion level in 2025—still significant, but lower than
Bengaluru, Pune and Mumbai. Existing providers advertise daily monthly walking
subscriptions there, indicating that the recurring-care category already has market
visibility.
Best role: should move earlier in a metro-first sequence than your original order suggests.
Gurugram
Current providers advertise monthly walking subscriptions in Gurugram. Its strongest
strategic fit is not merely walking volume, but premium society contracts, verified partner
pools and corporate-adjacent demand.
Best role: premium society and B2B validation city.
Mumbai

### Page 47

Mumbai offers high-value convenience potential, but TomTom recorded a 63.2% average
congestion level in 2025. Subscription competitors are already present, making tight
service radii and locality-level supply essential.
Best role: later premium launch after route-density and backup systems are mature.
Chennai
Walking services are currently marketed in Chennai, and Greater Chennai Corporation
operates an official pet-licensing system; it reported issuing 9,115 pet-animal licences
during 2024. Chennai also recorded 58.6% average congestion in 2025.
Best role: attractive later metro, requiring local-language operations, city-specific
licensing guidance and cluster planning.
Delhi NCR deeper expansion
The Municipal Corporation of Delhi operates an official annual pet-dog registration process
tied to vaccination validity. This reinforces the need for city-specific documentation and
resident guidance when expanding beyond Gurugram into Delhi’s municipal jurisdictions.
Best role: expand after Gurugram rather than treating all NCR jurisdictions as one
operating city.

Why Hyderabad moves ahead of Mumbai
Hyderabad currently combines visible subscription-market activity with lower measured
congestion than Bengaluru, Pune and Mumbai, making it a stronger intermediate
replication test before the most operationally difficult premium city.

• Faster testing of premium pricing
• Greater digital-service familiarity
• Larger subscription opportunity
• Stronger corporate partnership potential
• More recognisable national growth narrative

Gujarat-first strengths
• Lower management distance
• Local network leverage
• More efficient founder oversight
• Potentially lower customer-acquisition cost

Gujarat-first risks
• Lower premium pricing ceiling in some clusters

• Potentially slower subscription adoption
• Risk of mistaking regional advantage for national product-market fit
• Fewer experienced marketplace operators in some cities
8. Recommended expansion path for your situation
Choose Gujarat-first when:
• You already have strong RWA, veterinary or pet-parent relationships in Gujarat.
• A trusted Ahmedabad city manager is available.
• You can recruit walkers through local references.
• Your acquisition advantage is offline trust rather than large paid-media budgets.

### Page 52

• Capital efficiency is more important than rapid national branding.
Recommended sequence
Ahmedabad → Surat → Pune → Bengaluru → Vadodara/Rajkot
Choose metro-first when:
• You have sufficient launch capital.
• Your product and UX are already polished.
• You can hire strong city operators.
• Your strongest acquisition channels are digital, corporate and apartment
partnerships.
• You need fast validation of premium subscriptions.
Recommended sequence
Bengaluru → Pune → Hyderabad → Gurugram → Mumbai
