Phase 10 readiness principle
Do not expand because City 1 is large. Expand because City 1 can run reliably without
extraordinary founder intervention, and because City 2 has enough local demand,
supply and ownership to reproduce the operating model.
The real City 2 launch gate is:
Stable demand+reliable supply+positive contribution+documented systems
+local accountability
Phase 10 expansion readiness: GO only after the corrected three-month stability gates
pass.
Phase 10 City Launch Gate System
End-to-End Validation, Supply Build, Soft Launch and City-Scale Playbook
Executive verdict
Approve the gated city-launch model, but strengthen it before using it operationally.
Your four gates are directionally correct, but the current version has three weaknesses:
1. It measures too many vanity inputs—waitlist sign-ups and sitter applications—
without proving that they will convert into paying demand and active supply.
2. The proposed launch-quality thresholds are too weak. A 90% completion rate and
5–10% sitter no-show rate are not acceptable for a trust-sensitive pet-care
marketplace.
3. It omits explicit gates for economics, safety, compliance, privacy and launch
capacity.
The corrected sequence should be:
Gate 0: City selection and compliance
↓
Gate 1: Qualified demand
↓

### Page 58

Gate 2: Active supply and backup capacity
↓
Gate 3: Operational readiness
↓
Gate 4: Closed-beta quality
↓
Gate 5: Cluster economics and repeat demand
↓
Gate 6: Public city expansion
The operating rule is:
A city is not launched because its landing page is live. It is launched only when
customers can book, providers can fulfil, support can intervene, incidents can be
managed and the city can generate positive contribution.
1. How the gate system should work
Gates must be sequential and blocking.
A city cannot compensate for weak supply with strong demand. It cannot compensate for
poor service quality with high revenue. It cannot compensate for positive economics with
unresolved safety incidents.
Each gate should produce one of three decisions:
Decision Meaning
Pass All critical thresholds met; proceed
Conditional pass Minor gaps exist with a dated corrective plan
Fail/Hold Do not proceed; repair the failed gate
Critical gate rule
The following cannot receive a conditional pass:
• No accountable local operator
• No emergency escalation process
• No usable backup provider pool

### Page 59

• Critical safety incident unresolved
• Payment or refund process not working
• Customer support unavailable
• Required local compliance not assessed
• Negative contribution with no credible correction
• Serious privacy or access-control weakness
2. Gate 0: City selection and compliance
This gate should occur before you spend materially on recruitment, marketing or city hiring.
Objective
Determine whether the city is strategically attractive and legally/operationally launchable.
Required city-selection score
Score the candidate city across:
Factor Weight
Qualified customer demand 15
Founder/local network 15
Society/apartment density 12
Sitter recruitment potential 12
Local city operator availability 10
Expected contribution economics 10
Travel and cluster feasibility 8
Local veterinary/grooming ecosystem 6
Competitive intensity 5
Compliance readiness 4
Language/support readiness 3
Total 100
Decision bands

### Page 60

Score Decision
80–100 Priority launch candidate
70–79 Continue targeted validation
60–69 Run low-cost demand/supply experiments
Below 60 Defer
Compliance checklist
Before Gate 1, identify:
• Relevant municipal pet-registration or licensing processes
• State and local business requirements
• Service-provider documentation
• Veterinary professional boundaries
• Transport requirements if pet taxi is contemplated
• GST and marketplace settlement structure
• Customer grievance responsibilities
• Gig/platform-worker reporting obligations
• Privacy notices and city-level data access
Local requirements can vary materially. For example, Delhi, Ahmedabad and Chennai each
maintain their own municipal pet-registration or licensing systems, so the city playbook
must include a local compliance map rather than assuming one national process.
If the marketplace falls within India’s aggregator framework, current Social Security Rules
and Labour Ministry directions require attention to platform-worker details and eShram
onboarding/reporting. This should be evaluated before the provider network scales.
Gate 0 output
City Opportunity and Compliance Memo
• City score
• Recommended launch clusters
• Competitive map
• Local price hypothesis
• Operating-cost hypothesis

### Page 61

• Local compliance register
• Named launch owner
• 90-day city P&L scenario
• Go/hold recommendation
3. Gate 1: Qualified demand
Your proposed demand gate is useful, but the metrics need stronger definitions.
Corrected demand targets
Metric Recommended target
Qualified waitlist leads 150–300
Structured pet-parent interviews 30–50
Society meetings with decision-makers 5–10
Societies willing to pilot 2–5
Paid trial bookings or refundable deposits 20–40
Validated target clusters At least 2
Price acceptance Demonstrated
Search/social demand Demonstrated, not assumed
Why 30–50 interviews may be better than 100
One hundred informal conversations can produce less information than 30 carefully
structured interviews.
Each interview should capture:
• Current care arrangement
• Monthly service frequency
• Existing spend
• Main pain point
• Preferred schedule
• Service area
• Trust objections

### Page 62

• Willingness to trial
• Willingness to subscribe
• Preferred payment method
• Society restrictions
• Pet size, age and behaviour
Continue interviewing until new conversations stop producing materially new insights.
Defining a qualified waitlist lead
A qualified lead should include:
• Valid phone or email
• Confirmed city and area
• Pet profile
• Desired service
• Expected frequency
• Preferred time window
• Indicative budget
• Consent to receive launch communication
Do not count:
• Social-media likes
• Unverified giveaway entries
• People outside the service area
• Pet lovers who do not require care
• Duplicate contacts
• Event visitors with no booking intent
Demand quality formula
qualified service-intent leads
Qualified lead rate =
total waitlist sign-ups
A 200-person waitlist with 30 qualified leads is weaker than a 100-person waitlist with 70
qualified leads.

### Page 63

Society-interest definition
“Five interested societies” is too vague.
Classify societies:
Stage Meaning
S0 Identified
S1 Contacted
S2 Decision-maker meeting completed
S3 Resident-demand evidence shared
S4 Pilot verbally approved
S5 Pilot agreement signed
S6 Pilot active
Gate 1 should require:
• Five or more decision-maker meetings
• Two or more pilot-ready societies
• At least one society in each target cluster
AWBI guidance specifically addresses pet owners, caregivers, RWAs and apartment
associations, so society outreach should incorporate responsible coexistence, resident
duties and workable access procedures—not just discounts and sales offers.
Paid pre-bookings and deposits
Paid intent is stronger than survey intent, but deposits must be handled transparently.
Use:
• Refundable launch deposit
• Paid discounted trial
• Prepaid starter pack activated only after launch
• Clearly disclosed expiry and refund terms
Do not:

### Page 64

• Hold deposits indefinitely
• Hide geographic restrictions
• Convert deposits into subscriptions automatically
• Represent deposits as completed revenue before service is delivered
India’s E-Commerce Rules require appropriate consumer information, fair practices and an
adequate grievance mechanism. Deposit, refund and cancellation terms should therefore
be shown before payment.
Proving search and social demand
“Search/social demand proven” should mean at least three of the following:
• City landing page generates qualified leads
• Search advertisements convert within target CAC
• Organic local search impressions exist
• Society announcements produce registrations
• Local referral campaigns generate trial interest
• Retargeting produces booking-intent actions
• Price-test pages receive checkout starts
• Customers accept trial deposits
Gate 1 output
Demand Validation Report
• Leads by cluster
• Lead qualification rate
• Interview findings
• Price acceptance
• Society pipeline
• Paid-intent count
• Expected first 90-day demand
• Acquisition-cost scenario
• Pass/hold recommendation

### Page 65

4. Gate 2: Supply and backup capacity
The proposed target of 100 sitter applications is not a useful success measure by itself.
Applications are top-of-funnel activity. The city needs approved, active and schedulable
providers.
Corrected supply targets
Supply category Recommended launch target
Sitter/walker applications 50–100
Contact-screened 30–60
Documents verified 15–30
Practical trial completed 10–20
Launch-ready active providers 8–15
Backup-capable providers 3–5
Grooming partners 3–5
Veterinary referral partners 2–5
Trainers 2–4 if training launches
Boarding providers 3–5 if boarding launches
Do not onboard ten service categories simply to make the provider list appear large.
Launch the smallest proven service stack:
1. Walking
2. Sitting where supply is strong
3. Grooming through verified partners
4. Veterinary coordination/referral
5. Boarding only after facility/host controls pass
Provider funnel
Applicant
↓

### Page 66

Contact screened
↓
Identity/documents checked
↓
Skill and behaviour assessment
↓
Practical trial
↓
Cluster assigned
↓
Capacity declared
↓

Active supply definition
A provider counts as active only if they:
• Have current documents
• Have declared usable availability
• Operate inside the launch cluster
• Are willing to accept the planned pricing
• Have passed relevant trial requirements
• Have completed or accepted work recently
• Are not paused or under investigation
Backup sitter requirement
“Five backup sitters” is useful only when they match the actual demand.
Backup capacity must be mapped by:
• Area

### Page 67

• Day
• Time window
• Service type
• Pet size/handling requirements
• Gender preference where legitimately relevant
• Society access
• Travel radius
Five available afternoon walkers do not protect 20 subscriptions scheduled at 7:30 AM.
Backup coverage formula
recurring schedules with usable backup
Backup coverage =
total recurring schedules
Launch target:
90–95%+ backup coverage before selling recurring plans.
Grooming and vet partner target
The proposed combined “10+ grooming/vet partners” should be split.
Veterinary partners
Start with:
• Two general clinics
• One after-hours/emergency option where available
• One home-visit or teleconsult option where appropriate
• Registration and professional details checked
The platform should coordinate care rather than diagnose or prescribe.
Grooming partners
Start with:
• Three to five launch-ready groomers
• Defined package prices
• Hygiene and handling checklist

### Page 68

• Before/after reporting
• Backup arrangements
Ten weak or inactive partners create less value than four reliable ones.
Boarding host requirement
Boarding introduces materially higher custody and safety risk.
Before counting a host as launch-ready, verify:
• Facility/home inspection
• Pet capacity
• Separation rules
• Escape prevention
• Vaccination policy
• Emergency contact
• Trial process
• Resident/society permission where relevant
• Incident and evacuation process
If boarding controls are incomplete, launch the city without boarding.

• Document expiry risks

Support readiness

### Page 71

Define SLAs before launch.
Case type Initial response target
Normal booking question Within published business-hour SLA
Walker late/unreachable 10–15 minutes
Service cannot be fulfilled Immediate operational escalation
Pet lost/escaped Immediate critical escalation
Injury or bite Immediate escalation
Payment/refund issue Same day or defined finance SLA
Privacy/security issue Immediate compliance escalation
Consumer grievance access must remain available regardless of customer tier or
subscription status.
Incident readiness drill
Before soft launch, run tabletop exercises for:
• Sitter no-show
• Pet escape
• Dog bite
• Pet injury
• Customer lock/access problem
• Society gate denial
• Provider fraud
• Payment captured but booking not created
• Personal data sent to the wrong provider
• Emergency clinic unavailable
A city is not operationally ready merely because an SOP document exists. The team must
demonstrate that it can execute it.
Privacy and access

### Page 72

City expansion increases the number of people accessing:
• Addresses
• Access instructions
• Recurring customer schedules
• Pet behaviour details
• Medical-related notes
• Payment and contact information
The DPDP Act and notified 2025 Rules establish a framework for lawful-purpose
processing, notices, safeguards and grievance handling, with provisions commencing on a
phased timetable. Build city-scoped access and auditability now rather than retrofitting it
after national expansion.

6. Gate 4: Closed-beta launch
The original launch thresholds should be strengthened.
Recommended beta targets
Metric Corrected target
Paying beta customers 30–75
Completed paid services 50–150
Average rating 4.6+

### Page 73

Metric Corrected target
Completion rate 95%+
On-time rate 95%+
Provider no-show Below 1%
Provider cancellation Below 5%
Refund/dispute rate Below 5%
Critical preventable incidents Zero
Support SLA achievement 90%+
Provider report completion 95%+
Why 90% completion is too low
A 90% rate means that one in ten promised services fails to complete.
For 1,000 monthly services, that would mean 100 failures. A pet-care company should not
scale a city with that failure profile.
Why 5–10% no-show is unacceptable
A no-show is not a minor schedule change. It means the provider fails to arrive without
adequate handling.
Recommended distinction:
Metric Target
No-show Below 1%
Provider cancellation Below 5%
Cancellation covered by backup 90%+
Overall completion 95%+
Beta cohort
Use:
• Existing waitlist
• Referred customers

• Pilot societies
• Friends-of-customer networks
• Limited employer/community partners
Do not begin with city-wide paid advertising.
Beta geography
Open only:
• One or two clusters
• Supported pin codes
• Defined time windows
• Proven services
Use accurate language:
“Available in Baner, Balewadi and selected Wakad societies.”
Not:
“Now available across Pune.”

A successful beta does not automatically justify city-wide expansion.
The city must prove repeat demand and workable economics.
Validation targets
Metric Target

City financial model

GMV
Total customer spending.
Platform revenue

Direct city costs

City contribution
City contribution = platform city revenue−direct city operating costs
Do not scale based only on GMV.

Result Decision

Demand strong, supply weak Pause acquisition and recruit
Demand weak, supply strong Reduce provider pool and improve acquisition
Complaints high Freeze growth
Critical incident unresolved Stop city expansion
One cluster positive, another weak Scale only the positive cluster

Final city-launch principle
Do not launch a city when marketing is ready. Launch it when demand, active supply,

through the documented playbook, without founder firefighting or deterioration in
safety and customer trust.

### Page 87

Phase 10 City Operating System
Team Structure, City-Manager Scorecard, P&L, Unit Economics and Cluster Expansion
Executive verdict
Your city operating model is directionally correct, but it should be organised by city
maturity, not copied as a fixed seven-person team into every new market.
The recommended operating principle is:

### Page 88

Start with one accountable city leader and a small cross-functional launch pod. Add
specialised roles only when booking volume, support load and society activity justify
them.
The expansion hierarchy should be:
City
↓
Service cluster
↓
Society/locality
↓
Primary provider pool
↓
Backup provider pool
↓
Customer and subscription capacity
A city should never be opened merely because providers have applied. It should open only
when the target cluster has:
• Qualified demand
• Active, schedulable supply
• Backup coverage
• Local operational ownership
• Emergency escalation
• Positive projected unit economics
• Capacity for the services being advertised
Part I — City Team Structure
1. Review of the proposed team
Role Proposed count Decision
City Manager 1 Mandatory before public beta

### Page 89

Role Proposed count Decision
Sitter Onboarding Executive 1–2 Add according to recruitment volume
Customer Support Executive 1–2 Can initially be shared centrally
Society Partnership Executive 1 Add when the society pipeline is active
Operations Coordinator 1 Highly important from beta onward
Vet/Groomer Partner Manager Shared/part-time Appropriate initially
Marketing Executive Shared/part-time Appropriate initially
Safety/Quality owner Not listed Add as a central or regional function
Finance/reconciliation Not listed Keep central, with city-level reporting
The city manager must own city outcomes, but should not personally execute every
recruitment call, booking reassignment, complaint, event and financial reconciliation.
That creates a founder-style bottleneck inside each city.

3. Shared versus city-local functions
Keep central
• Product and engineering
• Payment infrastructure

• Tax policy
• Legal and compliance

• Practical trials
• Availability and capacity

### Page 93

• Live schedule management
• Backup activation
• Local event delivery
• Local partner relationships
• Customer-service recovery requiring physical intervention
Hybrid
• Customer support
• Partner training
• Marketing
• Veterinary/grooming partnership management
• Quality assurance
The operating rule is:
Centralise systems and standards; localise relationships and fulfilment.
Part II — City Manager Responsibilities
4. What the city manager should own
The city manager should be accountable for:
Demand
• Monthly completed bookings
• Paying customers
• Repeat customers
• Subscription adoption
• Society conversion
• Area expansion
Supply
• Active walker and sitter capacity
• Backup coverage
• Provider activation
• Partner retention

### Page 94

• Provider earnings health
• Document validity
Quality
• Booking completion
• On-time service
• Customer ratings
• Complaints
• Refunds
• Report completion
• Primary/backup continuity
Economics
• City GMV
• Platform revenue
• Direct contribution
• Acquisition cost
• Support cost
• Refund cost
• Society economics
• Cluster profitability
Safety
• Incident escalation
• Provider suspension
• Evidence preservation
• Root-cause reviews
• Corrective actions
• Emergency network readiness
Team
• Hiring
• Training

### Page 95

• Performance
• Shift coverage
• Operational discipline
5. What the city manager should not own alone
The city manager should not have unilateral authority to:
• Approve their own unsafe provider exception
• Delete or downgrade incident records
• Override expired documentation
• Hide refunds to improve city metrics
• Change clinical veterinary decisions
• Settle serious complaints without review
• Modify recognised revenue
• Access unnecessary customer medical information
• approve their own bonus score
Critical safety, compliance and financial controls need independent review.
Veterinary clinical decisions must remain with registered veterinary professionals. VCI
professional regulations also prohibit veterinarians from offering or receiving commissions
for procuring or referring clients, so city managers should not build veterinary revenue
around ordinary lead commissions. Use transparent coordination, event-management or
software fees subject to legal review.
6. City manager RACI
Workstream City Manager Central Team
Sitter recruitment Accountable Standards/support
Verification Operationally responsible Policy and audit
Booking fulfilment Accountable Product tools
Customer support Escalation owner First-line/shared
Pricing recommendation Consulted/responsible Final approval

Workstream City Manager Central Team
City P&L Accountable Finance validates
Safety incidents Immediate owner Independent review
Veterinary services Coordination only Clinical boundaries
Privacy Local compliance Data-protection owner
Tax and payouts Provides inputs Finance owns
Society agreements Accountable Legal templates
Part III — City Manager Scorecard
7. Why the proposed scorecard needs modification
The original metrics are useful, but three corrections are needed:
1. Revenue growth cannot override poor quality or safety.
2. “Incident rate below 3%” is too broad.
3. Gross margin must have a defined denominator.
Use a balanced scorecard with hard safety overrides.
8. Recommended weighted scorecard
Dimension Weight
Quality and safety 30%
Supply and fulfilment 20%
Demand and retention 20%

9. Hard scorecard overrides
A manager should not qualify for a growth bonus when any of these applies:
• Critical preventable incident
• Concealed safety event
• Expired provider credentials used knowingly
• Partner payouts materially delayed
• City P&L unreconciled
• Severe privacy breach
• Customer grievance process bypassed
• Systematic false completion reporting
Part IV — City P&L Structure
10. First separate GMV from revenue
A marketplace must distinguish:
GMV
Total customer spending through the city.
Platform revenue
The amount the platform recognises from:
• Commissions
• Service fees
• Subscription fees
• Society-management fees
• Event-management fees
• Software fees
Partner payouts

### Page 100

Amounts owed to walkers, sitters, groomers or other providers.
Depending on whether the company acts as principal or agent, recognised revenue may be
gross or net. Obtain a written accounting position rather than deciding this only through
product terminology.

12. City contribution levels
Contribution 1
C1 = Platform revenue−direct service and payment costs
Contribution 2

### Page 103

C2 = C1−variable city operations
City operating contribution
City operating contribution = C2−local fixed costs
Fully allocated city result
City result = City operating contribution−allocated central overhead
Use each level for a different decision:
Metric Decision use
C1 Is the individual transaction viable?
C2 Is the cluster operationally viable?
City contribution Can the local city operation sustain itself?
Fully allocated result Can the company scale profitably?
13. Tax and settlement controls
If the platform collects customer consideration for services supplied by third-party
providers, e-commerce-operator and TCS obligations may apply depending on the exact
transaction structure. Maintain separate service, provider and city settlement ledgers, and
obtain a written CA/GST position before scaling across states.
Tax payable is not ordinary operating revenue or contribution. Keep:
• GST collected
• TCS
• Provider settlements
• Refund liabilities
• Subscription deferred obligations
separate from commercial margin reporting.

Part VI — Area Expansion Inside a City
18. The city is not the launch unit
The correct expansion model is:
Cluster 1
↓
Cluster 1 passes quality and contribution gates
↓
Adjacent Cluster 2
↓
Cluster 2 passes gates

### Page 107

↓
Cluster 3
↓
Multi-cluster city operation
Cluster boundaries should be based on real travel time, not only postal codes or
neighbourhood names.
19. Why travel-time clusters matter
TomTom’s 2025 data reports average congestion of approximately 74.4% for Bengaluru and
49% for Ahmedabad. Bengaluru therefore requires tighter operating radii and stronger
peak-time capacity controls than Ahmedabad.
This is an operational inference:
• An Ahmedabad cluster may sometimes cover several adjacent residential areas.
• A Bengaluru cluster that appears close on a map may still be unworkable during
morning and evening peaks.
• Walker routes should be defined using measured service-to-service travel times.
20. Ahmedabad cluster review
Proposed sequence
1. Bopal, South Bopal, Ambli
2. Satellite, Prahlad Nagar, SG Highway
3. Vastrapur, Thaltej, Bodakdev
4. Wider Ahmedabad
Assessment
This is a credible planning hypothesis, but the areas should be split or combined based on:
• Provider home locations
• Society density
• Peak-hour travel
• Gate-entry times
• Customer time windows

### Page 108

• Local pricing
• Emergency-vet coverage
Recommended Ahmedabad structure
Cluster A — Bopal corridor
• Bopal
• South Bopal
• Ambli
• Nearby societies within the validated travel radius
Cluster B — Prahlad–Satellite corridor
• Prahlad Nagar
• Satellite
• Selected SG Highway societies
Cluster C — West-central premium corridor
• Vastrapur
• Bodakdev
• Thaltej
Do not open “wider Ahmedabad” as one final step. Continue adding smaller operational
zones.
Ahmedabad’s 2025 average congestion was lower than Bengaluru’s, but the city still
averaged close to 29 minutes for a 10 km drive, so route design remains material.
21. Bengaluru cluster review
Proposed sequence
1. Whitefield, Brookefield, Kundalahalli
2. HSR, Sarjapur Road, Bellandur
3. Indiranagar, Koramangala
4. Wider Bengaluru
Assessment
The first cluster is directionally sensible because the areas are comparatively adjacent.

### Page 109

The second proposed cluster may be too broad during peak periods. HSR, Bellandur and
different sections of Sarjapur Road may need to operate as separate capacity zones.
Recommended Bengaluru structure
Cluster A — Whitefield micro-market
• Whitefield
• Brookefield
• Kundalahalli
• Selected nearby societies
Cluster B1 — HSR
• HSR Layout
• Adjacent serviceable societies
• Separate peak-time provider pool
Cluster B2 — Bellandur/Sarjapur corridor
• Bellandur
• Selected Sarjapur Road communities
• Strict time-window and route rules
Cluster C1 — Koramangala
Cluster C2 — Indiranagar
Merge C1 and C2 operationally only when travel-time and provider-density data support it.
Bengaluru’s high measured congestion makes broad city-wide primary/backup pools
particularly risky.

• Irrelevant if the walkers are unavailable during peak times

29. Cluster-expansion gate
Open the next adjacent cluster only when the current cluster has:
• 300–500 monthly completed services
• 100–200 monthly paying customers
• 25–40% repeat rate
• Positive direct contribution
• Capacity utilisation below 85%
• Peak utilisation below 90%
• 90–95% backup coverage
• No critical unresolved incident
• Local operational owner
• Stable provider earnings
Do not use unused central-city provider capacity as evidence that a distant new cluster is
ready.
Part VIII — Compliance Controls for City Teams
30. Gig and platform-worker operations
The Code on Social Security recognises gig and platform workers, and the Ministry of
Labour’s June 2026 direction states that aggregators covered by the 2026 Rules must share
required worker details through the eShram framework. A pet-care marketplace should
obtain a formal classification opinion and build compliant provider-registration and
reporting processes if it is in scope.

### Page 115

City onboarding should therefore maintain:
• Provider identity
• Engagement dates
• Active/inactive status
• Earnings and payouts
• Government registration identifiers where applicable
• Document expiry
• Reporting-sync status
31. Data access
The DPDP Rules, 2025 have phased commencement. City expansion increases access to
recurring schedules, home addresses, gate instructions, pet records and payment
information, so city-specific role controls should be implemented before scaling.
Recommended access:
Role Access
Walker Assigned booking and necessary pet instructions
Operations Scheduling and fulfilment
Support Customer-service and booking data
City Manager Aggregated city controls and escalations
Society Executive Society relationship data, not medical records
Marketing Aggregated campaign data
Finance Payments and settlements
RWA Aggregate society reports only

Phase 10 City Win Condition
Each city and cluster has enough demand, active supply, backup capacity, local
accountability and positive contribution to operate without founder firefighting or
degradation in safety and customer trust.
City Team, P&L and Cluster Expansion System: APPROVED—with stage-based staffing,
severity-based safety gates and capacity measured by area and time window rather
than provider headcount alone.
Phase 10 Expansion Technology Architecture
Multi-City Configuration, Database, APIs and Command Centres
Executive verdict
Your proposed features are correct, but the current schema is too city-centric and too
static for production multi-city operations.
A reliable architecture needs six layers:
Country
↓
City
↓
Service zone / cluster
↓

### Page 118

Society or locality
↓
Service configuration and capacity
↓
Bookings, partners, pricing and P&L
The critical design rule is:
City should be a first-class operational boundary, while the service zone should be the
actual booking, pricing and capacity boundary.
A service may be available in Bengaluru without being available throughout Bengaluru.
Similarly, Whitefield may have walking capacity at 7:00 AM but no boarding supply or
evening backup.
1. Core multi-city architecture
Every city must independently configure:
• Launch stage
• Public availability
• Service zones
• Services offered
• Prices and partner payouts
• Operating hours
• Sitter capacity
• Support contacts
• Emergency veterinary directory
• Notification templates
• Society pipeline
• Staff access
• Subscriptions
• Financial attribution
• Incident ownership
The booking flow should become:

### Page 119

Customer enters address
↓
Resolve city and service zone
↓
Check whether service is active
↓
Check pet eligibility
↓
Check local price version
↓
Check partner and backup capacity
↓
Display available slots
↓
Create booking
↓
Attribute revenue, cost and incident ownership to city
Do not let customers select an arbitrary city from a dropdown and then assume the service
is available.
2. City and area management
What the feature must do
City management should control:
• Whether the city is visible publicly
• Whether it accepts waitlist registrations
• Whether it accepts paid bookings
• Which clusters are active
• Which services are enabled
• Which payment and support workflows apply

### Page 120

• Which staff can access the city

PAUSED
EXITED
Do not use a simple Boolean such as:
is_active = true
That cannot distinguish:
• A city collecting leads

• A city taking bookings in selected areas
• A temporarily paused city
• A fully mature operation
Recommended zone states
RESEARCH

BETA

ACTIVE
CAPACITY_PAUSED
SERVICE_PAUSED

3. Geography model
Problem with the proposed areas table
This is too limited:
areas
- city_id
- name
- pincode
A neighbourhood can contain multiple postcodes, and a postcode may not precisely
match your operational service boundary.
Use geographic polygons or multipolygons for operational zones.
Recommended model
service_zones
- id
- city_id
- code
- name
- slug
- boundary
- centroid
- status
- launch_stage
- priority
- timezone
- created_at

service_zone_pincodes

### Page 122

- service_zone_id
- pincode
Use boundary for accurate booking eligibility and pincodes for customer search, SEO and
fallback matching.
PostGIS provides geographic data types and functions such as ST_DWithin; when
geography values are used, distance is measured in metres. That makes it suitable for local
service-radius checks.
Example radius matching
SELECT p.id
FROM partners p
JOIN partner_service_areas psa
ON psa.partner_id = p.id
WHERE psa.service_type = 'DOG_WALK'
AND psa.status = 'ACTIVE'
AND ST_DWithin(
psa.origin,
:booking_location,
psa.max_radius_meters
);
Distance is only the first filter. Final matching must also validate:
• Availability
• Time window
• Pet size and behaviour
• Required certification or permission
• Society access
• Travel time
• Existing workload
• Backup capacity

Important principle
A sitter’s service radius is not constant throughout the day.
For example:
• 2 km during the 7:00–9:00 AM peak
• 5 km during the afternoon
• Same-society bookings only during high-density blocks
Your architecture should therefore support both:
• Geographic radius
• Time-window-specific operational radius

Access principle
A Pune city operations user should not automatically access:
• Mumbai customer addresses
• Ahmedabad financial data
• Bengaluru partner documents
• National incident investigations
PostgreSQL supports row-level security policies that restrict which rows a role can read or
modify. RLS should supplement—rather than replace—application-level authorisation.
Example security context
SET LOCAL app.user_id = '...';
SET LOCAL app.city_ids = '{city-uuid}';
Then apply policies to scoped tables such as:
• Bookings
• Partners
• Societies
• Incidents
• Subscription records
• City financial snapshots
Do not use database-owner or BYPASSRLS credentials for ordinary application requests.

14. Materialized dashboard views
National dashboards should not continuously aggregate millions of raw booking and
payment rows.
Use:

### Page 141

• Operational queries for today’s live bookings
• Materialized views or analytics tables for historical city metrics
• A finance ledger for reconciled revenue
• A separate incident source for safety metrics
PostgreSQL materialized views persist query results and can later be refreshed. A
concurrent refresh allows reads to continue, but it requires an appropriate unique index on
the materialized view.
Suggested views
mv_city_daily_operations
mv_city_monthly_financials
mv_city_partner_reliability
mv_city_subscription_metrics
mv_city_society_pipeline
mv_national_city_scorecard
Refresh cadence
View Refresh
Today’s bookings Real time or near real time
Capacity Every few minutes
Failed payments Near real time
City quality Hourly
Subscription MRR Hourly/daily
Financial P&L Daily after reconciliation
Monthly scorecard Daily until month close
Every widget should display its last refresh timestamp.
15. Index strategy
Typical high-value indexes include:
CREATE INDEX bookings_city_status_time_idx

### Page 142

ON bookings (city_id, status, scheduled_at);
CREATE INDEX bookings_zone_date_idx
ON bookings (service_zone_id, scheduled_at);
CREATE INDEX partner_service_area_geo_idx
ON partner_service_areas
USING GIST (origin);
CREATE INDEX active_price_items_idx
ON price_book_items (price_book_id, service_package_id)
WHERE is_active = true;
PostgreSQL supports multicolumn indexes and partial indexes, but the column order and
predicate should match real query patterns. Indexes improve reads while adding write and
maintenance overhead, so they should be driven by observed workloads.
Use pg_stat_statements to identify expensive and frequent queries rather than guessing
every index in advance.
16. Table partitioning
Do not create one physical bookings table per city.
Begin with a shared bookings table containing:
city_id
service_zone_id
scheduled_at
When the volume justifies partitioning, append-heavy tables are generally better
partitioned by time, such as month, rather than by a growing and uneven set of cities.
Good partition candidates:
• Bookings
• Notification events
• Audit logs
• Payment events
• Capacity snapshots
• Location events

### Page 143

PostgreSQL supports declarative partitioning and partition pruning; however, partitioning
adds operational complexity and should be introduced only when row volume and query
behaviour justify it.

18. API security rules
Every write endpoint should support:
• Authentication
• City and zone authorisation
• Input validation

### Page 146

• Idempotency keys
• Audit records
• Version or concurrency checks
• Explicit transition validation
Example
A city manager may:
• Assign a backup sitter
• Pause sales in their zone
• Add a service-recovery credit within a limit
They should not necessarily be allowed to:
• Publish national prices
• Edit another city
• Delete an incident
• Override veterinary credentials
• Mark an unpaid invoice paid
• Change recognised finance data
PostgreSQL privileges and RLS provide database-enforced layers for these scopes.

23. Privacy and city-level data isolation
Multi-city operations increase access to:
• Customer addresses
• Recurring schedules
• Gate instructions
• Pet behaviour
• Medical-related records
• Payment references
• Sitter identity documents

### Page 153

The notified Digital Personal Data Protection Rules, 2025 have phased commencement.
The architecture should nevertheless implement city-scoped access, purpose-specific
processing, security controls and grievance handling before expansion.
Minimum controls
• Role-based permissions
• Row-level city scoping
• Field-level restrictions
• Audit logs
• Secure exports
• Time-limited temporary access
• No individual data in RWA dashboards
• No cross-city customer browsing
• Redacted analytics
• Data-retention jobs
Example
A society partnership executive may see:
• Society account
• Meeting notes
• Aggregate resident registrations
• Event conversions
They should not see:
• Customer health records
• Home-access codes
• Payment-card details
• Detailed pet incident reports unrelated to the society relationship

25. Production acceptance tests
Before City 2 opens, test:
1. Customer address resolves to the correct city and zone.
2. Unsupported address receives waitlist—not checkout.
3. Walking can be active while boarding remains disabled.
4. City price differs without affecting existing bookings.
5. Existing booking retains its historical price.
6. Partner outside the radius is excluded.
7. Partner in the radius but unavailable is excluded.
8. Peak slot closes when capacity reaches the limit.
9. Backup capacity remains reserved.
10. City manager cannot access another city.
11. National admin can access all authorised cities.
12. RLS blocks direct cross-city database reads.
13. City landing page reflects service configuration.

### Page 156

14. Paused service disappears from checkout.
15. Society is attributed to the correct city and zone.
16. Booking revenue reaches the correct city P&L.
17. Refund updates contribution metrics correctly.
18. Incident routes to the correct city owner.
19. Dashboard refresh shows a freshness timestamp.
20. City closure disables new bookings without deleting historical data.

Phase 10 technology win condition
The platform can activate, price, staff, monitor, pause and financially isolate every city
and service zone without copying code, exposing cross-city data or manually
rebuilding operations for each launch.
Multi-City Technology Architecture: GO—with service-zone geography, versioned price
books, city-scoped access, capacity-enforced checkout and independent city P&Ls.
Phase 10 Multi-City Brand Strategy
One National Brand, Six Local Market Playbooks
Executive verdict
Approve the national-master-brand model with city-specific positioning.
Do not create six independent brands. That would fragment:
• Trust and reviews
• Verification standards
• Product development
• Support systems
• Search authority

### Page 159

• Safety reputation
• Marketing expenditure
Use one national promise:
Verified local pet care, documented at every visit.
Then localise five elements for each city:
1. Customer problem
2. Flagship product
3. Operational service area
4. Pricing and offer
5. Local trust evidence
India’s pet-care category continues to expand as pets are increasingly treated as family
members and spending shifts toward professional services, digital discovery and premium
care. However, national category growth does not prove that the same positioning or price
will work in every neighbourhood.
1. National brand architecture
Recommended brand core
Your proposed core is:
Verified pet care with trusted sitters, live updates, report cards and emergency support.
A safer and stronger version is:
Verified local pet care with trusted caregivers, live service updates, detailed care
reports and urgent-care coordination.
Why change “emergency support”?
“Emergency support” can sound like the platform provides veterinary treatment.
Your platform should promise:
• Emergency clinic information
• Human coordination
• Record-sharing assistance
• Transport coordination where available
• Customer escalation

### Page 160

It should not promise:
• Diagnosis
• Treatment
• Guaranteed clinic admission
• Guaranteed veterinary response time
• Medical prioritisation for paid members
Use:
Urgent-care coordination
rather than:
Emergency medical support
2. Four national brand pillars
Pillar 1: Verified people
Every provider profile should show exactly what was verified.
Valid badges
• Identity checked
• Contact verified
• Documents reviewed
• Practical trial completed
• Dog-walking skills assessed
• Cat-care skills assessed
• Society access approved
• Background check completed on [date]
Avoid vague badges
• 100% safe
• Government approved
• Fully certified
• Police verified
• Best sitter

### Page 161

unless the claim is literally true, current and documented.
A “police verification” badge should appear only when the relevant check was actually
completed, with the type and date visible. It must not imply government endorsement or
guarantee that the provider presents no risk.
Pillar 2: Visible service
The customer should be able to see that the promised care happened.
National service-proof standard
• Start check-in
• End check-out
• GPS route where appropriate
• Time and duration
• Photo or media proof with consent
• Toilet and behaviour notes
• Food, water or medication confirmation
• Incident flag
• Digital report card
GPS tracking and live updates are already promoted by existing Indian dog-walking
services. In competitive metros, these are becoming expected product features rather than
sufficient premium differentiators.
Your differentiation must therefore combine tracking with:
• Better caregiver continuity
• Better backup fulfilment
• Better reports
• Faster problem resolution
• Stronger society protocols
Pillar 3: Reliable continuity
Use:
Preferred caregiver with approved backup coverage

### Page 162

Do not use:
Guaranteed same sitter forever
Recommended service targets:
Metric Target
Preferred-caregiver fulfilment 85%+
Primary or approved backup fulfilment 98%+
Unintroduced replacement Below 2%
Provider no-show Below 1%
The brand promise should be continuity with resilience—not dependence on one person.
Pillar 4: Local readiness
A city page should never imply city-wide coverage when only one cluster is operational.
Every local page must show:
• Exact active neighbourhoods
• Services currently available
• Current operating hours
• Starting prices
• Verified local provider count
• Backup coverage where applicable
• Local support contact
• Local urgent-care directory
• Waitlist for unsupported areas
This prevents national marketing from outrunning actual city operations.
3. What remains national versus local
Keep nationally consistent
• Brand name and logo
• Core colours and typography

6. Bengaluru strategy
Recommended positioning
Reliable app-first pet care for busy Bengaluru pet parents—verified walkers, live
updates and dependable backup coverage.
Recommended hero
Bruno’s routine, handled—even on your busiest days.
Flagship product
Active Weekday Walk Plan
• 20 × 30-minute walks
• Primary walker preference
• Approved backup
• Live check-in and report
• Monthly activity summary
• Pause and reschedule controls
Existing operators already advertise monthly walking plans and GPS-enabled service in
Bengaluru. A new entrant therefore cannot compete only on “book from an app”; it needs
visibly stronger fulfilment, continuity and service recovery.
Corrected cluster rollout
Cluster 1: Whitefield micro-market
• Whitefield
• Brookefield
• Kundalahalli
• Selected Hoodi/Mahadevapura-adjacent societies where travel times work
This is the strongest grouping in your original plan.
Cluster 2A: HSR
• HSR Layout
• Selected nearby societies

### Page 167

• Dedicated morning/evening provider pool
Cluster 2B: Bellandur–Sarjapur corridor
• Bellandur
• Selected Sarjapur Road communities
• Strict peak-window radii
Do not treat HSR, Bellandur and all of Sarjapur Road as one undifferentiated operating
zone.
Cluster 3A: Koramangala
Cluster 3B: Indiranagar
They may share branding but should have separate peak-capacity calculations.
Cluster 4A: JP Nagar
Cluster 4B: Electronic City
Electronic City and JP Nagar should not be grouped as one fulfilment cluster simply
because they are both in south Bengaluru.
Bengaluru recorded a 74.4% average congestion level in TomTom’s 2025 index. I therefore
recommend smaller zones and time-window-specific provider radii rather than city-wide
matching.
Acquisition sequence
1. Successful-walk conversion
2. Apartment society partnerships
3. Google Search by supported locality
4. Instagram proof-of-service content
5. Referral programme
6. Corporate benefit pilot after consumer fulfilment is stable
Local proof content
Show:
• GPS walk examples

Mumbai’s average congestion level was 63.2% in 2025, and a 10 km drive averaged nearly

Radius rule
Use:
• 1–2 km during the most congested periods where density permits
• 2–4 km during off-peak periods
• Same-building or same-society route blocks
• Maximum service-to-service travel-time limits
A fixed radius should not replace actual route-time data.

Phase 10 brand win condition
Customers recognise one trusted national company, while every city page, offer,
provider pool and service promise feels genuinely local and is supported by real
operational capacity.
Multi-City Brand Strategy: APPROVED—with one master brand, smaller service
clusters, evidence-based local claims and no city-wide marketing ahead of fulfilment.
Phase 10: Six-Month Multi-City Expansion Plan
End-to-End City 2 and City 3 Execution Blueprint
Executive verdict
Approve the six-month structure, but treat it as a controlled replication programme—
not a commitment to fully scale two cities within six months.
A realistic Month 6 outcome is:
• City 1: mature and operating independently
• City 2: validated, with one or two stable clusters
• City 3: closed beta or early public launch
• National system: shared dashboards, SOPs, finance controls and quality standards
The plan should not force City 3 forward merely because the calendar reaches Month 4.
City 3 preparation begins only after City 2 passes defined quality, supply and economic
gates.
India’s pet-care category is growing, supported by pet humanisation, increasing consumer
spending and expanding digital discovery. That creates a favourable market backdrop, but it
does not eliminate the need for local density and reliable fulfilment.
Correct expansion sequence
City 1 readiness
↓
City 2 demand and supply validation
↓
City 2 closed beta
↓
City 2 quality and contribution gate

↓

↓

Six-month go/no-go system
Continue expansion when
• City 1 remains stable.
• City 2 reaches validated-cluster status.

• Provider payouts remain current.

• Subscriptions exceed backup capacity.

• Completion falls below 95%.

• Critical incidents are concealed or unresolved.
• Partner payouts are delayed.
• Customer refunds increase materially.
• City finance cannot reconcile payments.
• The founder is again personally coordinating daily bookings.
• Cross-city data access is uncontrolled.

Phase 10 win condition
At the end of six months, the business has reproduced its operating model in City 2,
started proving it in City 3, and can compare demand, quality, supply, subscriptions
and contribution across all cities without relying on founder firefighting.
Six-Month Phase 10 Expansion Plan: GO—with sequential city gates, starter packs
before subscriptions, and City 3 blocked until City 2 demonstrates stable quality and
viable economics.
Phase 10 City Budget, Ownership and Quality-Control System
Executive decision
Your direction is correct:
• Start new cities lean.
• Keep the first major cities company-owned.
• Use franchise or operating partners only after safety and operating systems are
mature.
• Standardise quality centrally while allowing controlled local commercial
flexibility.
The proposed budget ranges are useful as initial planning estimates, but they currently
understate several costs:
• Field operations support
• Verification and training
• Partner-payout working capital
• Refund and service-recovery reserves
• Legal, accounting and compliance
• Insurance

### Page 222

• Staff travel and local transport
• Central technology and support allocation
The recommended structure is:
Company-Owned City
↓
Lean Closed Beta
↓
Validated Cluster
↓
Controlled Growth Budget
↓
Contribution-Positive City
↓
Premium Scale Budget
Do not begin with the premium budget merely because the city is Bengaluru, Mumbai or
Gurugram.
Part I — Lean City Launch Budget
1. Review of the proposed lean budget

manager/operations ₹1,00,000 operations assistant

Tools/operations

Current salary references show why the city-manager range is broad. Indeed reported an
Indian operations-manager average of about ₹32,426 monthly in June 2026, while
Glassdoor’s broader self-reported range is considerably higher, reflecting differences in
seniority, city, team size and P&L responsibility. A genuine city general manager in
Bengaluru or Mumbai will usually require a different budget from a junior launch
coordinator.
2. Corrected lean monthly operating budget
A. City team
Item Recommended monthly budget
City manager/launch owner ₹40,000–₹1,00,000
Operations/onboarding generalist ₹25,000–₹60,000
Shared central support allocation ₹10,000–₹30,000
Team subtotal ₹75,000–₹1,90,000
At the earliest stage, one experienced city manager may perform part of the operations
role. Once the city exceeds approximately 150–300 monthly services, separate live
operations from supply recruitment.
B. Supply recruitment and activation
Item Recommended monthly budget
Recruitment campaigns ₹8,000–₹25,000
Referral incentives ₹5,000–₹15,000
Document/background processes ₹5,000–₹20,000

### Page 224

Item Recommended monthly budget
Training and practical trials ₹5,000–₹20,000
Supply subtotal ₹20,000–₹60,000
Do not measure recruitment success by applications alone.
The real metric is:
[
\text{Cost per activated provider}
\frac{\text{Recruitment and verification cost}}
{\text{Providers passing trial and accepting work}}
]
A city that spends ₹30,000 and activates ten dependable providers is healthier than one
that spends ₹15,000 and produces 100 inactive applications.
C. Local customer acquisition
Item Recommended monthly budget
Local search/social advertising ₹20,000–₹75,000
Referral and starter offers ₹5,000–₹25,000
Content and local creative ₹5,000–₹20,000
Marketing subtotal ₹30,000–₹1,20,000
Paid campaigns should be restricted to:
• Supported postcodes
• Active service clusters
• Available services
• Time windows with usable supply
• Areas below capacity limits
Do not pay for city-wide demand when operations cover only one locality.
D. Society partnerships and events

Item Recommended monthly budget
Small resident workshop ₹5,000–₹15,000
Grooming/training event ₹10,000–₹30,000
Larger society camp ₹20,000–₹50,000
Printing/announcements ₹3,000–₹10,000
Recommended monthly planning range:
₹20,000–₹75,000
Every event should have a funnel:
Registrations
↓
Attendance
↓

↓

↓

Do not evaluate events using attendance alone.
AWBI guidance covers pet owners, caregivers, RWAs and apartment associations. Society
campaigns should support responsible coexistence and workable gate procedures, rather
than allowing local sales teams to promise restrictions or rules inconsistent with national
animal-welfare standards.
E. Tools and field operations
Item Recommended monthly budget
Communications and support tools ₹5,000–₹15,000

The platform should avoid a city office during closed beta unless there is a clear

F. Risk, refunds and working capital
These should be separate balances.
Service-recovery reserve
Used for:
• Provider replacement
• Customer refunds
• Goodwill credits
• Emergency transport coordination
• Immediate operational response
Recommended:
₹25,000–₹75,000 monthly reserve
Partner-payout working capital
Used when:
• Partner payouts are due before payment-provider settlement
• Refunds are pending
• Subscription revenue covers future services
• Society invoices have longer payment terms
Recommended:
At least two to four weeks of forecast partner payouts

### Page 227

Do not use the incident reserve to pay ordinary partner earnings.

Category Recommended monthly range
City team ₹75,000–₹1,90,000
Supply activation ₹20,000–₹60,000
Local marketing ₹30,000–₹1,20,000
Society/events ₹20,000–₹75,000

Service-recovery reserve ₹25,000–₹75,000
Recommended operating burn ₹1.95L–₹6.05L/month
The upper end assumes:
• A two-person city team
• Significant local advertising
• Multiple society activities
• Dedicated support allocation
• Higher travel or verification costs
A highly founder-assisted launch may operate near ₹1.5L–₹2.5L temporarily, but that
should not be mistaken for a scalable city cost.

Do not release the entire budget on Day 1.

Part II — Premium City Launch Budget
6. What “premium launch” should mean
A premium budget should buy genuine capacity and operational capability:
• Full city launch team
• Multiple active clusters
• Strong customer acquisition
• Dedicated society sales
• Local support
• Provider training and quality audits
• Events
• Backup supply
• Faster service recovery
It should not simply mean spending ₹10 lakh monthly on advertising.
7. Review of the premium budget
Cost item Proposed range Assessment
Full city team ₹2L–₹5L/month Plausible for a 5–8-person mixed team
Marketing ₹2L–₹10L/month Too broad without CAC and capacity gates
Events/societies ₹1L–₹5L Appropriate only with multiple active clusters
Operations buffer ₹1L–₹3L Necessary, but should be split
Proposed total ₹6L–₹23L/month Broadly plausible for growth, not initial validation
A complete premium budget should also explicitly include:
• Provider acquisition
• Quality audits
• Insurance/compliance
• Local support

### Page 230

• Payment and refund reserves
• Technology allocation
Corrected premium range
₹7L–₹25L per month
Indicative six-month cash envelope
₹50L–₹1.7Cr, including approximately 15–20% contingency.
The top end is justified only when the city already supports multiple dense clusters.
8. Premium-spend unlock gates
Premium spending should begin only after the city demonstrates:
Requirement Target
Monthly completed services 300–500+
Paying customers 100–200+
Repeat customers 25–40%
Average rating 4.6+
Completion rate 95%+
Provider no-show Below 1%
Refund/dispute rate Below 5%
Active local supply 10–25 providers
Backup coverage 90–95%+
Direct contribution Positive or clearly improving
Critical unresolved incidents Zero
Marketing scale rule
Increase customer-acquisition spending only when:
[
\text{Incremental contribution}
\text{Incremental acquisition and fulfilment cost}
]

### Page 231

Do not scale advertising when:
• Peak slots are full
• Provider cancellations are rising
• Support SLAs are failing
• Customer ratings are falling
• Partner payouts are delayed
• Subscription capacity lacks backup
Part III — Company-Owned vs Franchise Expansion
9. Company-owned cities
Advantages
• Direct control over verification
• Consistent safety processes
• Better training
• Centralised customer experience
• Reliable data
• Easier pricing control
• Direct city P&L visibility
• Faster intervention after incidents
Disadvantages
• Higher capital requirements
• Slower geographic expansion
• Greater recruitment burden
• Higher central-management complexity
• Greater fixed-cost exposure
Best use
Company-owned operations should be used for:
• The first three to five cities
• Major brand-defining metros

### Page 232

• Cities containing new service experiments
• Markets with complex routing or high expectations
• Cities used to train future regional operators
Recommendation
Keep these company-owned initially:
• First/core city
• Bengaluru
• Pune
• Mumbai
• Gurugram
• Ahmedabad, if it is the core operating base
The exact list should follow the actual expansion sequence rather than an arbitrary
population ranking.
10. Traditional franchise cities
Advantages
• Lower central capital requirement
• Faster local hiring
• Local relationship advantage
• Local society access
• Faster geographic coverage
Risks
• Provider-verification shortcuts
• Different refund practices
• Hidden incidents
• Inconsistent training
• Poor payment discipline
• Unauthorised pricing
• Customer-data leakage

### Page 233

• Weak sitter payouts
• Brand damage spreading nationally
A franchise arrangement in India requires a carefully drafted commercial contract and
authorised trademark use. Franchise disputes are expressly recognised among
commercial disputes, while the underlying relationship also involves ordinary contract and
trademark law.
Franchise operation does not remove the need for consumer grievance handling, fair e-
commerce conduct or transparent cancellation and refund rules.
11. Recommended hybrid: Managed City Operator
Before offering a conventional franchise, use a more controlled model:
Managed City Operating Partner
Central company owns
• Brand
• Technology
• Customer accounts and data
• Payment collection
• Provider payout infrastructure
• Verification standards
• Final provider approval
• Price architecture
• Refund rules
• Incident classification
• Customer-support standards
• National marketing
• Subscription and credit system
• Quality auditing
Local operating partner owns
• Field recruitment
• Practical trials under central standards

### Page 234

• Local availability management
• Society outreach
• Local events
• Partner relationships
• Daily schedule coordination
• Local service recovery
• Local team management
Central company must retain step-in rights
The central team must be able to:
• Pause a city
• Pause a service
• Suspend a provider
• Take over customer support
• Freeze acquisition
• Audit customer and provider records
• Replace the operator
• Terminate brand access
12. What a franchise or operating partner must never control alone
Do not delegate final authority over:
• Critical-incident classification
• Deletion of complaints or reviews
• Provider identity verification
• Safety-policy exceptions
• Customer refunds
• Customer funds
• Partner-payout records
• Medical claims
• Customer-data exports

### Page 235

• Subscription-credit adjustments
• City-quality reporting
The DPDP Rules, 2025 have phased commencement, but a multi-operator model should
already use purpose-specific notices, role-based access, security safeguards and
grievance handling.
Platform-worker and aggregator obligations must also be assessed consistently across
company-owned and operator-managed cities. The Code on Social Security recognises gig
and platform workers, and 2026 central-rule developments reinforce the need for worker
registration, engagement and reporting systems.
13. Franchise-readiness gate
Do not offer franchise rights until:
Requirement Minimum condition
Successfully replicated cities 2–3 company-owned cities
Operating history 9–12 months of stable data
Verification process Standardised and auditable
Training academy Operational
City dashboards Real-time and standardised
City P&L Reconciled consistently
Incident system Centralised
Provider payout system Centralised
Customer support Central or centrally monitored
Brand/trademark protection Complete
Operator agreement Legally reviewed
Audit and step-in rights Contractual
Critical-incident playbook Tested
Data-access system City scoped
Franchise pilot One controlled territory

### Page 236

14. Recommended franchise economics
Avoid rewarding an operator on total customer GMV.
An operator should be rewarded for:
• Completed services
• Positive city contribution
• Customer retention
• Quality
• Society conversion
• Provider reliability
Better compensation model
Fixed local management fee
+
Performance share of city contribution
+
Quality and growth bonus
Illustrative structure:
• Small fixed operating fee
• 20–35% share of contribution after direct city costs
• Bonus for quality, repeat rate and society renewals
• No share on refunded or disputed transactions
• Penalties for concealed incidents or payout failures
This is strategically safer than giving a local operator a large percentage of GMV regardless
of service quality.

Phase 10 budget principle
Spend enough to create local service density and reliable backup capacity, but
release growth capital only after demand, fulfilment and contribution have been
proven.
Phase 10 ownership principle
Delegate local execution, but never delegate final control over safety, customer funds,
verification, incidents, refunds, data or brand standards.
Phase 10 quality-control principle
Every city may speak differently and price differently, but every pet must receive the
same minimum safety, verification, documentation and incident-response standard.
Final decision: START LEAN, KEEP THE FIRST MAJOR CITIES COMPANY-OWNED, AND
DEFER FRANCHISING UNTIL THE OPERATING SYSTEM HAS BEEN PROVEN ACROSS
MULTIPLE CITIES.
Phase 10: Central–Local Operating Model, KPIs and Expansion Risk System
Executive decision
Your centralisation model is fundamentally correct. The strongest structure is:
Centralise trust, technology, money, data and safety. Localise fulfilment,
relationships and market execution.
The corrected operating model is:
Function category Operating rule
Non-negotiable standards Central
Customer funds and financial controls Central
Data, permissions and security Central

### Page 245

Function category Operating rule
Daily service delivery Local
Local relationships and supply Local
Customer recovery and incidents Hybrid
Pricing and marketing Local within central guardrails
This avoids two dangerous extremes:
• Over-centralisation: local teams cannot respond quickly enough.
• Over-localisation: every city develops different safety, refund and verification
standards.

3. Technology: central platform, local configuration
Technology should remain centrally owned because multiple city-specific codebases
would create:
• Inconsistent features
• Security gaps
• Expensive maintenance
• Different credit and refund logic
• Reporting incompatibility
Local teams should configure—not rewrite—the system:
• Active service zones

### Page 248

• Operating hours
• Service availability
• Provider pools
• Capacity limits
• Local prices
• Society programmes
• Notification language
• Support contacts
Central product platform
↓
City configuration
↓
Service-zone configuration
↓
Local provider and capacity assignment
No city should be allowed to create an unofficial spreadsheet-based payment,
subscription or incident system outside the main platform.
4. Payments: fully centralised
Central payment control should include:
• Payment-provider accounts
• Subscription mandates
• Customer invoices
• Partner payout ledger
• Refund approval workflow
• Failed-payment recovery
• Tax records
• Reconciliation
• Fraud monitoring

### Page 249

• Payment-link generation
Local city teams may:
• Contact customers about an open invoice
• Submit service-failure evidence
• Request a refund
• Recommend a goodwill credit
• Confirm whether a service occurred
They should not:
• Mark an unpaid invoice as paid
• Collect unofficial cash
• Change provider payout records
• Issue undocumented refunds
• Create local bank or UPI accounts for customer collections
• Adjust subscription credits without an audit entry
5. Data and security: central and non-delegable
Multi-city operations process sensitive operational data such as:
• Customer addresses
• Recurring home schedules
• Society gate instructions
• Pet behavioural notes
• Provider identity documents
• Payment references
• Incident information
India’s notified DPDP Rules, 2025 establish the current national data-protection framework
and include a phased enforcement timeline. City growth should therefore use central data
governance, role-based access, security safeguards and auditable grievance handling
rather than giving every city broad database access.
Recommended access model

### Page 250

Role Permitted access
Walker Only assigned service and necessary pet instructions
City operations Scheduling and service-recovery data
Customer support Customer, booking and complaint data
Society executive Society account data, not private pet records
City manager City operations and aggregated performance
Finance Payment and settlement information
Safety team Incidents and required supporting records
National administration Cross-city data according to role
A Pune team member should not be able to browse Mumbai customer addresses merely
because both cities use the same platform.
6. Safety: central standards, local execution
The national safety team should define:
• Provider verification levels
• Pet-risk classification
• Service permissions
• Backup requirements
• Boarding standards
• Grooming hygiene rules
• Incident severity
• Evidence requirements
• Suspension criteria
• Root-cause review
• Corrective actions
The local team should:
• Conduct practical assessments
• Verify local availability

### Page 251

• Execute emergency containment
• Contact the customer
• Coordinate local veterinary assistance
• Preserve evidence
• Remove unsafe providers from active schedules
• Implement corrective actions
Incident authority
A city manager can open and escalate an incident, but should not independently:
• Reduce the recorded severity
• Delete the incident
• Close a critical case
• Restore a suspended provider
• Hide a customer complaint
Critical closure should require central safety approval.
7. Training: central curriculum, local delivery
Centralised training content ensures every city teaches the same fundamentals:
• Pet handling
• Leash and gate safety
• Escape prevention
• Customer communication
• Service reports
• Privacy
• Incident reporting
• Medication boundaries
• Aggression escalation
• Cancellation and backup procedures
Local teams may add modules for:
• Society access

• Local language
• Climate and weather
• Local travel conditions
• City-specific service procedures
Certification language
The platform may issue:
• Platform course completed
• Practical assessment passed
• Dog-walking assessment passed
• Cat-care module completed
It should not call internal training a national or government certification unless that status
is formally supported.

City operations must be local because they require real-time knowledge of:
• Traffic
• Provider locations
• Society entry
• Weather disruption
• Local events
• Provider leave
• Customer availability
• Emergency providers

Today’s services

↓
Check-in

↓

↓

Customer recovery
Central teams provide software and standards; the city team owns the actual outcome.

Society acquisition depends on local trust and cannot be managed entirely from a national
office.
Local teams should own:
• RWA and facility-manager meetings
• Resident demand assessment
• Gate protocols
• Society events
• Pilot implementation
• Local escalation contacts
• Pilot-to-paid conversion
Central teams should supply:
• Agreements
• Data-sharing terms
• Safety presentation
• Event SOP
• Monthly report format
• Commercial boundaries
• Brand claims
AWBI publishes national guidance concerning pet owners, caregivers, RWAs and
apartment associations. City teams may adapt operational arrangements, but they should
not promise society policies that conflict with the national welfare framework.

### Page 254

Local teams are best placed to find and manage:
• Groomers
• Trainers
• Veterinary clinics
• Home-visit professionals
• Boarding facilities
• Event providers
Central teams must control:
• Verification requirements
• Service permissions
• Contract templates
• Scorecards
• Customer-data access
• Medical-claim restrictions
• Payout and refund structures
Veterinary boundary
The platform coordinates access to registered professionals. It does not allow local staff,
support agents or AI systems to give final diagnoses.
11. Customer support: hybrid three-level system
Level 1 — Central support
Handles:
• Booking questions
• Credit balance
• Payment status
• Subscription changes
• Basic cancellations

### Page 255

• Report access
• General service information
Level 2 — City operations
Handles:
• Walker late or missing
• Backup assignment
• Society gate denial
• Customer access issue
• Local rescheduling
• Physical service recovery
Level 3 — Central specialist escalation
Handles:
• Serious incidents
• Privacy cases
• Large refunds
• Legal complaints
• Fraud
• Medical-boundary issues
• Repeated city quality failure
India’s E-Commerce Rules require consumer-facing e-commerce entities to maintain
grievance-handling arrangements. A hybrid model works only when the customer has one
visible support path and the central system records the entire escalation.
12. Emergency response: local action, central command
The first response must be local because the incident is happening in that city.
Local responsibilities
• Secure the pet
• Contact the customer
• Stop the service

• Contact an appropriate clinic
• Activate local transport where available
• Preserve photographs, location and timeline
• Suspend the provider temporarily where required
Central responsibilities
• Classify severity
• Guide customer communication
• Review legal/privacy implications
• Approve major refunds
• Manage insurers or advisors
• Conduct root-cause analysis
• Issue network-wide corrective action
Local containment
+
Central governance
=
Effective incident response

Part II — Correcting the city-level KPIs
14. Autopsy of the proposed KPI table
Your proposed targets contain three important mathematical issues.
1,000 bookings and 500 customers
This equals an average of:
1,000÷500 = 2
completed services per customer per month.
That may be reasonable for a mixed-service marketplace, but it is low for a walking-
subscription-led city. Track customer frequency distribution—not only the average.
1,000 bookings and 50 active sitters
This equals:
1,000÷50 = 20
services per provider per month.
That may produce weak provider earnings and high supply churn unless many providers are
intentionally part-time or provide other services.

### Page 258

₹5 lakh revenue and 1,000 bookings
This equals:
₹5,00,000÷1,000 = ₹500
per completed service.
This is meaningful only after defining whether ₹5 lakh means:
• Customer GMV
• Platform-recognised revenue
• Gross profit
• City contribution

18. Active provider KPI
Replace a fixed “50 active sitters” target with:
• Active providers
• Capacity supplied
• Capacity consumed
• Provider earnings
• Provider retention
• Peak-window coverage
• Backup coverage
Better calculation
forecast completed services
Required active providers = ×reserve factor
safe services per provider
Example:
• 1,000 services
• 50 safe services per provider
• 15% reserve
1,000÷50 = 20
20×1.15 = 23
Approximately 23 active providers may be sufficient, depending on service mix and peak-
time concentration.
The target should therefore be:
Enough healthy provider capacity—not the largest possible provider headcount.
19. Repeat-rate KPI
Define repeat rate consistently:

### Page 262

Repeat customer rate
customers completing a second paid service within the selected period
=
eligible first-time customers
Report:
• 30-day repeat
• 60-day repeat
• 90-day repeat
• Subscription renewal
• Repeat rate by acquisition channel
A customer acquired one week ago should not be treated as a failed 90-day repeat
customer.
20. Subscription KPI
“Subscription MRR growing” is insufficient.
Track:
• Active subscribers
• New MRR
• Upgrade MRR
• Downgrade MRR
• Churned MRR
• Renewal rate
• Credit utilisation
• Subscription contribution
• Backup capacity required
Recommended mature-city target
• First renewal: 60–80%
• Mature monthly churn: below 10–15%
• Recurring platform-revenue share: 25–40%
• Credit utilisation: 70–90%

### Page 263

• Subscription fulfilment: 95%+
21. Rating KPI
A city’s average should be accompanied by:
• Number of reviews
• Rating distribution
• Percentage of services reviewed
• Recent 30-day rating
• Low-rating count
• Complaint rate
A rating of 4.8 from 15 reviews is less reliable than 4.7 from 1,500 reviews.
Recommended gate:
• 4.6+ average
• At least 50 verified reviews for a validated city
• No major service category below 4.4
• No unresolved pattern of low ratings
22. Incident KPI
Reject one combined target of “below 3%.”
Use severity-based reporting:
Category Example Target
Pet escape, death, serious fraud, major Zero preventable and zero
Critical
data breach unresolved

Minor reportable Below 1%
minor service issue
General
Report quality, communication, timing Below 3–5%
complaint

### Page 264

Category Example Target
Refund/dispute Financial/service dispute Below 3–5%
A city with 2.5% minor communication incidents is very different from a city with 2.5% pet
escapes.
23. Gross-margin KPI
Gross margin is valid only when every city uses the same accounting basis.
If partner payouts are recorded as cost of service:
Recognised revenue−direct cost of service
Gross margin =
Recognised revenue
If the platform recognises only its commission as revenue, the denominator is different.
Therefore:
• Use a central finance definition.
• Do not allow each city manager to calculate margin independently.
• Show contribution alongside gross margin.
• Review margin by service, cluster and acquisition channel.

28. National revenue KPI
Use four separate values:
Metric Meaning
Network GMV Total customer spending
Platform revenue Recognised company revenue
Contribution Revenue after direct operating costs
Operating result Contribution after central costs
A ₹25 lakh GMV target can be commercially weak if provider payouts, marketing and
refunds consume most of it.
Recommended national gate
• Network contribution positive or improving according to plan
• At least one mature city contribution-positive
• At least one new city with a contribution-positive cluster
• No city scaling through unreconciled discounts

Use a volume-weighted average, but preserve city minimums.
Recommended:
• Network weighted rating: 4.6+
• Every validated/mature city: 4.6+
• No public city below 4.4 for more than one review period
• Critical service categories reviewed separately
A strong Bengaluru rating must not hide poor performance in a new city.

32. Expansion-too-fast risk
Early signs
• City 1 support response worsens
• Founder becomes involved in daily booking assignments
• Provider payouts are late

### Page 270

• New-city dashboards are incomplete
• Training standards are shortened
• Unresolved complaints increase
Prevention
• One city at a time
• Gate-based budget release
• Dedicated local ownership
• Central capacity review
• No City 3 launch until City 2 passes stability gates
33. Weak city-manager risk
A poor city manager can create:
• Unsafe provider shortcuts
• Inflated booking numbers
• Unresolved customer complaints
• Society relationship damage
• Inaccurate P&L
• Hidden incidents
Prevention
Use a balanced scorecard:
Dimension Weight
Safety and quality 30%
Fulfilment and supply 20%
Retention and demand 20%
City economics 20%
Society and strategic growth 10%
No growth bonus should be paid after a concealed critical incident or materially
unreconciled city P&L.

### Page 271

34. Platform-worker risk
Provider onboarding and worker reporting should not vary casually by city. The Code on
Social Security recognises gig and platform workers, and a June 2026 Labour Ministry
direction states that covered aggregators must share required worker details electronically
under the 2026 Rules. Classification and reporting should therefore be centrally governed
even though recruitment is local.
Centralise:
• Worker classification policy
• Required fields
• Engagement records
• Earnings and payouts
• eShram/reporting workflow where applicable
• Document retention
• Audit history

technology, verification, safety, incidents, refunds and data.
2. Phase 10 upgrade decisions
Upgrade 1: Mandatory city-launch gates
Decision: APPROVE—NON-NEGOTIABLE
No public launch should occur until five areas are validated:
1. Demand
2. Active supply
3. Operations
4. Safety/compliance
5. Economics
Official gate sequence
Gate 0: City selection
Gate 1: Qualified demand
Gate 2: Active supply
Gate 3: Operational readiness
Gate 4: Closed-beta quality
Gate 5: Cluster economics
Gate 6: Public expansion
A strong demand gate cannot compensate for a failed safety gate.

### Page 283

Upgrade 2: First three to five cities company-operated
Decision: APPROVE WITH FLEXIBILITY
Use “company-operated” rather than requiring one exact legal ownership structure.
The first major cities should remain under direct company operational control because
they establish:
• Safety standards
• Training standards
• City staffing ratios
• Unit economics
• Society sales processes
• Support SLAs
• Incident patterns
• Technology requirements
A tightly managed operator may be tested later, but not before the central operating system
is mature.
Upgrade 3: Mandatory area-cluster expansion
Decision: APPROVE—NON-NEGOTIABLE
City-wide availability must not be activated as one Boolean setting.
Every service requires cluster-level control over:
• Availability
• Partner pool
• Price
• Capacity
• Hours
• Backup reserve
• Support ownership
• Stop-sales threshold

### Page 284

The public landing page and checkout system must read from the same serviceability
configuration.
Upgrade 4: Mandatory city-level P&L dashboard
Decision: APPROVE
Track four separate financial layers.
A. GMV
Total customer spending.
B. Platform revenue
The company’s recognised commissions, service fees, memberships, subscriptions,
society fees and other retained revenue.
C. City contribution
City contribution = Platform revenue−direct city operating costs
D. Fully allocated operating result
City contribution after its share of central technology, finance, support and leadership
expenses.
